Theo smiles and says, "Isn't it fortunate, Al, that we left our itinerary open?" "Theo," I admits, with all sincerity, "I'd rather find 'that' open than the safe of the First National bank.” 99 99

99 The frills are looking at each other in a way that plainly says, "They got us figured wrong,' so I apologizes for the "tête-à-tête" me and the boy friend has just been partaking of and informs them that we don't mean nothing but good. Then I asks for more information on this hydro school they belong to. It seems, as near as I can figure out, that some Wall Street bear, with a flare for adventure in his makeup somewhere-that's meant to be funny, in case you didn't recognize it can you imagine anybody in Wall Street without a flare for adventure in him?-well, to continue with this educational tour, this bozo, who has lined his pockets with plenty of wool fleeced from the sheep that stray into Wall Street-Wailing Wall Street-has decided to turn over a new leaf and steal money legitimately in the form of giving two thousand dollars worth of goods for four. I must give this baby credit. He hits the pocketbook where it opens the easiest and hurts the least. He sees the sons and daughters of wealthy families running wild around colleges-gin, janes and jamborees-the parents see in the press where football has become the backbone of every university and so this Wall Street graduate, just at the psychological moment, organizes a floating university, manned by outstanding college professors and offers the parents to take these rah-rah students on an educational tour of the world at only four thousand dollars per head. Boy, that sounds good, and many a parent sees the opportunity of a lifetime to place the education of their boy and girl in the hands. of another and know that money couldn't buy a more profitable and complete form of instruction. And the result is, a hundred animated inmates from colleges all over America are herded together for this world tour that promises to be a panacea for all education's ills. 

Pardon me while I yawn.

WORLD BEATERS 

17 We asks the girls what countries their tour takes in and K gives us her schedule, claiming one between them is ample. After lunch me and Theo repairs to the lounging room and studies the cruise of this floating university and, believe it or not, folks, it turns out to be just the trip we has planned on ourselves; and why shouldn't it? On the back of this plan is the schedule for attending lectures. It seems that the students. are divided into classes and must attend so many lectures a day on various subjects, ranging from ancient religions to indoor sports. I see from this sheet that 

K is booked for two forty-five minute periods each morning and three thirty minute periods in the afternoon, and one of them afternoon courses is astronomy. "Hot dog," I exclaims, reading that out loud to Theo, "get this, fellow. K is taking a course in astronomy. Boy, if she flunks in that course I'll wear the dunce's hat for her. I'll lay you any odds that K takes the Nobel prize in astronomy before this school proves whether Voliva is a nut or not-unless, of course, that human curfew forgets to drop dead. You know, Theo, he ain't such a bad guy but he's got one habit I'd like to break him of." "What's that, may I ask?" "Breathing," says I, "and if he interferes in our study of the stars, I will.'

We shoots a little table golf, plays a couple single sets of deck tennis, watch the flying fish dart about and before we knows it the Chink is out with his one-piece orchestra again and it's time to put on the feed bag and see the Playfair sisters. Ain't that a name, though?

During dinner we gives the girls a break by letting them in on the secret that we're going to be table mates until we reaches Japan, at least. Not being able to show their great pleasure at hearing this by kissing us-we're in the dining saloon, you know,-K remains

null and void, while B looks a long way off and drawls, "Oh, ye-ah?" Not so good, I'm thinking hard to myself, when Theo breaks the terrible silence with usual diplomacy. "By the way, girls, we're docking at Havana in the morning. Are either of you familiar with 

Cuba?" He knows I'm not-though I will be by this time tomorrow. "Yes," responds K, "I see, by the bulletin, that we arrive at 7 A.M. No, neither В nor myself have ever been in Cuba and we're looking forward with great anticipation to seeing quaint old Havana." "I spent a couple weeks there a few years ago,' 

Theo informs us all. "It truly is an attractive country and Havana has much to offer the traveler-if he knows his way about-I'm wondering, would you jeopardize your standing in the estimation of your professors if you were to allow us to show you Havana?" "Thanks, a lot, Theo," smiles K, "but we were warned, just this afternoon, against leaving our party while ashore. I'm sure you could afford us a much more pleasant day than riding around in rubberneck busses-but we're still in school, you know-and teacher says 'No.'" And we all laughs but we don't get a date for Cuba. Our first foreign country to be visited and no date. Well, it could be worse-much worse we might be alone on the boat with the two delegates. I gets consolation for everything out of knowing that we might of been with them. 

The evening is spent in dancing and chattingnever leaving the main crowd-until eleven P.M. and then the main crowd leaves us-thanks to the nemesis of liberty-some day he's going to turn up missingno foolin'. But just before the curtain drops on the evening's performance, Theo, addressing the three of us, B in particular, suggests, "Since we are to be denied the pleasure of your company on land tomorrow, perhaps you'd be willing to start the day off bright

WORLD BEATERS 

19 

for us by arising early in the morning and observing our approach to Cuba with us. I assure you, the view is one never to be forgotten. On either bank of the bottle-necked entrance to Havana harbor, stands out in bold relief, against the first gray streaks of dawn, the obsolete arguments of kings. These forts, having so well resisted the elements of time, hark back to a day when piracy was more than romance. Oh, it can't help but thrill you.' And I can see they're plenty thrilled already—can't that boy talk, though? "You will join us on the promenade, under the bridge, at six o'clock, won't you, please?" And as they dash away we gets plenty assurance they'll be there-and they were. Theo hadn't overdone it, one word. The view, as we approached the harbor, was everything he had told us it was, and K squeezed my hand as we cut in between them two old moss-covered forts. I felt something playing the spinal cord up and down my back as

I thought about what Theo had said about the days of piracy. I don't pretend to know what it is, but something has made plenty of changes since them days and it was a mighty comforting feeling to glide through that entrance, within a stone's throw of either fort, and know there wasn't any rocks or hot tar going to rain on you.

We was soon tied up at the dock and the girls waved us a fond farewell as they took off in their party to see Havana. Theo stood there on the deck, smiling down on B and Tosti's goodbye-if you can't stand puns, I mean "tossed his goodbye." Forgive the attempt. We gets into lighter clothes-both in weight and color-and goes out to see Spain's contribution to America. The first street we gets into gives me a bad impression of Havana, Cuba and all things Spanish. It's about twelve feet wide and wouldn't rate even as an alley in Hoboken. It's clean, that's all you

WORLD BEATERS 

can say in its favor, but the buildings, three and four stories high, just about completely shut out all the light and you jam your hand into your side pocket-or your hip pocket-wherever you happen to be packing your $2.25-and keep an eye on all natives as they pass. Of course, we don't know yet-that is, I don't know yet -that this precaution ain't necessary. Even Coolidge (he was President of the United States, remember?) would look like a desperado in that environment. And while I'm just at the illest of ease, Theo exclaims, "Al, aren't you completely captivated by the ghosts of a former civilization that just haunt every nook and crevice?" "I guess so," I says, with a poor attempt at feeling at home, "but can't we get out in the open some place where we can see all this from a little distance? What's that line about 'distance lends enchantment'? Let's get out of here where the sun hits us." And I've no sooner filed my request than we turns out of this cell into the most beautiful street that ever stopped my looks. Theo tells me it's the El Prado -that's Spanish for something in English-and it's all of two hundred feet across with a fifty foot promenade of marble down the centre. On both sides of this walk is a hedge of tropical foliage which affords shelter from the sun and there's seats in the shade where you can rest the body and look the burg over. 

This is the pedestrians' paradise-and I don't mean crap shooters'. This must be several miles long, right through the heart of the city. Boy, if Boston could only trade in her cowpath for this El Prado thing, I'll bet they'd throw another tea party to celebrate.

After basking in the rays of old Sol a while, we grabs a cab and tells the toreador to show us his town and he does. Besides manipulating that old bus through crowds that would have made a New York taxi driver detour, he finds time to swivel his head

WORLD BEATERS 

21 right and left and free himself of a lecture on most everything that we pass. "The structure on our left is the new capitol building, costing ten millions of dollars, the interior decorating being done by Warring and Gillow, of London, England, at a cost of one and one-half millions. The building, while appearing to an American to be a twelve or fifteen story building, has but four stories. On the right is the -," and so he goes on ad finem-to the end-my first foreign country and I'm already speaking like a native. We turns into another of them beautiful boulevards-this one the El Calle, and it's equally as swell as Prado's -I don't know who the el-I mean-what the el stands for. This Calle stretch runs right into a mean dump that turns out to be nothing else but the palatial residence of President Gerardo Machado. Mister, what a drum he rates. According to our information bureau at the wheel, this Machado baby is not so dumb. It seems there's three political parties in Cuba -same as in America-Republican party and two 

Democratic parties-only in Cuba they calls them

Conservatives, Liberals and Independents. Well, this 

Machado matador takes exactly no chances, he belongs to all three parties and gets himself nominated as the parties' choice to all of them, so when the dear public trots to the polls to exercise their "X" rights as free citizens, they have to weigh the relative merits of all three candidates-Machado, Machado, and Machado, and cast their ballots according to the dictates of their consciences. After the returns are in from all precincts, it appears that Gerardo Machado has defeated the other two candidates by 1,327,110 to nothing what a victory. And we thought we had clever politicians in America-why, even Vare never thought of pulling that one. But imagine where a chap like Vare could get to, in a country where there's only

WORLD BEATERS 

a little over a million votes inside the four boundaries. 

With his $1,780,000.00 election expenditure he could buy all the votes in Cuba and give $452,890.00 to Bill

Thompson to help pay the running expenses of Chicago that is, figuring votes in Cuba at the market value of votes at home. But no matter how Machado got in, he's in and strong; and who ought to be living in the finest palace in the city if not the guy who's clever enough to work the trick he worked? This palace of Mac's has quite the garden around it—not to mention the two or three hundred armed guardsand it looks out over the most beautiful part of all Havana from the front door, and the servants' quarters overlook the sea-but anybody democratic enough to belong to three parties undoubtedly mingles with his servants-unless there's a Mrs. Mac-so he probably gets a view of the aqua too. And just here let me tip the hat to Gerardo for cleanliness. If anybody ever kept a city clean, Gerry does it in Havana. 

You could eat off the curb in any part of town and not need to even know the address of the Tanlac Company. 

What I'm waiting to have our taxi orator point out is the same thing you're wondering about-the places that lure thirsty Americans away from home. This doesn't happen so I asks the pilot of our gasburner if there was any truth in the rumor, current in New 

York, that one could quench his thirst in Havana without bidding for a berth at Sing Sing. He actually laughs in my face and says, "There are only 35,000 bodegas in Havana catering to the 500,000 inhabitants. If every resident in the city visited one bodega a day," he tells us, "there would only be fourteen customers a day and that wouldn't allow them enough profit to live. What keeps them going then?" he asks himself, "Americans. You never see a drunken

Cuban on the streets of Havana-only Americans." 

And then he laughs and laughs right in our faces. I'm just about to say something nasty when he brings the old bus to a stop and opening the door says, "There, boys, that's Sloppy Joe's, the most famous bar in Havana.' And looking in we sees several passengers from our boat trying to hold themselves up by the help of each other and the talk of them is disgusting. I still want to tell our chauffeur what I was about tobut taking another look into Sloppy Joe's I turns to 

Theo and says, "I guess we'll have to let this bullthrower give us the ha! ha!, Theo. What can you say in defense of that?" and I waves my hand at Joe's place. Now I'm not going to pull a Billy Sunday on you folks. I'll confess one of the big things I was looking forward to was a flyback to the days of old when a guy could wet his whistle if he liked-but listen and don't get me wrong-one look into Sloppy Joe's together with this Cuban's nasty crack and dirty laugh -folks, I felt something inside that I never felt before. I used to get the heebie jeebies listening to soapbox orators in Columbus Circle and Washington

Square trying to arouse in their hearers a sense of national pride-I thought it was the bunk-and I still do, for those at home, but I realized, for the first time, how come the people and the press of foreign countries had us down so low. I couldn't understand how

Americans - who at home were an egotistical, selfpraising gang of obvious successes, could go into another man's country and be such complete fools, brainless beyond hope. I turns to Theo, who is still in the cab-I'm standing with one foot on the running board. -and I says, "What are you thinking of, Theo?" And for the first time since we left Mac's place, Theo speaks. "Al," he says, "I'm as much against the 

Eighteenth Amendment as Governor Smith was and

WORLD BEATERS 

I considered it an affront to my intelligence as well as a usurpation of my inalienable rights as a born citizen of the United States to deprive me, by law, of something, which, taken in moderation, is admittedly unharmful claiming that, for my physical and moral welfare, it is better that I be kept entirely from it. 

I'm not a drunkard by any interpretation of the word, though I take and enjoy an occasional drink and I don't like to have to feel a criminal when I do it. It was the realization that in other countries I could be myself without breaking the law, that I looked forward to with considerable anticipation - but, Al, Havana has caused me to make a new resolution. It is obvious, from our driver's attitude, that the resident Cuban has come to expect the visiting American to indulge in a drunken debauch. They have, apparently, lost all respect for our countrymen. And the saddest part of it, Al, is, they are justified in that opinion. The resolution I have made, Al, is this, I am going around the world and show the peoples of the countries we visit that there is, at least, one American who still has some self-respect. I am not going to touch liquor in any form until this trip is over. Am I alone in this or are you with me?" "Shake," I says, "I may not be a very representative American, Theo, but I'll attempt to convince those we come in contact with that Americans can be away from home and sober."

I climbs back into the cab and the astonished driver, probably thinking we're already drunk, takes us for a run along the sea drive, one swell boulevard, believe me, out past the country club-and what a club-into the country and through the sugar cane fields of Cuba. 

I thought, if we only had another Burbank to cross the sugar cane with the coffee bean those rolling lands of Cuba would produce a million breakfasts each morning. Unless you're one of the two fellows in

WORLD BEATERS 

25 25 the U. S. A. who allows himself more than two minutes for breakfast, that coffee and rolls thing will go over your head like Lindbergh.

Well, that's Cuba-or all of it that you can prove by me and at night we're back in the city seeing it by Mazda. It's even prettier at night than in the daytime. There's just enough modern buildings in town, which, when all aglow with Mr. Edison's idea, gives the town a touch of America, but then when you cut through one of them side streets to get some place, the creeps run through you and you long for home. 

We don't see the Playfair sisters in all our travels and the morning after we had given Cuba the air and stole past them forts again under cover of darkness -and I must confess, as I stood out at the rear of the boat at 2 A. M. in my pajamas, and watched the flickering lights of Havana fade away and then just disappear like they had sunk in the sea, I was just a wee bit sad. But as I was saying, the next morning we arrives in the dining room for breakfast, beaming all over, awaiting K and B, so's we can compare notes on Cuba-but they don't show up. I looks over at the traveling delegates' table and it's vacant too. 

There's scarcely anybody in the room and I'm wondering if I'm on the right boat. If we've missed the floating university there'll be a man overboard almost immediately. Just then I hears the girls coming down the aisle and they enters at the other door and takes seats at the table where the old femmes had been. They don't see us at first and they don't see me even second, third or last. I gets a horrible idea that the cards have been mixed again and we're back where we started with the battle axes. That idea has scarcely come to a stop when I ducks out of the room and leaves everything to Theo. I immediately looks up the steward and he tells me the sweetest story about

WORLD BEATERS 

how those two delegates to the women's conference in South America had missed the boat in Havana and how sorry the captain was that he had to sail on schedule and leave them behind. I says, "Gosh, that was a tough break, wasn't it?" I fails to inform him that I meant it was a tough break for Havana. Then 

I asks him how come the Playfair sisters ain't at our table no more and he looks dumb and says, "Aren't they?" And I says, "Not by several tables." And so he takes me back to the dining room and would you believe it? I know you wouldn't, if we hadn't made that resolution in Havana-it was me and Theo who had made the mistake. The two tables were in the same position on opposite sides of the boat and we had entered from the wrong side and it is US who would have been back with the Amazons if a kind fate hadn't got them lost in Havana. What a narrow call that was! So we trips over to our proper places and exchanges happy "Hellos" and "How did you enjoy Cuba's."

Being polite we gives the girls the floor and hears what happened to them in Cuba. It seems they're in school all right both on board and ashore. They was met at the dock by representatives from the University of Havana and showed the city by that body and then taken out to the college where they suffered through several addresses of welcome and a couple more on what they're trying to do for the Cuban youths. It's plain to see that K and B was bored with it all-the only bright spot in the whole affair coming when they escaped from the gang for a few hours and got over to Sloppy Joe's and got some real good drinks. And then they looks at each other and says, "and we succeeded in smuggling a few bottles aboard so we can have the odd cocktail." 

Me and Theo looks at each other kind of funny

WORLD BEATERS 

27 like, and I guess the girls feel a sinking sensation-at least they should of because they dropped plenty in our estimation-to think that girls like them, college girls, that's supposed to have brains-to think they didn't realize what even a dumbbell like me could see. No wonder that taxi driver tried to amuse us with his rotten stories about American girls. Was it any wonder he thought the things he did when refined looking girls like K and B go to a dump like Sloppy Joe's, without even a male escort, and probably drink all they can stand and carry away all they can lift.

I wanted to get up on the table and preach a sermon to them but Theo, reading my mind, gives me a kick under the table, which plainly says, "This is a problem for a diplomat-leave it to me." So I swallows a gem of a lecture on "The Volstead Act for Women" and leaves it at that. 

That day we don't see much of the sisters except at the table, and conversation then is noticeably stifled -Theo contributing little more than a "yes" or "no."

That evening after we had mixed freely with the other guests on the dance floor, K suggested the four of us go to their room for a highball before the curfew went off. We're in a bad fix now, me and Theo, and for once I was perfectly willing that Theo air his Yale, while I merely become the background. We're off in one corner by ourselves and K, sensing the tension, says, "What's the matter? You act as though we were offering you some cheap bootleg liquor-this is good Cuban rye. What else could a gentleman do but go? But when we gets to the girls' room Theo remembers his resolution and speaks for both of us, thusly, "Girls, this is positively the most embarrassing position I have ever found myself in. I have, perhaps egotistically, prided myself on possessing an enviable 99

WORLD BEATERS 

vocabulary, and never have I experienced the discomfort of one who gropes for a word or a phrase to properly convey his thought, but this evening I become a thirty-three degree member of his brotherhood. You must pardon any attacks of stoppage or stuttering as 

I unburden myself." And he goes ahead and tells them of our experience with the taxi driver in Havana and of what we seen at Sloppy Joe's and about our resolution and then he becomes evangelistic and attempts to win K and B over to our way of thinking and he hasn't more than got started when B opens the porthole and swish, four quarts of rye goes to Davy 

Jones' locker. And turning to her sister, B says, "What a couple of blind fools we are. And we would slap that Spaniard's face for getting fresh at Joe's; he was perfectly justified in thinking that we were 'low-down'." Then, facing us, she goes on, "Theo, if you two keep your word there's going to be, at least, four Americans girdle the globe, sober." We shake all round and once again I'm proud I'm an American.

The next day passes with few or no events of importance and the next morning we are all up early to see ourselves enter one of the wonders of the world. 

We had glided from the Caribbean sea into the harbor that guards the entrance to one of the engineering feats of all time. A little while and we were entering the famous Gatun locks, where, in three attempts, we were hoisted eighty-five feet to the level of Lake 

Gatun. I heard a passenger next to me saying that this was the largest artificially formed lake in the world. Just imagine, it's eighty-five feet above the 

Atlantic and there's only a ridge between them. It's twenty-three miles across this lake and we take the crookedest course I ever did see. I doubt if there's a crookeder course any place-outside of the course a large element of Chicagoans follow-and all about the

WORLD BEATERS 

29 29 lake are tiny islands and bare trunks of trees, standing up like scarecrows in a corn field. The weather through these parts is ideal and it's quite a change from the mosquito-infested swamps that was here when they started this short cut to Hollywood. The specks of islands and the mainland are grown as thick as jungle with all forms of trees, shrubs and flowers, and here and there is a native's hut with thatched roof and every window full of wondering faces. Up the gentle slopes running back from the lake, are great herds of cattle-they must be those "contented cows" they get condensed milk from-they sure look happy, up to their necks in clover and nothing to do but eat. 

From this we nose into Gaillard Cut, a ditch 300 feet wide, 45 feet deep and 8 miles long-blasted through rock almost the entire length. We're taking in the view together, when Theo, properly figuring that there must be bits of information about this thing that has slipped my memory, says, "You know, Al, it was these eight miles that so defied man's persistent efforts to break through as to make the whole enterprise look hopeless. But challenge, to the determination of real men, is fuel to the fires of ambition and so, with rock and fever saying 'No', the latter having already taken a toll of 28,000 lives, the ingenuity of a civil engineer sprayed swamps with oil, defeating the mosquito; his determination laughed at landslides and his ambition ignored the resistance of rock. What a demonstration of sheer courage it was, a feat to stagger the imagination. Flanked on all sides by seemingly insurmountable obstacles strewn along its course, a sinister warning to those who would disembowel nature, were the bleached white bones of failure; and, still, into this forbidden land, this playground of hell men dared to go, dared to do and dared to conquer. What a sermon it preaches.' 99

WORLD BEATERS 

Can you feature a guy with a line like that being stopped for words when he has something to tell a broad?

Soon we're through the Cut and, by the help of a couple more elevators, our boat is dropped down to the level of the Pacific ocean and we steam along to Balboa, on the extreme end, where we tie up a few hours while the passengers go ashore and visit Panama City. We've only been about six hours crossing from the Atlantic to the Pacific and just think, the Panama Canal, so Theo tells me, cuts seven thousand miles off the old route round the Horn. But the biggest surprise of all was when I learnt that after we got across to the Pacific end of the canal we was farther East than when we left the Atlantic. Oh, one certainly picks up things when one travels, now don't one? We spend a few hours in Panama City—which is more than ample-it being merely a poor copy of 

Havana-minus the pretty boulevards, capitol building and cleanliness. I guess it was too hot for me and 

Theo to appreciate it for there must of been some beauty spots from the way the artists were running around the place, doing them in oil-one almost thought himself in Wall Street-by the way, were you ever done in oil? Yes, one could almost dismiss Panama City by stating that the climate was hot but the city wasn't. I suppose another reason this man's town didn't register with us was because K and B was again under the professors' eyes and we were lamping the burg alone. But by sundown we're all back on deck and getting organized for the nine-day run to Los 

Angeles-"where summer spends the winter." As we pull away from Balboa we pass Uncle Sam's submarine base, just inside the mouth of the canal, and boy, they're wicked looking babies, what I mean. With a couple of them looking up at the bottom of your boat and a squadron of Sam's army planes looking down

WORLD BEATERS 

31 at you from the clouds you're mighty glad you know the words to the "Star Spangled Banner.'

Them nine days along the coast of Southern California-I don't know just where the southern limits of Los Angeles begins but I suppose the last three or four days we was opposite some of their latest subdivisions-I don't know how many minutes it would be to the station-but anyway, it was mighty pretty scenery. We and the girls traded lots of remarks over our afternoon teas, as we looked up into the Rockies or out across what Theo referred to as the "silvery sea."

Then one morning, it must of been about the ninth, we comes to a full stop in San Pedro harbor in the midst of all the battleships in the world. I think I counted seventeen gunboats almost within a stone's throw of us. I'm thinking how lucky we are that our country owns them when Theo goes off on another track. "Al," he asks, "are these mighty dreadnoughts arguments for peace or war? If foreigners, would this, our first impression, be good or bad? Are they evidence of one country's faith in another? Do they reason for the universal brotherhood of man?" Then

Theo looks at me for his answers, expecting, as usual, to find me stumped, but I fools him. I calls all my resources into play and I throws back a like volley of questions. "But Theo," I begins, "would it be wise to destroy them? Would the country that owns them command the same respect of other countries if she was to be without them? Would we, who are opposed to war, vote to have them battleships junked?" Then it's my turn to wait for answers. Instead of answers gets a slap on the back, with, "Bravo, bravo, I didn't think it was in you, Al. You belong in Washington or Geneva." But, no foolin' them boats is something to think about.

Again we must bid farewell to the dames and go

22 

WORLD BEATERS 

ashore as womanless as Robinson Crusoe and his man Friday. We takes the tender into San Pedro, where every other man on the street is a "gob"-and every other one is a sailor. It's about as attractive as ten years in Leavenworth and it's from here we start over the twenty-three mile drive to Los Angeles. You'll probably think I don't belong to the Chamber of Commerce at Los Angeles from the way I boost that twenty-three mile stretch from San Pedro harbor to town. It's the bleakest, dreariest, most God-forsaken piece of roadway in America, and I haven't got any real estate in Florida to sell, either-it ain't that-I mean it's a poor approach to a real city, in spite of real estate men not because of them. We also done 

Beverly Hills and Hollywood and them places but they ain't paying me anything for advertising so they don't get any-I guess that'll make them mad, eh?

The only stunning girls we met while there was at the Warner Bros. studio, and I thought they'd be a couple of the stars but they told us they were making a tour of the world with a relative and a private tutor. It seems anybody of any importance is making a tour of the world. But Miss Durant and Miss London ain't traveling on our boat, so I forgets them, with difficulty.

Another jaunt along the coast and we finds ourselves passing through the Golden Gate into San Francisco harbor. And, man, what a harbor they've got at

San Francisco. We spend several days in this town which is rightly called the "meeting place of nations."

It was our peculiar fortune to get our first bird's-eye view of San Francisco at night. Docking late in the afternoon we stays on board for dinner and what should happen but the girls let us in on a secret, if we promise not to breathe it to a soul. It seems an older couple from the student body used to live here and they know every foot like a cobbler. They gets

WORLD BEATERS 

33 permission from the faculty to take a party of students through the city at night and K and B have joined the party, and they plan to give the gang the air once they get ashore and if we ain't doing anything that evening we can meet them at the Palace Hotel at nine o'clock. 

Was we happy? Don't ask. Well, about two weeks later, nine o'clock drags round, like a snail with rheumatism-I never put in such a long two hours in all my life-and the girls show up per schedule. What to do? That's the question-then like when any problem needs solving, Theo speaks up. "I was thinking, since it's a beautiful moonlight night, how thrilling it would be to stand atop one of the Twin Peaks and look down on the bejeweled cities of Frisco bay and watch the lights twitch and flicker on the surface of the water like gems in the moonlight." And looking right past us, like he's in a trance, he goes on, "It would be as though we had climbed beyond the heavens and looked down upon the starry firmament." The girls' mouths are open like a pup's on a hot day—

Theo simply has them wowed off their feet—and they exclaims, "Oh, let's." Just like there was nothing else in all the world to do. But it's a good idea-even if 

I didn't think of it myself—and so we starts out arm in arm for Twin Peaks. I'll never believe different than that Theo had been there before in the moonlight. The most perfect imagination couldn't have pictured the view from Twin Peaks as accurately as Theo had done in the hotel. It was everything he had thought it should be, and then the associations, can you imagine anything more divine, unless it would be a similar hike up the other peak. We gets back to the boat at 12:30 and the girls succeed in getting to their cabin without being detected and it's the end of a perfect day-and a perfect night, too.

I'd like to rave about the business section of San 

Francisco, her hotels, her restaurants, Golden Gate

WORLD BEATERS 

Park, her open-air concert hall, museum, lake, Japanese tea-gardens; about the stage celebrities she produced, of the concert singers and men of literary fame who hailed from there-but I don't want to get in wrong with Los Angeles-if there's one thing I can't stand it's partiality-heh, heh. 

But, as we glides through Golden Gate a few days later Theo turns to me and says, "You know, Al, Kipling was right. San Francisco has only one drawback." "And what's that," I asks, being behind with my Kipling. "It's hard to leave."

When we're out of sight of America everybody realizes for the first time what a great place it is. A spirit of fellowship seems to be abroad that you didn't recognize before. I guess everybody realizes what a long time it will be before we see the Statue of Liberty again, and they seem to have a sense of union. You know that "you-stick-by-me-and-I'll-stick-by-you” feeling. We're going to be strangers in a strange man's land, where a mistake might mean your name don't appear on no more voters' lists. I'm already wishing 

I'd smuggled one of them submarines aboard or had a folding dirigible in my bag. But whatever it is that makes the difference I'm indebted to it, because that tension that existed before is gone and the other ninety-eight students suddenly become as friendly as K and B-almost-the professors getting so democratic as to let us attend some of the lectures with the students. Of course that was like old times for Theo, but the idea of a "ain't hound" like me attending university was as out of place as Al Smith would of been in the White House. I remember one class we sat in on-I think it was "trigger-nometry," a special course for Chicagoans, which teaches how to guage the distance your victim might be from a given point, this point they call the 'sine', there's always a sign near by and the measurements are taken from that, the course

WORLD BEATERS 

335 guarantees to double your percentage by increasing the accuracy of your aim. Oh, it was an interesting class, and just at the end of the lecture the whistle blew. It was Thursday afternoon fire-drill, when the sailors man the life boats and give exhibitions of getting into life belts, and the professor says, "Now everybody on deck and pay particular attention to the adjusting of the life belt because the new 1930 model of bullet-proof vests, which the store will be showing by the time you reach home, are adjusted the same way." Oh, I tell you, this is a modern university. They may not give a course in dietetics but they sure teach you how to live longer.

One afternoon the girls persuaded us to sit in on an art class and I'm telling you it was quite the show. 

This dame from New York who handled the whip sure was acquainted with her crayons. As Theo observed, "Her silhouettes are fairly animated." You can look up what he meant sometime when you're working a cross-word puzzle-I just mention it because it looks good in a book. I'm beginning to appreciate the faculty of this school. They're really clever.

So this is how we killed the hours and the days the first week out from America and then one morning we're all up on deck about 6:30. It seems, according to the girls, back in 1893 there was an old flame on the throne by the monicker of Queen Liliuokalani, who got such a swelled head over her job that her crown fell off and she was out of a job—just like that—and since they didn't have any royal employment bureau in Honolulu -like they seems to have in Afghanistan-the old girl was out in the weather, without even a palace, and then the people asked to be annexed to the U. You know I don't think there's anything makes you feel more at home in a strange place than a smile from a cop. Maybe it's the conscience pricking, I don't know, but I'd like Honolulu if it was for nothing more than her happy cops. If they ever lose their jobs I'll speak to Jimmy Walker about a place on his staff. 

It's getting rather late in the afternoon and the girls were only supposed to be away a few hours so we drives them to their hotel and, after fond farewells and hopes of seeing them again somewhere, we beats it back to the boat because we sail about dinner time.

The native boys are boarding the boat and lining up on the life-boats, diving for coins tossed in the water by passengers. It's about an eighty foot dive and them chaps would make a seal look like a landlubber. They jack knife, toe kiss, bow rib and everything that was ever done in a bathing suit on their descent from the life boats. It's a picture to watch and that is just one more of the many touches of native color that helps to paint such a beautiful picture in your mind. And then, as we sails away from the Isle

I see by the bulletin next morning where we arrive in Japan at four P. M. the following day. That's when we leave this ship for good. As you've probably guessed, the next day immediately follows and four o'clock ain't long in coming round. As we enters the harbor at Kobe we gets our first glimpse of the Orient. 

The harbor looks like 42d street at 5th Ave. on Saturday noon, with fishermen's small sailing boats and sampans-I'm speaking Japan already—that's "sliteye" for row-boat. Kobe looks like a town of 600,000, built along some seven miles of the coast and back nearly two miles to the mountain range that seems to run through the centre of Japan, from one end to the other like a spinal column. I guessed at the population after reading the article about it on the back of the day's menu, where it said there were probably 600,000 inhabitants-it's quite a busy station from

We exchanges goodbyes with passengers who are going on with the boat and we get off with the floating university. The students are taken along immediately to the railway station when they leave for Kyoto, but me and Theo roams around Kobe for a few hours. 

The first experience we have with a native in the Orient is not so good-not for us. We hires a couple rickishaws at the pier and instruct the boys to take us to the money exchange in town. After a ten-minute trot they drop the handles of our two-wheeled baby buggies and points to a sign which says in English "Money 

Exchanged" and we goes in while Theo buys $20.00 worth of that Japanese Iron. They tells us at the desk what each piece is worth but by the time we gets back outside where the rickishaw boys are waiting for their fee we've both forgot even the names of the pieces let alone what they're worth-so Theo, with plenty of faith in his fellowman, holds the lot out to the boys to take their legitimate charge. They picks off three or four pieces, which seems fair enough, and it was fare enough. When we later learn and remember what this junk is worth we realize it cost Theo $4.00 for the first ten-minute ride in the Orient-the legal tariff was ten cents. If you could see Kobe when all natives were under cover you could almost imagine yourself in a middle class American seaport as far as city plan and architecture is concerned; but when you have to side-step rickishaws, listen to the clatter of them wooden shoes that are built up on stilts, hear a garbled chatter that passes for talk, and look into their pans-boy, you just know you're away from home or else Big Ben is leaving you sleep in.

Since Kobe has little more to offer the tourist than a rickishaw gypping-and anyways the girls have left town-we invests in a couple of one-way pasteboards to Kyoto-a jaunt of some five hours by the iron horse. 

The station was packed and I was feeling good about having nothing to look after but yours truly-our bags having been sent to the Miyaka Hotel in Kyoto. After passing through a turnstile to the platform, where a much uniformed Jap punches your ticket, a train pulls in and the battle is on. Try and get on one of them cars. That camel and needle act that Matthew used to stage would of been a washout in Kobe. There was plenty customers on that platform for nine cars and when that train of three cars left Kobe everybody was gone from the platform, including me and the boy friend. It was cruelty to animals to herd them into that car in the first place but to have to stand this double-check book-keeping system is criminal. I borrow enough of that iron from Theo, which passes for money, to fill my glove and if another of them inspectors tries to worm his way through I'm going to tip him right behind his ear with the works. But it seems. they was short of men that day and was getting along with just the two checkers.

The shops are one-story affairs about as substantial looking as those along the boardwalk in Atlantic City. The next morning we're just out sunning ourselves before lunch and we strolls down a block and into a side street and my face turns red and I looks at Theo, who is trying to get mad with a comical grin on his face. "And you thought they were all in New York, didn't you?" I asks Theo, and Theo only says, "Can you beat it?" And I says, "No, but I can beat that taxi driver and his whole family-and I'm not forgetting that that might mean an army—and I will if I ever see him again on this side of the Jordan." This was the exact spot where we had hailed a taxi the day before and it took that slit-eye thirty minutes to go around the corner to our hotel. "But it might have been worse," Theo put in. "How," I asks. "It might have been a train instead of a taxi," he answers, and 

I feels better.

Now folks, Theo informs me-and that makes it a fact that there's been all of several volumes written on Japan and every other country they got over here, so there'll likely be a few little things about some of the countries that I won't be able to speak of since 

I've only got enough paper for the one book. From what I've overheard already the main industry in the Orient is religions, and I think, well, that's one big thing off my mind because what I know about all religions could be written on the picture side of a stamp without reducing the distance it could take a letter by

WORLD BEATERS 

51 so much as a fraction of zero. But I'm not allowed to remain in this blissful state of ignorance long because Theo says, "Al, let us go out and visit the famed temples and shrines of Kyoto." What can I do but submit and I takes a sympathetic look at my dogs because I read in a folder the night before where Kyoto had, at one time, boasted over three thousand Buddhist temples and Shinto shrines-back when Kyoto was the capital of Japan-and that over fourteen hundred are still in a good condition of repair and doing a paying business every day. Imagine, 1400, and Theo suggests that we visit them. It turns out that I've been on worse sight-seeing trips. Most of them temples have the most picturesque settings one could imagine -this one set on the side of a hill, overlooking a beautiful mountain stream-that one in the centre of a heavily wooded park and another half hid behind gorgeous flowers. The style of them too is interesting because I've never seen anything like them before. 

You know them many storied pagodas you've seen in pictures of Japan-well, they're Shinto shrines where, Theo tells me, "The believers in Shintoism go to make their daily offerings of food and drink to the spirits of their departed loved ones, thereby appeasing their wrath and winning the favor of the gods." Now a mugwamp like me wouldn't dare to criticize anybody's religion the faithful Jap placing a roll of bread dough and a cup of water on his kin's tomb or at the base of his shrine, with a prayer, may have the right idea, or his fellow-countryman, who walks by the 

Shinto shrine to the temple and prays to Buddha, might have the proper dope about this thing called religion-I don't want any part in a discussion on that -but I do say this in favor of the Jap; right or wrong, he sure is a faithful believer and practices what he professes to believe and that's more than can be said for the so-called believers of some other religions I

WORLD BEATERS 

know about. All of these churches (or what are they) are made of wood with great hand-hewn timbers. The remarkable thing is how they have resisted the elements of time. Some of them are a thousand years old and look as new as this year's model home out in White Plains, N. Y. I can't figure it out why wood in Japan should last so much longer than the same material in America. I remember the only thing I ever got without working for, besides this trip, was when my grandfather took the air and left me the family homestead. I had often heard my father tell about how his dad went into that primitive mountain district and literally hewed a home out of that mighty forest and I was looking forward to having quite the place, don't you know. So, after everything had been straightened up and I had the deed to the old homestead, I took a party of friends up to my mountain lodge for the week end. After making more enquiries than is necessary to find your way round Boston, we eventually found the birthplace of my sire. Suffice to say that I got a raw deal from my dad's old man. 

That place was only 72 years out of the trees and any insurance company in the world would cancel its policy on a member who dared to risk his life by entering that original tumble-down shack. But out here in Japan a temple stands the wear and tear of a thousand years and don't think it ain't used-it's a bad day for the monk business that several hundred don't tramp through and it's usually thousands. We took in several dozen of these places and after the first 24 or 36 they looks pretty much alike. But there's a few that stood out, even in my mind, like the Heian shrine, almost lost in the great gardens of irises, wistaria and azaleas; the Kinkaku-ji or Gold Pavilion, built in 1397, was once covered with gold foil, much of which still remains. Its gardens are almost as beautiful as the 

Heian shrine. Then there was the Ginkaku-ji or Silver

WORLD BEATERS 

53 33 Pavilion; Chion-in temple, a representative Buddhist monastery in the centre of Maruyama Park, Kyoto's main public park. They really are worth seeing but what they really need is a Charlie Schwab or a Doheney to go over there and consolidate them. If they had all the iron they collect at the 1400 stations coming into, say, fifty-and if they had good live-wire monks in charge, twenty-five should do-just imagine what dividends them twenty-five could pay; and that would release 1375 good men for the rice fields—or maybe the railway company would put them on triple checking the conductor.

One thing I notice about the Japanese, they seem to be a naturally happy people. No matter where you walk through town there are kiddies out in the road playing a game something like badminton-but they're clever we keep the feathers going with a racket, they keep it in the air by kicking it with the heel of the foot out to one side. Try it, if you think it's simple. I sprained both my ankles and wrenched my favorite back trying to connect with the thing just once that way. And these kids are laughing and the grown-ups will stop and watch them and cheer and root like they're watching Bobbie Jones drop a thirty-foot putt.

The masses may not have everything we're used to at home but I doubt if we get as great a kick out of living. Would you expect to get away with your life if you asked the Mrs. to carry her dirty clothes down. to the creek that runs hard by the town and swish them around in the water and beat them against the rocks until they were clean? You'd know better. Well, that's what the masses do in Japan and they're actually happy while they do it. Try and find a home with a Hoover over there. They keep the place clean with a handful of switches and they keep it clean too. They wouldn't know what a Frigidaire was and if they heard a radio they'd likely do a Nurmi to the nearest Shinto

WORLD BEATERS 

shrine or call on Buddha to save them. And any family with an automobile is probably related to royalty. It struck me funny, you know, and I remarks to Theo, "Ain't it queer, Theo, how nature strikes a balance? At home we got all the conveniences and luxuries of life but we ain't happy, and here these people are without everything, almost, and still there's more real joy and happiness in one block than we got in lots of whole towns." "Yes," adds the boy friend, "the law of compensation is ever at work in all the universe. If we choose to utilize all the time at our disposal in pursuit of that commodity which will enable us to purchase the material things of life we will, it is logical to assume, come into the possession of much of this world's goods. But, if this be our choice, we must resign ourselves to the fate of the lonely. We cannot have the joys of companionship because we do not take the time that is necessary to cultivate them, and so the end comes, only to bring the realization that our worldly desires have been gratified-but, oh, how ephemeral is the satisfaction and how sad the heart as it contemplates, when it is all too late, that those things that were bought with gold had a deceptive glitter-and so, a life that was all consumed with gaining the wherewithal to buy the conveniences and luxuries of life, now sits in their midst, shrouded in sadness. And here, here we have just the reverse-whatever the innate differences in peoples which are responsible for their desires, I do not profess to know, but here, where the lack of desire or the lack of knowledge, or the lack of opportunity or whatever it is that deprives these people of the things we strive for in America, we find the very essence of happiness. Isn't it a wonderful scheme, Al, this divine plan?" "Theo," I says, coming to, "you took the words right out from behind my molars. And

I think it's a great law-this compensation idea-but

WORLD BEATERS 

55 if it must be one or the other, I'll sign, seal and deliver, free of all dower and encumbrances, my interest in said creek and accept in exchange one fully equipped bath room with modern American plumbing." Theo only sighs, then, smiling, claims I'm hopeless. 

Kyoto is called the "Fine Arts City of Japan" because of its artistic hand-made products, such as lacquer, silk embroidery, brocades, kimonos, bronzes, porcelains, fans, dolls, bamboo ware and oodles of other things. It's great to be able to nose into these establishments like you was a vice-president and look over a guy's shoulder while he does his stuff. I tried to get through the Victrola factory in Camden once to see how them things was turned out and, after being given a seat in the waiting room of the main office and told to wait just a moment-which I did for two and one-half hours-the girl comes over and says how sorry she is but she hadn't been able to get me a pass to go through. Of course, if a dumbbell like meor you-was to be allowed to tramp round a factory where there's more machinery per square inch than inside a watch, we'd probably be the same as a monkey wrench, but over here in Kyoto where a silk factory maybe has three or four Japs running as many centuryold hand looms, there really ain't the same chance of holding up production. 

That afternoon we dared to board another train for an hour's run to Nara. This time, however, we happens to stumble into a plush pullman and it must of been a mistake for we were the only ones in the car and what a joy that was. Reaching Nara we dickered with rickishaw boys for their go-carts for a couple hours and started out to see this famous deer park we had heard so much about, and the snappy bust of Buddha they rave over. This park turns out to be really as advertised. I've seen plenty of parks in my time but this Nara place has THE park. There's

WORLD BEATERS 

hundreds of acres, just thick with cryptomeria trees and the drives worm through the woods like in Central 

Park, over 'ome. And I never saw so many deer in one place in all my life-not even in Ziegfeld Follies. 

The natives have souvenir stands through the park, where they also sell a kind of hard-tack biscuit which you're supposed to buy and feed the deer. It's great fun. These little scamps come running alongside your rickishaw actually crying for biscuits and they take them right out of your hand. There'll be five or six licking your hand at once-and I never so much as lost a finger. We pass the great five-story pagoda built, so the boy says, in 1426-long before Queen' 

Lizzie hocked her wrist watch to send Mr. Columbus to find America for her-and it's still in much better repair than my mountain lodge. A little farther on and we come to the thing that really puts Nara on the map. Now, personally, this park and the deer should be ample to bring anybody to town, but the Japs hand first prize to the Daibutsu, a bust of Buddha that stands fifty-three and one-half feet, rather it sits not stands. As I gets it, this boy Buddha had a bad case of dropsy and whenever he stopped he dropped down into a squatting position with his legs crossed and his hands out-that's about the only hangover our ministers have today from Buddha-their hands outand so, while only the trunk of the body is noticeable, one gets the idea of it being only a bust, but it's all there if you look close. And don't think he's just sitting on the ground-this baby rates a lotus petal. 

But anyway, it's quite the bust and I turns to Theo, knowing how he hates puns, and I says, "Why, I didn't know Buddha played the stock market." And Theo looks at me, like I'm screwy and just says, in that tone of utter despair, "Alfred." And I says, "Well, I see he's busted." It seems, Theo, no matter how foolish 

I am, can't resist being amused and he laughs and says,

WORLD BEATERS 

57 "Al, if Buddha was on Wall Street today, he'd be too wise to play the market." And I says, "Well, it wouldn't matter which way he turned to escape they'd get him. You know, Theo, the East River's at one end of Wall Street and a cemetery at the other." Theo gives up.

We got back to Kyoto that night without any casualties and there was a dance at the hotel after dinner -quite a nice affair-and we had to dress for it. K and 

B, we learned, had gone to some lake in a party and weren't back yet so my eyes went round the dance hall like the tour of inspection they was on in the sun room of the Royal Hawaiian Hotel when they discovered Miss Durant and her friend. This time they bring back a unique report indeed. Over in one corner of the hall I sees something wrapped up in a Parisian creation that promises to be interesting. I only gets a flash at her profile but that's plenty encouraging. 

She's with a party of Japs, all dressed in native garb, and I'm wondering can she, too, be a Japanese. Just then she turns her head in my direction and sure enough, she's one of them. But she's plenty different. 

In the first place her gown would attract attention at the Ritz and her glistening black hair was cut in a wind-blown bob; silk chiffon hose tried desperately to conceal two beautiful extremities and high-heeled pumps finished them off. Now, I'm claiming this numpumps is something to think about, even as a Jap. I wasn't too far away to see that her facial features were beautiful and her makeup was a piece of artno foolin'. That's where she stood apart from her native sisters who are over-made-up. If you think

different. I motioned the hotel hostess over to our table and pointed this dame out to her and asked if there was any chance of meeting her. She tells me she'll see and a couple minutes later we're meeting

Miss Tashamoto-the pride of Kyoto.
