It appears, in Japan, when you desire a date with a Miss Tashamoto for instance, the proper thing to do is to take the matter up with 

Mr. Tashamoto first; the "pick-up" business in Japan is nil. If Mr. H. Ford ever expects to develop Japan as a market for his coupes he'll first have to change that old custom. 

The next morning we favors the Imperial Palace with our presence and it ain't what I thought it was. 

It's not so bad, you understand, but it ain't my idea of what I'd have if I was king. The buildings suggest those temporary types you see at exhibitions like the Sesqui-centennial at Philadelphia a while agogaudy but unreal. The grounds are pretty but I wouldn't feel that my job was safe if I came home after a busy day of laying cornerstones and had to

WORLD BEATERS 

59 walk into such a so-called palace. Of course, the royal family don't live here any more-that's how we come to get in-but they still have their coronations here and according to the rules, always will. I suppose my disappointment come in finding everything built of wood-and my grandfather is responsible for my being prejudiced against wood, but I always thought of a palace in terms of towering edifices of stone, granite and marble; and to find this one-story frame building running all over a twenty-acre lot, it just didn't fit in with my plans somehow. But don't let this keep you away from palaces.

When we got back to the hotel for lunch at noon, one of the boys from the university invited us to join a party of students who are going up into the mountains to spend the night at a Buddhist monastery. He tells us that this place is 1500 years old and not a soul around the place speaks a word of English. He further relates that in all them years only nineteen foreigners have ever visited it and part of this number was the Prince of Wales and his party. He makes it sound so spooky I don't know if I want to go or not and then he says the Playfair sisters will be in the group and I can't wait to get going. After getting the Jap at the desk to write us a letter to the head monk, telling who we are and what we want and why, we start out for the Shigisan monastery. By streetcar to the edge of town, train to the base of a distant mountain and funicular railway to the peak, we are eventually ready to commence the overland hike to the monk mill. By a stone walk, about two miles long, gradually ascending, we actually gain a view of our objective; down in a peaceful valley, the sun brilliantly reflected off the red-tiled roof of the monastery, nestles Shigisan. We continued on our way, now on the down grade, and at last comes to a full stop in front of the main gate. A much-bearded but kind-

WORLD BEATERS 

faced monk met us, read the letter we presented and with a smile of welcome in his whiskers motioned us to follow him. We were led to the door of the main building and by frantic gestures he convinced us that we were not to enter any part of the monastery with our shoes on, so we traded him our boots for a pair of felt slippers and made our triumphal entry, as they say in history books. They got quite the establishment up there, a dozen buildings or so and the grounds are terraced like the great rice fields in the foothills of the mountains-only here the terraces are covered with myriad hues of flowers. The buildings, of course, are of wood, with red-tile roofs; which, by the way, is the usual roofing material used throughout Japan on palace and hovel alike. The floors are waxed and you can imagine how polished they naturally would be with nothing but felt slippers skidding over them all the time, and, take it from one who failed, walking on them floors without falling is some feat for the feet. At last we're escorted up a narrow stairway and showed into a suite of rooms which is furnished with exactly nothing. Having no place to go and no one to talk to but ourselves, (which wasn't so bad, K and 

B are with us, you know) we do start wondering what it's all about when in comes a couple more inmates with cushions and pots of coals, one of each for everyone-it gets cold up there at night-and we squat on our cushions and instinctively starts rubbing our hands over our pots of coals. We sits around there telling ghost stories 'til I'm getting kind of creepy feelings myself and by now the sun has set and we gets some lights of one candle power each-how could one candle give more power than that-and then our hostess returns and we're expecting it's dinner time. But instead he goes into a pantomime which tells us lots less than nothing at all and we are sure in a sorry mess. We know the poor fellow has something on his mind but

WORLD BEATERS 

61 with all the jujitsu he can summon he can't throw it off. He goes for help and his assistant beckons us into a room with a big metal trough about six feet square and nearly as deep filled with water, and a fire underneath has it almost boiling. He holds his stomach to indicate hunger, then he picks up a dish of food that the other one has brought in and is about to eat when he has the food taken from him and is pointed. to the wash-tub. Then he goes through the motions of taking a bath and is handed the groceries and it sinks into our skulls that apparently one must bathe before eating in this dump. So the girls go in first and wash the bodies and then the boys do likewise-I don't know how many years that water's been there or how many baths it's been responsible for, but it cleaned up fourteen of us that time and I never got such a parboiling in my life. But after the swim the monks smile and bring in fourteen dishes of the most atrocious food I ever hope to lay an eye on. There was about ten different kinds of vitamines on each plate and, honest to Hoover, you couldn't get a calorie down your throat if your ribs was playing traps against your spinal column. Boy, it was a great argument for matrimony. Here was a specimen of food from a joint where they've no use for women and I gets more friendly with K than ever. We all buries parts of our meals in the pots of coals, so the chef won't get discouraged, and leave them, but not a morsel of food passed the lips of a soul—that is, not to stay-and to think we had to take baths to get ready for that. turns to Theo and says, "This'll be tough on you, Theo. I don't suppose you've ever missed a meal before in your life." And he laughs and says, "Well,

I guess I can stand it, I've attended a few banquets in my time."

After dinner, rather, after dinner-time, we goes down to the door and gets into our shoes and starts

WORLD BEATERS 

out to explore the hills before retiring. We found a path leading up a mountain side with arches across the path every eight or ten feet with lanterns burning, so we starts up. It was almost a mile to the top-and the arches and lights was all the way-and at the top was a shrine where we found a monk chanting his prayers. It seems there's one on duty all the time. It was a full moon and you could see the lights of distant cities and glimmers on far-off mountain peaks, and all about was the great silent forest with spooky shadows everywhere made by the moonbeams, and nearby, in the little shrine, the droning monk. It was the strangest of all sensations that passed over me. The world didn't seem real and it must of struck us all alike for no one spoke-which made it worse-and for some reason we never spoke of it later, either. We had descended from the peak, the whole distance, and gone back to the main building before anyone broke the silence and, strange though it may seem, it was none other than your own little correspondent who broke it and I was so weak from that hike and so famished for food that it's still a miracle to me how I had enough strength to even break the silence. I says, "Well, here's where we get another lesson on how to sneak in on the wife at 2 A.M.," as we all sits down on the porch and takes off our shoes and enters in our stocking feet.

We finds our way back to the same rooms without meeting a soul and what do we find but fourteen beds made on the floor, seven in a room. It was the first time I had ever slept on the floor but, boy, I was so tired I could of slept in that trick bathtub, upside down. With a few goodnights and a "Hope you're all alive in the morning" from some crepehanger, we ducks the lights and goes into a coma, not even caring whether we come out of it or not-but we do-and how. About 4:30 in the morning one of them monks

WORLD BEATERS 

68 gets up to practise on a drum right at our door and on investigation we decides it's the alarm clock and we're being called to prayer in the temple. So we drags ourselves out from between the mattresses and tries to fight off the rigors as we gets into our clothes. At 

5:30 we're all lined up in the monastery temple and put through our paces as good Buddhists are. The meeting is handled by six monks, dressed in more clothes than I thought a fellow could hold up, and they take turns in ringing bells, striking cymbals, clanging triangles, stopping between each to drone a prayer I guess and then getting caught up with their music again. The whole affair took about thirty minutes and then the meeting broke up and we beats it back to the main tent and gets our shoes and I suggests, before anybody gets a chance to come across with a different idea, that we tip these boys and beat it back to Japan. Thus it was, that before sunup, we were heading for Kyoto and food. It was one strange experience-don't think it wasn't-and as the father says, who is just informed by the doctor of his ninth offspring, "I wouldn't take a million dollars for this one, but I wouldn't give you a dime for another." 

What we done to the menu at the Miyaka Hotel that noon was a crime. My conscience didn't bother me though until I saw our waiter that afternoon with his arm in a sling-but then I figure he's lucky to be alive, the way he had to cart nourishment to our table. 

We just loafed that afternoon and hit the timothy early, because we had a real trip ahead of us for the morrow.

We had already arranged for a guide to accompany us on a hike to Mt. Hiyei and Lake Biwa. We were given to understand that this was a pretty exhaustive hike so we didn't invite the girls along. We took a car over to Yase Park, at the base of the mountain range, and boarded the cable car which takes you almost

WORLD BEATERS 

straight up for almost a mile. These cars are built so that when they are on the level your head is almost in your lap but when it starts up you're sitting pretty.

When the conductor goes through the car I forgets for the moment where we are and begins wondering where it was I broke our Cuban resolution. I'm sure 

I'm drunk when I sees that Jap walking through the car on about a ninety-five degrees angle. I can't believe it when Theo tells me that he's walking straight up and I gets out in the aisle to prove to Theo that he's all wrong and I falls flat on my back like I'd just punched Jack Dempsey's fist with my chin. How I'm hoping Buddha is watching over that cable. It's snow and ice on top of Mt. Hiyei, but I'm out in a perspiration when we reach the summit. I was first out-and 

I'll be behind the last one in if we have to go again.

The tough part of the climbing is over but now we must hike over the range a few miles to the opposite side if we want a view of Lake Biwa, the largest lake in Japan. It's pretty hard hoofing through the snow and we have to slow up our guide who seems used to it.

After an hour on the trail we comes to Enryakuji, a famous old Buddhist monastery, established in 788, and headquarters of the Tendai sect. By the way, folks, don't give me any credit for all this information about sects, monasteries, etc. Remember we have a native guide along. No need to describe this placewith one monastery described they're all described as far as I can see, so we only halt here long enough to fill our radiators. Continuing on through snow and slush for it's loosening up some now in the sun-we punish the dogs some more. If it wasn't for the sights in the forests that kept my mind somewhat off my feet 

I'd of flopped right there and give up the ghost. Theo looks up into the mighty pines that cover the mountains and exclaims "Poems are made by fools like me but only God can make a tree." And as long as I've

WORLD BEATERS 

65 known him I never heard of him writing a single verse. We come to a break in the mountains and our guide points across a bottomless ravine and says, "There's the continuation of our path." And I looked at Theo and says, "And we come away without our wings." And our guide laughs and says, "You won't need them, Mr. Al. We cross right over here." And he leads us into a little waiting room on the edge of the chasm and points to a basket-like affair with three or four seats in it and says, "Take seats, gentlemen," and he steps in and sits down. We're no sooner in our seats than the basket leaves terra firma or vice versa or whatever them old Italians called mother earth and we're swinging through space over that yawning cavern toward the opposite cliff. I looks over the side and can't see nothing but space below us-no tracks, just dangling in the air, and baby, I grips the side of that basket 'til I have to pry my fingers off it when we eventually reach the other side in safety, though I'll never be the same man again. Disembarking,—and I don't even look back, Mr. Lot-we take up our trail again. Our objective ain't so far away, thank Buddha, and as we come upon a clearing in the forest and look down three thousand feet upon the glistening waters of Lake Biwa Theo goes into another of them monthly trances. He surveys the scene in awed silence and then, with one of them majestic sweeps of the arm, like Miss Durant passed over Honolulu from 

Punch Bowl Crater, Theo cuts loose. "Al," he says, "entranced by the mysteries of eons ago, kept secret in the diary of nature's private sanctum, I gaze upon this panorama in all its primitive glory, and long for just a glimpse into its yesterdays." Don't Theo know his 

Webster? He goes on, "Could the aborigine of those forgotten centuries stand with me, here on Mt. Hiyei, would he ask in the tongue of his day, 'What manner of man is this?' Would he see in this mighty canvas

WORLD BEATERS 

before me, draped by the canopy of heaven, a manifestation of the handiwork of God-my God and yours? Did he look into that valley in his day, upon friend or foe? Did the view inspire him to higher walks or did it mean but more lands to conquer? But he has gone. 

Untold millions have come and gone; and here, in a day they perhaps never dreamed of, I stand, on the same cliff, under the same sky, looking down upon the same sparkling water, perhaps wondering no more than they of yesterdays, and no more capable of conceiving its last tomorrow." I'm weak from just listening to him but the guide's mouth is open even wider than mine so I figure there must of been more of it go over his head than I missed so I don't feel as dumb as I likely am. But honest, don't Theo put on a wonderful juggling act with them Funk and Wagnalls'?

I was going to tell you what it was like to look out around the lake, with the mountains on the opposite shore upside down in the water; to see the little villages that rim the banks, busy with their daily life and the small craft skimming along the mirrored surface -but Theo discouraged me from attempting it. I got a lot of nerve to ever open my mouth with him around. Eventually Theo tears himself away from the spell which the surroundings have cast on him and we start back for Kyoto. The return ain't near as exciting which accounts for my present state of good health-and the sun having set and darkness overtaken us on the way, we arrives back at the Miyaka Hotel footsore and weary in body but feeling we've feasted the optics on one of the best sights Japan can possibly offer. 

Again it's a bad night for the groceries and as I orders my third piece of pie I remarks to Theo, "You know, I'm beginning to like this globe-trotting business.' 99

WORLD BEATERS 

67 Fully aware of the fact that Kyoto has much to offer the tourist which we didn't indulge in, we decides that, inasmuch as there are several other towns on this and other maps that are awaiting our arrival, we must leave the rest for another call and so it is that the next morning we endanger the finances of the insurance companies back home by boarding another Japanese train for Yokohama, feeling that if we never see another spot of the country Kyoto has shown us plenty. Having gotten down to the station early we were well up to the fore when the gate gave way and the mêlée started for seats. Having checked my courtesy, valor and chivalry along with the bags I just fought my way to the seats and dragged Theo along with me. I was just getting my wind back to where I was able to speak-I was aching to tell Theo how that was the only way to travel in Japan-when the first line of conductors comes through tearing tickets.

When Theo hands him ours he goes into a Hebrew argument with his hands which tells us nothing. A fellow traveler who hadn't been as lucky as us, we thought -for he was wedged in the aisle-touches me on the shoulder and says, "This car is for Wipa and is being switched at the next stop. He's telling you, you must go into the car up front." I thanks our informer as

Theo reminds me how clever I was in getting a seat. I hope none of them Japs near me understand English,

I mean low-down English, because I forgot for the moment that we had resolved to travel in such a way as to impress the natives of countries we visited with the fact that all Americans were not sots, suckers, and so and so's. But imagine, after crippling my two best feet and having elbows shoved clean through to my appendix, I learn we're in the wrong car. But this was only the beginning of our tough luck. We eventually extricate (I know that's a word for Theo used it) ourselves at the next station and drag the bodies

WORLD BEATERS 

68 down the platform to the next car; but try and get in. 

There's parts of Japs sticking out every window and the chances of getting in, as I says to Theo, are the same as getting into the White House on a four-year lease. "Well," Theo remembers optimistically, "Hoover got in;" and fired by a high resolve to get places, I puts my shoulder to the nearest Jap in the entrance and heaves ahoy. When I comes to a stop-on my left ear-I'm actually inside and Theo is right behind me only right side up. But we're in, that's the point. What if we do have to ride all day in this position. One advantage of such a jam is that each one holds the other up and it's almost as good as a seat. As we slips through the country we gets a good view of the terraced rice fields of Japan. These 

Japs are real gardeners, no matter what they ain't. 

They raise rice like we raise violets-personal and constant attention. If you can find a weed in a Japanese rice field I'll eat it and I mean the weed.

There are countless little lakes-we'd call them swamps in America-all through the lowlands of

Japan and they have kind of irrigation canals from these lakes through the rice fields so they can keep the rice under water most of the time. As I tells Theo, rice should of been an American dish, the stuff is so darn thirsty. Another very common sight is their silk worm ranches. Do you know that these little worms in Japan turn out more silk than their brothers and sisters do in all the other countries of the world combined? And not only that, but the rest of the world admits it's better. So, my fellow countrymen, when you stroll up Fifth Avenue next Sunday morning, sightseeing, thank the little silk-worms of Japan for the eye-soothing sights that bathe your optics.

Every few miles we pass through a town of anywheres from 40,000 to one-half million Japs, but when Theo tells me there's 60,000,000 people in

WORLD BEATERS 

69 Japan and that the whole country could be lost in Texas, I figure the towns ain't any too close together. Imagine half as many people as we got in the United States and they can spare more ground for parks and flower beds than our cities can at home. But then, I suppose there's no sense in planning parks and raising flowers when everybody spends all their spare time at the movies.

At last we're in Yokohama and after massaging the limbs a bit I gets the circulation back and falls out on the platform. They tell us here, that before the earthquake in 1923, Yokohama was a city of about one-half a million and one fine town to be sure. But you'll remember that shimmy-party the earth had back in 1923 almost shook Yokohama off the map. They showed us one corner down town where 2500 Japs was killed.

Boy, that was bad. I thought we had wild parties in 

New York but when mother earth takes one too many and does a Gilda Gray it's another case of "too bad." 

While Yokohama is heroically recovering from that tragedy it still has the appearance of a mushroom town that grew up over night like Gary, Indiana. If you visit Yokohama in a few years from now you'll probably think you got turned around in mid-Pacific and landed back in San Francisco.

About twenty miles around the bay to the north is Tokyo, the capital of Nippon (as we say in good society) and a bustling city of two and one-half millions it is. When you step out of the massive railway station and look across the boulevard into the largest office building east of Suez you got a new appreciation of Japanese industry and progress. As we drives through wide avenues, between the largest office buildings and department stores I ever saw outside of New York, to the Imperial Hotel-which is claimed by world travelers to be the most unique hotel in the world-Theo turns to me and says, "I'm sure Kipling

WORLD BEATERS 

has never been in Tokyo-at least, not before he wrote those lines 'East is East and West is West and 

Ne'er the Twain Shall Meet'." It was obvious from every sight that West had come to the East and that the Imperial Hotel was but one of the many contradictions to Kipling.

At the same time that Yokohama almost checked out, back in 1923, Tokyo suffered nearly as much property damage. In fact the business district was practically eliminated; this Imperial Hotel being about the only structure in the section that absolutely refused to be injured. Would Emily Post consider it ethical if I was to mention just here that it was an 

American that built it? I'll take a chance. It was.

And when they wired him that it had resisted the earthquake he just wired back, "Why not?" Now I could of just told you that story about the builder and not mentioned where he come from-you'd of guessed it. But don't think a little thing like an earthquake keeps a Jap down. Unless it kills him outright it just inspires him to bigger and better things. He just swept up the old bricks (and sold them to Ireland I understand) and built himself a new Tokyo—a real 

Tokyo. A traveler may lament that the introduction of modern methods has destroyed the native Japanese atmosphere-and in the business district, at least, it ain't done nothing else—but they've got the kind of a business district that enables them to borrow millions from America and that's more than Nanking or Leningrad can do. But all of Tokyo don't resemble Broadway. No, no. That touch of "native color" travelers talk about is there, odor and all. A visit to Asakusa 

Park convinces you, in no uncertain terms, that you are in Japan. If modern Tokyo has crowded out the native life she has moved it into Asakusa Park, good and bad alike. Here is Japan at its best and its worst-at least I hope it's the worst. We go through the place

WORLD BEATERS 

71 on Sunday and it's busier than Coney Island on a Jewish holiday. The narrow streets are built solid with little tent-like shops, dealing in everything-food, clothing, pictures, furniture, drink, novelties. There are gambling dens, fortune tellers, beggars or what have you. And right in the midst of all this glamour, filth and indulgence, stands the famous old Kwannon temple. The traveler who told you not to bother seeing Tokyo because it was merely a modern occidental city didn't get out to Asakusa Park. And this is the town where "Hizzoner" the emperor lives. They crown him in Kyoto-not the way they crown them in 

America-and then he moves to Tokyo where he rates a regular palace. This place where the emperor boards is in the middle of a beautiful garden park-at least I'm told it is. It's surrounded by a stone wall, which you can't see over, and that's surrounded by a deep water-filled moat, so you can't climb over it. I guess these gents who rule countries over here have learned from history that the higher the wall and the deeper the moat, the safer the neck. Across from the 

Imperial Hotel is Hibiya Park, forty-five acres of playgrounds and flower beds. The arsenal gardens at 

Koishikawa are considered the finest examples of landscape gardening in all Japan, and it's her gardens that lend the beauty to this Empire of the Rising Sun. 

You'd enjoy a visit to the Kokugi-Kwan, where 15,- 000 can sit in on a wrestling match; and stop at the jujitsu school where the Jap learns the science of selfdefense. If you've any suspicions that a Jap has ever had as much as the first lesson in this jujitsu thing, don't get mad at him, even if he does gyp you for a rickishaw fee. He'll have you heading north over his left shoulder before you know what it's all about. 

It's Thursday evening and the regular weekly dinner dance is scheduled for the Imperial Hotel. The floating university has arrived in town from Kyoto

122 

WORLD BEATERS 

and is being housed over at the Maranouchi Hotel. To say it's a second-rate hotel is to brand yourself a terrible liar, it's a fifth rate. The faculty wanted to take the students to the Imperial but it seems a gang of American students had stayed at the Imperial two years ago and had succeeded in doing what the earthquake had failed to do-to wit-practically ruin the hotel. So when the floating university went there with one hundred American students the management saw them coming and had the doors locked, so they had to take the best they could get-hence, the Maranouchi.

We figures the girls would appreciate getting inside a real hotel so we invite them to dinner and the dancebeing careful to remind them that they mustn't act like students or we'll all get throwed out. Of course, we're used to Imperial food and take it unconcerned but the Playfair sisters, after two days at the Maranouchi, forgets they hail from Boston and attack their nourishment like a couple hyenas. After dinner the nine-piece orchestra turns from dinner music to the latest Broadway jazz and we takes to the dance floor like a dime takes to Woolworth's. Paul Whiteman couldn't teach them boys a thing and on that floor, that glittered like Chicago's Trianone, with girls to surround like K and B, can you tell me what's wrong with that picture? If I could get an eternal lease on a life like that there'd be a mansion for rent in heaven. 

When we return to our table and the boy comes round with the drink list I'm pleased to hear the girls order ginger ale and Theo just tells him to make it four. 

After the dance K and me leave the others and finds a cosy corner chair in front of an open fireplace in one of the private reception rooms. There's the dying embers of a log fire twitching nervously in the grate. 

Except for the ray of light that streams in from the reading room we're in darkness and when an ember almost flared into flames, forming a golden back-

WORLD BEATERS 

73 133 ground against K's profile, I gets a bad case of palpitation of the heart. K has let her head rest on my shoulder, my arm has found its way round her waist and I'm holding her hand with the other mitt. I'm glad we was alone because I was in that particular state when, to the rest of the world, a fellow looks a proper sap. You know how it is-or do you-when you feel urged to do baby-talk? I tries to fight it offbut no use, I weaken-we both weaken-and I tells her a lot of them simple things that mean so much,at the time. She looks me straight in the eye and says, "Alfred, are you sure you really love me?" And the last flare of the fire shows me a liquid glitter in the corner of her dreamy eyes. I let myself unconsciously slip down on one knee and have the proper setting for such an eloquent reply as I am about to broadcast when B and Theo comes rollicking in. 

And you thought Napoleon had a tough break at Waterloo. 

B opens up with, "Why, K, it's 1:30 and we have to check in by 11 o'clock." Then Theo chastised me by informing me that I have probably been responsible for having the girls denied any further privileges. I'm sick myself, positively sick. My one big moment arrived and so did Theo and B. So the affair is left hanging right there and I couldn't feel worse if I was hanging too. Just think, we're leaving Tokyo in the morning and I don't know when we'll meet up with the Aqua College again. We take the girls back to the Maranouchi and bid them plenteous good-byes and depart-not knowing but what they'll get expelled all through me and then it didn't get me any place. On our way back to the Imperial, Theo says, "Aren't you getting a little too thick with K, Al?" "Why do you ask, Theo?" I wants to know. "Was you and B eavesdropping on that line of molasses we was handing each other?" And, laughing, we drops it there.

The next morning we're up bright and early-at

WORLD BEATERS 

least, Theo is-I'm only up early-I'm still too sad about last night's affair to be bright-and we takes our belongings to the station and buys a couple chances to Nikko. This Nikko place is way up in the mountains in northern Japan, where snow and ice abound. You 

You know, snowbound-icebound-well that's Nikko. They claim it's the cats and that nobody should leave Japan without seeing the temples of Nikko. For once we actually gets into a regular car, without any bloodshed and walks, unmolested, to a vacant seat. There was lots of seats in them other cars we rode in only they wasn't vacant. After we're a couple hours out of Tokyo we notice all the Japs looking out the far windows with upturned faces. We walks across the car and takes a "look-see" ourselves and what is it we're passing but the sacred mountain of Nippon-Fujiyami herself—the highest peak in all Japan. Now, I've seen about every sight worth seeing in the Rockies-and there's plenty-but this Fujiyami is the most colossal and inspiring thing I ever saw in the form of mere piled up dirt. It looks exactly like the 3,000,000 pictures I've seen of it—which in itself would be enough to impress anybody. It is so far above any other peak near it that the whole mountain seems to be just sprung off the level; and it's even lines make it look so artificial, and then that snow-capped peak, with the fine, vein-like streams of snow running down the peak and fading away into the green of the forests that cover the whole. Do you know what I thought of when I first lamped it? Not that it gives you any idea of what it looks like but I thought of a crowned canine tooth.

A few hours more and we're at the end of the railroad, which fortunately is Nikko. The train has been on such an ascent for the last few miles that we almost had to line ourselves up with trees to see if we were moving. I expected a call from the engineer at any mo-

WORLD BEATERS 

75 ment to get out and push. I thought I had solved the thing that puzzled me so-why the absence of the usual jam? But we're here at last and an old Studebaker from the Kanaya Hotel is there to get us. The town is almost snowbound and from the way this bird tears through the one-way rut that seems to be handling all the town's traffic, I figures that Nikko should be a great place to open up a line of chain-store undertaking parlors. I didn't know whether he was trying to get us through town before we noticed it had snowed-like the first night I spent in Florida. It was only three degrees above zero and the old boy that ran the hotel in the little town that darkness had overtaken me in broke up a couple chairs and tore the back out of one of the beds to make a fire, saying, as he built it, that no matter how nice the weather was in Florida he always liked to see a wood fire; and all the time his knees were knocking like a second-hand 

Chevrolet. Or, maybe the manager of the Kanaya

Hotel was holding off the sheriff 'til we got there to be fleeced. But why should I boil over when Theo is looking after that department?

We register, wash up, take lunch, observe the mountain scenery from the hotel windows while we rest up a bit and then, in tow of a guide, we starts out to see what Nikko is famous for. We don't leave the hotel grounds 'til our guide says "There's the Daiya River, one of the most beautiful mountain streams in the world. And that bridge up there is only used at certain religious ceremonies and then it is only crossed by members of the royal family and high officials of the temple. Now we will go up into the mountains and see the temples of Nikko, the most beautiful temples in the world." When the guide gets far enough ahead so's he can't tune in I says to Theo, "I'll bet this Jap would claim that old Studebaker is the best car in the world." But, kidding aside I couldn't prove he was

76 off yet. I don't recall any mountain stream that offers the eye any more than the Daiya River, except that I think that the name of "river" is a bit far-fetched I'd call it more of a leak. But it must of been on a rampage once for it's cut itself some gorge through them mountains; and as far as red-lacquered sacred bridges go, I never even knew there was one, so it's O.K. by me to let both stream and bridge pass as the berries. But now he's heading us into what he claims are the finest temples in the world. We heads into another of them cryptomeria groves the grandest in all Japan and, friends, that grove don't need any building to make it the finest temple in the world, as far as I'm concerned. If the sun could of got through them branches I'd of sure had a sweet case of sunburn on the roof of my mouth.

Our guide goes on to tell about how these sixty-three temples was built three hundred years ago as a kind of memorial, as well as mausoleum, for Emperor Ieyasu and his grandson Iemitsu. He points out the fact that every building is finished in red or black lacquer-as if you could miss seeing it. But he's the kind of a guide that if he was down on the boardwalk at Atlantic City he'd point out the ocean and tell you the water in this ocean comes closer to the shore than any other ocean in the world. He was a hound for detail. But describing the temples separately is like describing each shingle when you want to tell about a roof. Not that they're so much alike but they each are such an inseparable part of the whole that you go away feeling that it was just one great temple there under the cryptomerias. Standing before the gilded shrine of the main temple, Theo says, with venerable eloquence, "What noble conception, what super-human handiwork, what artistic grandeur-oh, what a monument to the religious faith of a Nation are these divine Temples of Nikko." Don't that boy clean up a situation

WORLD BEATERS 

77 though when he says things? I was going to rave on and on into the night about those temples but Theo said everything, so let's be up and going. We takes in some other places like waterfalls and lakes but if anything ever happens to the temples of Nikko there'll be a good hotel up there for sale, cheap, and the government would probably consider some stock in Dr.

Cook's oil company for that part of their railway that runs to Nikko.

Feeling that we have done right by Japan we buy, I mean Theo buys, a couple punch boards from the ticket agent and we dangle back to Tokyo. And it's not bad going back. Most anything will run downhill. We reach the capital about 10 P.M. and we plan to go right through to Kobe where a boat will be pleased to bear us on our way. It's about a ten-hour run, with luck, and I'm afraid I'll be a cadaver before ten hours have passed, because this is the main line where the masses move hither and yon, I'll be withered and wan, at least. But Theo steps up to the window and orders two sleepers. I feel like somebody just lifted the rock of Gibraltar off my neck. Our train pulls in and we step to one side of the mad mob and go, like gentlemen, to our Pullman. "Watkins, my one-eyed monicle."

Having tucked the body away I lay there for some time waiting for the train to start so I can go to sleep, I don't want to clutter up the aisle with my person by being heaved from the meadow when the engine jerks ahead. After thirty or forty minutes I sits up in my berth and reads a pamphlet on "Canned Crab❞ somebody has left in the hammock. After learning that 

Japan ships practically all the canned crab that's exported from the country and getting an insight into the domestic life of the crab-and it was while reading that chapter that I got to figuring what a big business could be worked up if somebody would only can

WORLD BEATERS 

the domestic crabs in America-and what a blessing it would be-but then, I realized there'd be no market for them-when the conductor comes through calling, "Yokohama,—all out for Yokohama." And here I'd sat up all that distance waiting for the train to leave 

Tokyo. If you think The Twentieth Century rides smooth, don't ride a Pullman in Japan or you'll probably lose your false teeth the next time you take the flier to New York. We're up early the next morning because we're due about 8:30 in Kobe and when we pass through Asaka, Theo informs me that it's the leading manufacturing city in Japan and I says, "Well, if you can give me any sensible reason why a guy from the land of Ford and Standard Oil should get off to see a manufacturing city, I'll get off and walk into 

Kobe." He can't, so I don't. But it did look like quite the town from the station.

Not long and we're again in Kobe and I keeps a sharp lookout for that Jesse James rickishaw boy.

Our boat is in, loaded, and about to sail in three hours; so we rush our bags from the station to the boat, gets our cabin assignment and eat. Then there's just an hour and a half to go down on the dock and buy some souvenirs of Japan. Of course, people who have folks back home buy real things in the proper places-right where they're made-but we're on a sightseeing tour and not a shopping expedition.

There's about every kind of trinket you could imagine spread out on the dock, also silk goods, lacquer and porcelain ware, jewelry and whatnot. I dickers with a kindly old Jap for a cigarette case with Mt. Fujiyami (of course) on the back of it in gold. He talks a little English but he talks that little a lot and he at last comes down from 9 yen ($4.50) to 5, leaving me think I'm taking the rice right off his chopsticks, so I buys two, figuring I'm getting them really for the price of one. I gives one of them to Theo. I spends

WORLD BEATERS 

79 a little more time among these high-powered salesmen and then the fifteen minute whistle blows and it blows the bottom right out from under all prices. On my way back to the gangplank I pass the old boy I had beat out of four yen on the cigarette case and he says, “Oh, American, I give you more cigarette case for two yen." And that was my last experience in Japan. 

As our steamer moves gracefully away from its berth and sails majestically out to sea I have a mingled assortment of ideas about this "gem of the Orient." Sailing down around the southern part of Japan we heads due west for China. The scene is pretty much like our arrival in Kobe, since we are almost constantly in sight of small islands, each with their own little fishermen's village, and the sea is dotted with those small fishing boats with the peculiar squareshaped sails. The second day out we've lost all sight of land but it's only for a few hours as we're due in 

Shanghai sometime during the night. The next morning when the breakfast gong awakens us I sits up in bed and sticks an eye out the porthole and we're right in the middle of all the old derelicts in the world. 

Honest, I thought I was in Davy Jones' locker. I never even imagined, let alone seen, so many wrecks afloat in my wildest nightmare. Theo wants to know what my first impression of China is. I'm still looking out of the porthole and I notices that every boat is inhabited. I says, "Theo, my first look at China clears up the only thing I ever wondered about Chinks, namely, why they all went into the laundry business. 

But why shouldn't they, Theo, when they all live on the water?" But when we dress and go out on deck for one of them panoramic views of the situation we sees we're at anchor in the middle of a fair-sized river, a few miles above Shanghai, and these boats, we learn, are the junks that handle most of the river freight and they're just laid up for the night. There's not only

WORLD BEATERS 

dozens of them, there's hundreds of them and if I'd looked out our porthole during the night and seen them boats I'd of figured I had the D.T.'s. There's a pair of big eyes on the front of each boat-one on each side of the bow, which looks like a witch's beakand brother, if they don't remind you of the night you whistled through the cemetery, then you ain't never been through a cemetery at night. I asks a Chink,my mistake—I asks a Chinaman-Theo told me never to refer to the native of a country in any way except the proper name; he says, "Just as we would resent the Chinese calling us 'Yanks,' so do they resent our reference to them as 'Chinks'." So I asks this Chink -I mean Chinese-the idea for all the optician ads. on the boats and he tells us them eyes are painted on the front of the boat so's they can see any evil spirit that might be hanging 'round the river to do it harm and thereby be able to miss it. Some of the boats, he points out, have very ugly faces painted or carved at the bow to scare away all these evil spirits. That's a new idea on me. Just another part of the religions of the Orient. As I said before-they may be all right- 

I know if my spirit was hovering round these parts it would sure take the air if it got a look at one of them faces on the boats-yes, they may be right, and I'm sure if we had faces like them painted on the front of our automobiles in America there'd be fewer accidents, but just as many fatalities I suppose, because you're just as dead after they scare you to death as you are after they jack up a Mack truck off your Adam's apple. But it's China we come to see not just the 

Whangpoo River, so we has breakfast, packs our bags and takes the tender to Shanghai. We takes a taxi to the Astor House Hotel-we choose that one because it smacks of home and when we're washed up we strolls through the hotel and it has been some hotel, once, too-if a troupe of interior decorators from

WORLD BEATERS 

81 home could be given their way with this Astor House for about a week it would be a real hotel again. It's large and well laid out but it needs a new dress. Imagine, our room is finished in blood red. When the boy opened the door and I got a look inside I thought we must be intruding on a session of the Soviet government. The rug is red, the drapes are red, the wallpaper is red, and dirty, the plush on the chairs is red. 

The whole thing looks like a four-alarm fire and the worst of it is, the money that bought these screaming decorations would of been ample to fix it swell. But I don't have stock in this hotel any more so it's no gold out of my teeth. 

It's only 10:30 in the A.M. so we steps out to see 

Shanghai and form some ideas about China. Leaving the Astor House by the front door, which is opened for us by a handsome Sikh, we strolls toward the Bund -that's the main street-I notices the cops on all the corners are also Sikhs. Fine looking, big, strapping chaps, hiding behind a heavy growth of brunette whiskers which covers everything but their eyes. But even through this underbrush you can tell they're good looking. It seems England polices Shanghai and she does it with the natives of India. And is a Chink-I mean a Chinaman-afraid of a Sikh? One bellow out of a Sikh and every Chinaman in that block jumps right out of his bare feet. They tell stories of how a single Sikh has gone into a den of fighting Chinks and come out with enough pigtails to make a skirt for a

Hulu dancer-and do that without so much as getting a hair of his whiskers ruffled. And still, if everything is going as it should, these Indians are the most pleasant fellows you'd ever hope to see. I guess England knows how to "cop" a city, eh? And why shouldn't she afford protection? Everything on the Bund that's worth a dime belongs to England, America, Germany, France or some other outside nation. Several of the

22 

WORLD BEATERS 

finest banks in the East are right here on the Bund, and office buildings that would be a credit to any city in the world. There's blocks and blocks of the most modern buildings you can find any place and don't think, either, that the safety of these investments is left entirely to the Sikhs. This Bund, you know, runs along the river and right out there, about two hundred yards away, are always five or six great gunboats from the different nations of the world and their guns are trained on the Bund every second. I confess this ain't what you like to see one country have to do to protect her interests in another country, but when a nation's already at war and all messed up with internal strife, I'm telling you it's a mighty comforting thing to see them old Stars and Stripes waving in the breeze and that old Union Jack up there.

Of course, if this is any of my business, then I should rush right to the Astor House and personally supervise the work of interior decorations. But, you see, we happen to be in China just when she's having her annual war for the reduction of overhead in the form of cutting down the voters' list. It seems the country long ago reached its peak in the production of rice and is only capable of feeding so many-440,- 934,236 to be exact-and, through natural methods, it appears that a few millions are added to this number each year, in excess of those who go to Confucius -and so the country throws these little elimination contests each year, which, helped by starvation, succeed in putting out of business the proper number of chopsticks to let the Minister of Internal Affairs-or is it the Doctor-balance his books. 

It's time for lunch so we traces our steps back to the Astor House, feeling that from what we've already learned about China, it's in one terrible mess.

Even discounting my knowledge of national affairswhich would be rather a clever act in itself; even Ein-

WORLD BEATERS 

883 stein would have difficulty in taking nothing away from zero-but as I was saying, even one as dumb as me can see that China ain't at her best.

After lunch we takes rickishaws-I mean they takes us-and that word "take" is well chosen, I've given up trying to escape being "taken" by a rickishaw boy or a taxi driver-they'll skin you if they have to do it dishonestly-so we have a couple of these chopstick musicians trot us round Shanghai to see if we can find something in the town that don't remind us of Broadway-this Bund affair makes you feel you got a sticking when you left America to see something different. 

But them yellow babies ain't long convincing us that while the Bund looks occidental there's plenty of real China just around the corner. They take us through dirty, narrow streets that just ooze filth and poverty. 

Any kind of imagination can supply the proper odor for that setting. You can't believe that just within a stone's throw of them great banking houses on the Bund is a section of China at her worst. It's interesting, you understand, but as Theo observes, "What depressing pathos."

At night we puts the bodies into tux's and go out to see where they got any reason for calling this burg "the Paris of the East." Not that I've ever been in

Paris but Theo has and he'll know. The Chink at the desk, I mean the slit-eye, or no-no, the, the,—the clerk, one that was born over there, he tells us the names of the leading night clubs and we starts out to take them in, but they'll probably turn the tables on us. The first one we go to ain't a night club at all but the Majestic Hotel. It's considered the "dunky-dora" of all swell places in Shanghai. And ain't it, though?

It was built for the home of Lord Helpus or Count de Chink or somebody who toted an appendix to his monicker and then the bottom fell out of his correspondence from home or something happened to his income

WORLD BEATERS 

and his little love nest went under the hammer and he didn't save as much as a fixture out of one of the eight bathrooms. The Shanghai-Hong Kong Hotel Com pany bought it and opened it just about as it was, as the Majestic Hotel, and boy, what one grand place it is. As we stroll through this Ritz I feel as important as the budget for enforcing prohibition in America. 

We looks the place over, unable to suggest any improvements, and then looks up the dining room and dance hall. We hears it before we finds it. They've got the hottest orchestra out and it sure is tearing up a snappy fox trot and my toes starts twitching. We takes a table and orders ginger ale, much to the disgust of the rice-burner, and, I know you won't believe we're so lucky, but across the floor-if my eyes don't fool me is the Durant and London dames with the old uncle and the scholar. I can hardly control myself but Theo suggests he go over to their table, which he does, and kind of break the ice with the right words so the two fossils won't get to thinking. When all is clear Theo motions me and I puts my best foot forward and says how charmed I am. If the old gang on the Keith circuit could of heard me say "charmed" they'd of crowned me, and I wouldn't of thought I was in Kyoto, either. We dances that dance and the next, and the next, etc., etc., and also etc. It just seems that when Buddha takes his eye off us Confucius starts bringing us luck. After the dance the old boys. consent to Theo's request to allow the girls to take in a night club with us. I'll never believe different than that the old uncle took to us when everybody ordered hard drinks but me and Theo. He says, "Don't you boys drink?" And before I can tell him the truth, Theo says, "Only this way." So at 12:00 midnight the uncle and teacher goes to bed-they're staying at the Majestic-and the four of us goes out looking for the Black Cat, the Little Club and what have they.

WORLD BEATERS 

85 We finds all of them and do a few numbers at eachjust real high-class night clubs-and at 5 A.M. Theo and me is dragging ourselves into that murderers' den in the Astor House where our bed is. Boy, am I glad we ain't been drinking. When this room looks so bad when I'm sober, what would it look like if I was lit? 

Providing the uncle is willing we have dinner dates for the next day, rather, for this evening. So we tumbles in and gets a good morning's sleep. After lunch we dashes out about a mile and a half and lamps the seven-story Lunghwa pagoda, takes in the meteorological observatories at Siccawei, which, as the guide at Nikko would take great pleasure in informing us, is one of the most complete observatories in the world; only he'd of probably left out "one of." On our way back to the hotel-our driver probably went out of his way to show us the University of Shanghai and, though we didn't order it, it is worth a look, if for nothing else than the satisfaction of knowing that there's at least a place in China where a Chink can learn something in case he wants to. It's time to dress now and call for the girls. We takes dinner right there at the Majestic and pretty much repeat the performance of the night before, except I gets in a little deeper with the Durant baby. She may not know it, nor care, but I'm getting nuts about her-and it ain't because she's the only child of a widowed mother worth $3,000,000 either. There's a thousand things about her 

I like yes, there's 3,000,000 things about her I like. 

But I shouldn't be going on this way about her when we're leaving them tonight, perhaps forever. But we tells them we hope to see them again some place and when we left them-I guess I acted kind of foolish-Miss 

Durant kissed me goodbye and said what a dear I am. 

Ain't love grand-or whatever you call that thing that makes a girl like her kiss a sap like me.

The next morning, feeling we'd seen enough of

WORLD BEATERS 

Shanghai, at least for the moment, we decided to get us an eyeful of China herself. We has a conference with the American consul and he tells us, in his diplomatic way, what means in English that if we don't care a damn what happens to us we can go right ahead and see China, but, he points out, since China is in such a state of warfare we must travel hither and yon at our own risk-and I laughs to myself because he /don't know we're insured.

We decided to go to Pekin but he tells us the railroad has been seized by rebels. Theo suggests a steamer up the Yang-tze-Kiang and into the heart of 

China that way and the consul says, "Well, you may get through a trip like that alive since the river boats have enclosed their promenade decks with sheets of steel and carry a troop of British marines aboard." "What's the idea," I gasps. "Oh, just the usual piracy along the river." "Oh," I says, "just a little piracy, eh? I thought it was something serious." And turning to Theo, with all jokes aside, I says, "Listen,

Theo, let's buy some picture books of China and catch that steamer we come in on before she leaves us in this den of scalpers." "No," Theo says, firmly, "we have come to see China and we're going to see it, both of us." So Theo buys tickets for a river boat that'll take us a thousand miles up the Yang-tze into the bowels of China. May Buddha and Confucius go with us all the way. 

Brother, I ain't foolin' you any when I tells you that after that ride up the Yang-tze I could go to sleep on a scenic railway or yawn myself into a stupor doing a parachute drop. I wouldn't of bet a busted chopstick against Germany's national debt that we'd of ever got back to civilization alive. We leave Shanghai in the morning and about twelve miles down the Whangpoo we turns into this Yang-tze-Kiang thing and it's a river-I mean a real river. Where we hits it it's about

WORLD BEATERS 

87 sixty miles wide but as we travels on it narrows up some and soon we're where it averages about a mile across. It's a dirty, muddy river but it's about 3500 miles long the captain tells us, so I'm not surprised that it's dirty by the time it's traveled that distance through a country like China. What I'm dumfounded at is that it ain't too thick to work a propeller in. 

As the U.S. consul had told us, the decks are all enclosed with sheets of one-inch steel, about six feet high, and padding the decks is a gang of British marines, with bayonets fixed. I hate to think of them having to be on board but them bayonets are mighty comforting things to think about when you're trying to go to sleep. We pass little thatched-roofed communities and the odd temple through the first day and the next day we're due in Nanking, the present capital of China. At least, it's the capital today; I don't know where the capital might be tomorrow. Theo suggests we go on to Hankow on this boat and take in all these towns on our way back-if we get back. I guess 

Theo figures since nothing has happened to us so far maybe this boat is immune to evil spirits and so we should stay on. He don't have any trouble convincing me that we shouldn't get out of sight of them bayonets on board, so when the boat leaves Nanking, so do we. 

Outside of a missionary and his wife going to Hankow we're the only passengers aboard, except the two or three hundred Chinks in the hold, riding steerage. The boat makes its money these days out of the freight it carries. Well, everything's going fine and the four of us are quite sociable. We learn from our missionary friends who had been in the middle of China for many years that they're just returning to their base after barely escaping with their lives in the 1927

March disturbance. They're in the midst of telling us how a foreigner's life wasn't worth a dime in China at that time and the only place he was safe was in the

WORLD BEATERS 

international settlement in Shanghai-you know, back where them Sikhs do their stuff and them cute little gunboats smile on you-they're just telling us all this when something that sounds exactly like pandemonium breaks out on deck. I rushes to the nearest exit and takes a look-folks, I was right. It is pandemonium himself. A band of pirates had boarded at Nanking, under the guise of steerage passengers, and now, as soon as we're out in the river away from all towns, them slit-eyed scalpers has come out of the hold and they're out there on deck doing a hand to hand murder with them British marines. The missionary's wife has fainted so we don't have to listen to the screams of any women and children. But don't think I wouldn't of screamed if I'd been able. The three of us, Theo and the missionary and me, have our heads stuck out the door wondering how long it'll be before they come for our wigs. I'm telling you they won't have any trouble collecting mine because every hair is standing up like the quills on a frightened porcupine. 

The Chinks have long guns but the marines have rushed them so close they can't use them and they got them wicked looking knives out and are slashing at the British like Simon Legree at Uncle Tom. I never see such game babies as them British in all my life. 

There's ten Chinks for every Britisher, but that ain't enough. I'll leave out the gruesome details of that little battle, but after the Yang-tze had been dirtied up with the carcasses of fifteen or twenty laundrymen and a couple dozen more was put in chains, them Britishers wiped off their bayonets and started pacing the deck again like nothing had happened. Folks, I used to think the beheading block in China was a bad thing to get mixed up with but it's a pleasant experience compared to getting mixed up with a bunch of British marines. Theo's going to have trouble getting me away from them boys until I gets back to Shanghai.

89 After the free-for-all the missionary's wife comes to and asks what happened but we just tell her it was a false alarm and the four of us go for a little walk round the deck. We're just passing the marines when we hears the sergeant telling four of his men to keep their guns trained on the stairway from the hold and if a cheek-bone appears, shoot, and shoot to kill. The missionary's wife throws up her hands in what is known as "righteous indignation" and asks the sergeant what he means by ordering his men to shoot those poor unlightened souls. She tells him how she and her husband has spent fifteen years in the heart of China trying to impart the word of God to these unfortunate people and she thinks he should be ashamed to so misuse his authority as to order his men to kill these oppressed people. When she's run down, this sergeant uses the best words he's got and says, "Madam, so you've been trying to talk the hell out of these Chinks for fifteen years, eh? And you ain't had much luck, have you? Well, now you stand right there and if one of them slit-eyes pops his pigtail above that floor we'll show you how to do, in a fraction of a second, what you ain't been able to do in fifteen years. 

We'll blow the hell out of them." She looks plenty disgusted and says something about the Chinese not being the only people who need to be prayed for. I'd hate to be as tough as that sergeant myself, but while 

I'm a passenger on this boat I'm sure glad he ain't chicken hearted.

We travel for days through this famous Yang-tze River valley, that's the most fertile part of China and produces most of its agricultural produce. Once or twice a day we puts in at a town and exchanges freight and drops a couple dozen Chinks from the hold and adds a few more to murderer's row-only the marines search all the new ones getting on after that. When there's no war on the deck sailing up the Yang-tze is

WORLD BEATERS 

about as exciting as an old maid-but don't think I'm kicking about the quiet of the boat, I'm all caught up on my thrills if I live to see the Democrats win a national election—but I'm just remarking that it is quiet. 

You'd think in a country where there's over four hundred millions the towns would be as close together along the river as they are along the railroad in Arkansas. So close that when the boat was leaving one town they'd have to back up to whistle for the next; but the facts are they're hours apart. In between there's just the level farm land as far as you can see with its rice, wheat, maize, millet, sugar-cane, beans and all the crops peculiar to China. Perhaps for a few miles we cut in through a gorge in a mountain rangethat just seems to spring up out of a rice field-and our missionary friend tells us that all these mountains have rich deposits of all kinds of ore and that it's never been exploited. He tells us of the great deposits of coal throughout China, especially in Sansi province, but for superstitious reasons the Chinese don't use it. 

Only a few hours more and we're in Hankow, where the river is pretty well crowded with Japanese battleships. It appears a Japanese official accidentally killed a Chinese coolie with his automobile and China and Japan are practically at war over that. Imagine, in a country where one of their big problems is figuring out new ways of reducing the population, they just about go to war with another nation for helping them out. I suppose they figure if that coolie had to be killed China was entitled to the privilege, not

Japan. Anyway, when we gets ashore-and maybe you think I ain't plenty sad at leaving them marineswe finds Hankow in one sorry mess. Japan has barbed. wire entanglements around her concessions with Japanese soldiers behind sand-bag battlements every few yards. It don't look good. All the foreign countriesexcept Japan-are seriously considering giving in to

91 China's request that they give up their concessions. All the foreigners are nervous about what might happen and they ain't got nothing on me. The town is somewhat like Shanghai; the main street, or Bund, is taken up with foreign legation buildings and banks, and behind this district is the typical Chinese city. We stayed at a mission house in Hankow and we meets a couple of young Englishmen there-and since that battle on the boat I'm strong for Englishmen and these chaps take us out to their country club. And up there, in the heart of China, where you'd no more expect to find a golf-ball than Senator Borah, they've got a golf and polo club that would grace any part of Long Island. 

The foreigners built it and the club house must of been copied off the hotel in Coral Gables. These boys shows us through the factories they work for, and I wish you could of seen the thousands of eggs that comes in there and is packed to be shipped abroad. 

You've likely had some of them on your ham before this. And away up there they had a show window with a Ford and a Chevrolet and some European makes on exhibition. It looks like home to see a Ford up there. 

But I'm personally in favor of getting back where them Fords is made. Aside from what we've already seen, Hankow has nothing much to offer a tourist, except the fact that if China ever settles down and gets places this Hankow place is apt to become the largest and most important city in China. It has 800,000 rice bowls already in use and that's a fair start on a real city. You see, ocean-going vessels can go all the way to Hankow and it's the centre of the only railroad system in the country and in the heart of the most productive part of China; so, if this station ever gets to the top, just remember I told you so.

environment. At least, that's my first impression as we approach, but as we gets into the squalor of the town, it's not so picturesque-not so. I'm only wondering two things-How they live and Why they want to. The only building in some of these towns, that could be called a building, is the temple and most of them is in about the same shape as my mountain lodge. I turns as usual to Theo for an answer to it all and he says, "Al, I wouldn't be too hasty to form an opinion of, or to pass judgment on, these people. It's true we know a little about their past, but there's undoubtedly much we don't know. We can't judge them by our standards because the opportunities that afforded us our standard was never offered them-as far as we know. Potentates and false gods have ever oppressed them, but the few that have risen from the ranks, from time to time, like Confucius, has been sufficient proof that they are a nation as capable as any on earth, if only they could be freed from the fetters of tradition and loosed from the bondage of unscrupulous leaders. Poverty is not necessarily evidence of incompetency. If Edison had been denied the means wherewith to work out his suppositions we'd have probably been using kerosene lamps in America today.

And so, since these people have been kept in such abject circumstances, who can say how many Edisonian ideas have died a prenatal death? No, Al," he finishes, "it is only for us to wonder, not to judge." "I hadn't thought of that," I says. As if I could.

After enough of this we heads back to Hankow and boards another vessel that's heading outwards to sea.

When we arrived in Kukiang thirty-two years ago the Chinese treated us like we were some kind of gods. We were very safe then and we made good progress, at first. But today, when we retire at night, we not only close the door but we bar it. We never dare to go out at night, and during the disturbance in March, 1927, we'd have all been killed only for the British gunboat that came up the river and rescued us. These Ming tombs are approached by a driveway that's lined with great stone images of camels, elephants, lions and what have we.

These tombs are about as attractive as a week's product from a brick kiln would be if put up in a square pile; and to make it even less so, the interior decorator from the Astor House in Shanghai must of done the paint job because the pile of brick is painted red-can you feature that? A Red Tomb. Help!

I suppose the wall around Nanking is worth speaking of, since it's the longest and greatest wall enclosing a city in the world today. It's twenty-two miles long and sixty feet high in some places, and never less than forty, and it varies in width from twenty to forty feet. I'd call that a wall, wouldn't you? I ain't much good at figures but I suppose there's enough brick and stone in that wall to replace every mud hut in Wuhu with a brick house. 886 ture is shamefully disgusting." And then I thinks so too.

Her eyes are like new moons and as black as night. 

The cheek bones ain't too high and the mouth curves sympathetically with the eyes. The lips are a fever red and through them beams a perfect hedge of pearl.

Her skin is a natural suntan and draped over the whole is a head of glistening black hair, brought back over one ear and knotted low on the side. Her figure is slender and graceful and her gown drapes her form like a window model. I don't know if I'm fickle or not but for the third time since leaving New York, I have a sinking sensation behind the lungs. As she sits down, her long slim fingers, immaculately manicured, drops on the table. Theo is stunned. The closest I ever come to seeing anything like that was in Benda's pictures. "Tell me," Theo breaks the silence in an attempt to break the spell she seems to hold over him, "do you speak my language?" "Yes," she assures him, "I was educated at the university here," and, sighing, she goes on, "but I haven't made much of my opportunities. I had intended going into the heart of China as a missionary and school teacher, but during my senior year I met a young American who swept me off my feet; we loved each other passionately-at least, I loved him that way-and he said he loved me that way too—I didn't know what he meant then, I do now -and he brought me here nearly every night and I

WORLD BEATERS 

99 met others here and thought I was having a wonderful time-I neglected my studies, and when the students marched up the aisle for their diplomas on graduation night, I wasn't among them; I was over here, laughing at the world-then a boat left for America and my 'Richard' went with it-just like that-without even saying 'Goodbye'-I've never heard from him since and I've never been able to get out of the atmosphere he accustomed me to." Theo's lips curve viciously and he mutters something to himself like, "The cad," and then addressing the dame he asks, “Is it too late yet to break away?" And he places his big hand over hers. That boy is slipping. "Don't get me wrong, Miss China," Theo tells her, "I'm no relation of the boy who wronged you-if I were I'd get caught up on my family-tree pruning-but from what I've seen of China in the few weeks we've been here, it needs some of its own people, someone it will trust, with ideals like you had when you entered the university, to save it. Its soul is dead. I hope you'll forgive me for making that statement, but-""No," she interrupts, "you're only too observing-but your lack of knowledge of our country's past leaves you unfit to see the awful truth. No, a thing that does not exist, cannot die. China never had a soul." "But she can't go on like this," reasons Theo, taking the whole problem of China's salvation on his back. "She must be given a soul like America." "You mean, she must accept 

America's God?" questions this flower of the Orient. "Well, yes," admits Theo, "if you choose to put it that way-she must receive America's God." "Never," cries China's gift to the night club industry, and her eyes narrows into mere slits and she half arises in her chair. "Accept their God," she shrieks, waving a hand that takes in all the foreigners in the place. "They who wallow in drink, who misuse our women-yes, and your women too-who beat coolies with their

WORLD BEATERS 

canes when they demand their rickishaw fees-you mean their God must be our God? Never. Better my country never has a soul than one like theirs, much better." "Please," Theo pleads, "let's not get mad about this thing. Surely we can reason tolerantly and still differ in opinions. I agree with you these people are poor examples of what Christianity intended to make of them, but no teaching has ever succeeded in reforming a hundred per cent of its numbers; many beautiful characters have been shaped in its mould and China would do well to emulate the good type of American." "But what China needs," it's her opinion, "is not someone to preach about a new god. China's trouble has been her gods-her countless gods. No, she doesn't need anybody's god-at least, not now she doesn't. She's had so many and none of them have shown her the light, that her people are now convinced that their present state is the best any god can do. 

What China needs is for someone to come into her midst and live a new life. Not preach a new philosophy but live a new philosophy. One who will be pro gressive but not worship the profits of progress. One who will be a leader among men and yet not forgetting that those he leads are also human like himself. 

Someone who will lead a proper life and, while doing so, be tolerant of those beneath him-lend a lifting hand-pass on a kind word-smile on the downtrodden. That's what China needs; an example in her midst, not telling her but showing her. That type of man can save China and only that type. Take back your missionaries and send us men who will live like that."

When she stops, nobody speaks. I know better than to try and Theo for once is in almost as bad shape. If he knows anything to say he's scared to say it. And, imagine, Theo asked her if she could speak his language. Boy, I'm glad I didn't ask her that. She speaks my language like I wish I could.
