About 5:30 one morning we cuts away from the main sea and slides in between two rocky cliffs into the great harbor of Hong Kong. 

A group of passengers, including me and Theo, has succeeded in getting out of bed at such an unearthly hour and lines the bridge in our pajamas to witness the approach. As Theo says, "It really is one of the most impressionable sights in the Orient, entering Hong 

Kong harbor just as the blackness of night is turning into the gray of dawn, and the mountain islands, on either side, are silhouetted against the sky and, here and there, the faint glimmer of a lamp in a fisherman's houseboat off shore." 

Docking shortly after sunrise we passes inspection and are ready to see Hong Kong. From where we ties up, at Kowloon, we gets a real view of Hong Kong across the little bay as it nestles at the foot of a great mountain that seems to be the whole island. It strikes me funny that this little rocky island—with absolutely no value-should be one of the most thriving spots of the Orient, in fact one of the leading ports of the world, but I sees it all when Theo informs me that the British owns it. And after seeing them marines

WORLD BEATERS 

fight on that river boat, I'm not surprised when Theo goes on to tell me that the British won this island in a battle with China back in 1841-and that the battle lasted only one and a half hours. When these British marines want something they don't fool.

From our view, as we're crossing the bay, Hong Kong looks strictly occidental, but we finds later, as you'd expect where the Chinese outnumber the British fifty to one, that while the town is British in government, architecture and, as far as possible, customs, the atmosphere of the place savors strongly of the Orient. 

The business district-which is as large as you'd expect to find in a town of half a million-is built along the level of the bay, and running up the side of the mountain are beautiful winding drives, with here and there the palatial home of a British official or merchant; and crowning the summit is the Peak Hotel.

Since the island is in the tropics, summer is at home here the year round and all the bloom of the tropics add their bit to beautify Hong Kong. Ascending to the Peak Hotel for lunch we take rickishaws around the famous Peak path that affords, what is claimed to be, the finest panoramic view in the East. The color schemes from here are gorgeous. It's late in the afternoon and, standing on the peak and looking first off over the sea and then down on Victoria, the city of 

Hong Kong and at last into the West, where the sun is setting, Theo says, "Isn't it marvelous, Al, how the sun reflects these colors of the tropical light, from blue to gray and white in the morning, to gray, rose and gold in the afternoon, and then, sinking as it does into that background of flaming glory behind the hills, it seems the whole world has been dipped in a sea of violet." I'm beginning to think Theo's years at Yale was wasted-he should of been studying art under 

Michelangelo or some of them famous Irishmen.

What might he of painted with his natural gift of

WORLD BEATERS 

103 blending colors. We takes dinner on the Peak and starts back for Hong Kong just at that mysterious time when romance seems to rule the world-how I'd care to run into K or Miss Durant just now—and

Theo takes up his discourse on color schemes again and continues, "Al, I'm positively thrilled at this symphony in colors. As the violet turns to purple and the purple deepens to sable, the lights of the hillside dwellings begin to dance like myriad stars, until the whole mountain takes on the form of a mighty gem, glistening in the blackness of the night." We've descended the mountain now and there's nothing to do until 11:30, when we're to be the guests of some Chinese that come down from Shanghai on our boat.

They live here, and they confess they went to Hong Kong to go in business so they'd have the protection of the British. Well, they invited us to a midnight dinner, what they call "take chow with us." It's to be a regular native dish and ain't it though. Midnight soon rolls round and we meets our hosts per schedule, and is taken to a real swell cabaret-a Chinese cabaret. It's a four-story building and the cabaret occupies all four floors. I don't know if the different floors is for different classes or not-if you know you'll be. able to figure out our class because they took us to the top floor-and we was showed into a private dining room about fifteen feet square. There was me and 

Theo, two other Americans and a French couple-all from the boat-and these two middle-aged, welldressed and educated Chinese.

It was about 12:30 when we eventually got to the room and it was all of an hour before food appeared but when it started coming, it came. They must of got the orders mixed in the kitchen and sent us all the orders that come in from all four floors. Don't try to believe it but we has a twenty-three course dinner. 

None of this chop suey or chow mein-they don't even

WORLD BEATERS 

know what that is in China-that stuff was concocted in Mott Street-we has a regular Chinese feed and, just so's everything will be consistent, we all has to eat with chopsticks. Holy mackerel! It's 3:30 before we gets away with twenty-three courses with chopsticksand I had lots more on my lap than I got past my tonsils. Did you ever try to eat with chopsticks? You gets the object of your appetite pinched between the ends of two long sticks, holding the other ends between your first, second and third fingers; you starts up toward your teeth with the load and just as you gets opposite your tie-pin your first finger, or maybe it's your second or third, any of them can do it, moves just the fraction of an inch and the next picture shows a perfectly good morsel of food doing a high-fly over the left shoulder or taking an indrop for the front of your vest. 

But after the first dozen courses I ain't doing too bad and, as one of the other Americans says, "If it weren't for the fact that we drop so much we'd never be able to manage twenty-three courses." 

With the food over, the place is cleaned out and native girls enter with native instruments and they play and dance and try to sing. This goes on for another hour and since our boat pulls out at 6:30 Theo claims we'll have to be excused, and, after telling how much we enjoyed the evening, we tears ourselves away to the boat again and hits the hay, and as we leaves the last bit of China behind, me and Theo is dead to the world.

When we "comes to" in the morning we're out on another of them silvery seas heading for the Philippine Islands and maybe you don't think it's warm.

About four o'clock the next afternoon we slips into 

Manila Bay, like Admiral Dewey, and takes possession of the city. Boy, what a city! And it ain't just because it belongs to Uncle Sam, either, that I'm for it-though we do feel like we was home, being on

WORLD BEATERS 

105 American soil again-but this town would rate high with me if it belonged to Russia-and that's the worst country I can think of. Our boat docks at the longest and best equipped pier in the world-I didn't know they had so many championships in the Orient-and after the usual goodbyes to newly made friends aboard, who continue on-we grabs a cab with orders to take us to their best hotel. It's the Manila Hotel and it looks to be in the middle of a city park. It's a good thing we notices from a distance that it's a swelllooking place, because when we gets up to it we can't see the hotel for flowers and bushes. Our bedroom turns out to be another suite of rooms and we can't wait to get a cold shower and into some dry, clean, light clothes. Brother, I'm warm. Said showers over and light suits on-and I purposely lose my underwear -we starts out to see what we can absorb of Manila before dinner. We strolls across the Luneta, a double-way boulevard running through the centre of the park in front of our hotel, and notices that this park is nothing more nor less than a golf course. Hot dog.

We gets into the centre of the open space and gapes around. In front of us is the beautiful Elks Club and 

Army and Navy Club. On our left the Manila carnival in full swing; behind us our own flower hidden hotel and to our right we looks across beautiful Manila Bay. What a grand place to spend a few weeks in, I thinks.

It's still pretty warm, even this late in the afternoon, and we don't want to have to make another change before eating, so we halts a carriage and says, "The business district, please." It appears we're in the new Manila because in a few minutes we're passing through gate in a stone wall that seems to enclose this part of town. 

It does. We're now in the old medieval city of Manila, built centuries ago by the Spanish-oh, I know some history when it comes

WORLD BEATERS 

to our own country-it seems we suddenly was transferred back to Havana. It's a little bit of Spain as she was at that time, and the sudden change is most pleasant. He takes us through the narrow streets of shops and on to the Pasig River which runs through the city. On our way back we stops a minute at the old fortress and we can see our hotel from the window. We notices, too, that the old moat outside the wall is now filled up and has fairways and greens in it, as part of the golf course. It's almost time for dinner and as we approaches our hotel the sun is setting across the bay and I turns to Theo and says, "Well, boy friend, what do you think of Uncle's town in the 

Orient?" And Theo would go into another of them trances. He says, "What a magic city, where pleasing contrasts are the rule and artistic extremes are so much at home. How primitive the Old Manila and what distant yesterdays her native atmosphere recalls.

But from her walls, that seem to frown on the intruder, comes the friendly call of the golf links nestling in that one-time moat. From the iron-barred windows of that old fortress appears across the park the beautiful Manila Hotel. The roar of a plane overhead drowns out the faint sounds of the ripples in the wake of a 'sampan' as the native mariner propels himself homeward through the waters of the Pasig."

Our carriage has now rounded the corner of our hotel and we're in full view of Manila Bay and a gorgeous sunset, and as the soft notes of the hotel orchestra fill the air, Theo goes on, "And when the sun sinks mystically into the sea-leaving the funnels of Uncle Sam's navy, in Manila Bay, standing out against a flaming background like skeletal forms piercing a sea of gold, one imagines he hears the luring strains of a native instrument-serenading the sweetest girl in the world-drowned out by that crying trombone or that weeping saxophone." He had me so far gone that if

WORLD BEATERS 

107 he'd of just gone one step farther and mentioned a moon, I'd of either kissed him or the driver.

In the dining room that evening we meets a chap from New York who is in Manila on business and is rather well acquainted in town. He's alone so we kind of hooks up together. During the dance that evening we meets a young German chemist, also in Manila on business, and alone, so we adds him to our club of strangers. The chap from New York invited us all to join a party he had arranged for the following night, as a kind of appreciation party for a young American architect in Manila who has won his indebtedness through service, and so a gang of us are found at the 

Santa Ana cabaret the next night, stepping high and handsome with the Filipino girls; visitors, while their boat's in, and wives of naval officers, whose husbands are out charting islands for Uncle Sam, all strangers but happy. This Santa Ana place rates as the largest cabaret in the world-I'm getting fed up on that expression, "largest in the world." The next time anybody uses that line I'm going to tell them about Chrysler Tower, Ford production and the amount of money used to buy a seat in the Senate-you'd think we didn't have any world records in America. But Santa Ana is plenty large and nobody needs a larger one. The dance floor is divided into two parts with the orchestra in the centre of the openwork partition. One end -about three-quarters of the whole-is for natives, marines, and who cares to go there; the other quarter is for nice people-like you-but this night that end of the floor could of been on the Byrd Antarctic Expedition; a void would look like a traffic jam compared to the number in that part of the hall. And do we make "whoopee"? Most everybody takes "giggleliquid" but me and Theo, we stands four square on ginger ale. And you know, I'm beginning to think that 

I'll stay on the wagon even after I get back home. I

WORLD BEATERS 

never knew how much I was missing on a party like this 'til I stayed sober through one of them. Me and

Theo has the best time of anyone in the hall and, not only that, but we could tell the next day what had happened the night before. This architect turns out to be a regular fellow and suggests the five of us goes for a hike into the Benguet mountains through Baguio.

He has a new Buick sedan, so early on the third morning a car leaves Manila with a New Yorker, a German, a Manilan and ourselves a little league of nations, what? Our drive follows the Pasig a ways, and if you care for beautiful streams you should see the Pasig with its surface practically covered with great white water lilies. We now turns off through the paddy fields or rice fields, if you like—and on into the sugar cane plantations. The roadway is lined with graceful coconut palms and way off in the distance, through the blue haze that almost obscures their view, is the Benguet mountains. Here and there, protected from the hot rays of the burning sun by a preserved piece of the jungle, is a native's hut, high up on stilts to be safe from snakes or from water in the rainy season. These quaint little homes are mostly built of hemp straw.

It's a good gravel road we're on and the bus is moving along at about fifty-five miles an hour without a jolt. As we approach a native settlement we slows down and sees that the hand of Uncle Sam has penetrated farther into the island than Manila. Here's a fair looking schoolhouse, and while most of the cheap little buildings don't remind you of home, there's a sure enough gas station. I looks around for the tourist camp and the hot-dog stand, but they ain't got that far yet. Our friend from Manila tells us that the Philippines boasts nearly 8000 schools with a daily attendance of 1,000,000 students. He sure tells me something new when he says the University of

WORLD BEATERS 

109 the Philippines in Manila, with seven thousand students, is the oldest university under the American flag-older than Harvard-we must touch the hat to Spain for that, also for the numerous beautiful churches in Manila and elsewhere on the island. In fact, we was in one church in Manila where they got the only bamboo pipe organ in the world-Ye gods, there it is again-but let's get back on this highway for Baguio, along which there's over four hundred varieties of orchids. This town of Baguio that we're heading for is about 160 miles north of Manila and was laid out by Americans as a kind of summer capital and a resort camp for Army and Navy officers. We've been on the road now for several hours and at last we've reached the foothills of the Benguet mountains and starts to climb. Baguio, you know, is 5000 feet above Manila and you make all that climb in twentytwo miles, most of the way over the world-famous "zigzag"- -a narrow ridge, chiseled in the side of the mountain, just the width of the car. On one side you look straight up to dizzy peaks and on the other side you look down to dizzy depths. As if that wasn't plenty the turns are so sharp the radiator almost knocks the spare tire off when we breaks around bend. The only danger we ain't got is approaching traffic. This zigzag thing is gated every couple miles and they phone ahead from one station to the next which way traffic is coming-because it would be impossible to pass. At each gate there's a little rest house where you can go in and let your heart stop palpitating and these little rest stations are completely covered with wisteria of every hue. Boy, they sure are beautiful sights. I suppose that's to give you courage to try and make the next lap. As we starts on again Theo says, "Aren't these chasms fascinating?" "Yes," I says, "they remind me of the day they had a sale down at Sweeney's casket factory-I could hardly

WORLD BEATERS 

keep myself at home." "Al," he reproaches me, "haven't you any eye for beauty?" "Sure," I says, "but this kind of beauty is like your line of Yale chatter-it's too deep for me." At last we reaches the top and sure enough, nestling down in a little valley, around the edge of a lake, is this city in the clouds, Baguio. It's almost worth the chance you take on the zigzag to see Baguio-but not entirely; nothing completely justifies that road-but this summer capital, with Camp John Hay, is dangerous competition to the optometrists of Manila and the world. "If looking down into this series of valleys, designed by nature and landscaped by man," as Theo says, "doesn't heal the vision of all infirmities, nothing will."

We winds our way through what Theo terms as "the tortuous drives of Baguio" to the Pines Hotel.

They sure go in for snappy hotels over here. We gets a suite of rooms that accommodates all five of us and what a mistake the management made when he done that. It's dinner time when we arrives at the Pines so we have to snap into it and get the clay out of our ears and ready to raid the dining room with the rest of the nice people. We should of been here by two or three o'clock but we stopped off along the road to watch a cock fight. That seems to be one of the leading industries, in spite of the fact that the Filipino enjoys the reputation of being the "only Christian people in the Orient."

We sees a gang of natives gathered around an old barn and coming down the road is another bunch and every last Filipino has a rooster under his arm. We decides, after much ado, that it must be either a cock fight or else this is where the rabbi is guzzling chickens for the members of his synagogue. Either being worth a look we parks and joins the crowd. We were right the first time, it is a cock fight; and all around the

WORLD BEATERS 

111 barn is Filipinos strapping knives to roosters' hoofs that looks every bit as wicked as the tools of a Chinese pirate. The two that's in the ring when we arrives is being held apart by their owners until all bets is laid, and after everybody in the place, except us, bets on one or the other, they are let go and they sure are game. They seem to know that the loser goes into chicken pie and so they fight to escape the knife and fork. But it ain't long. After a couple of flies at each other one of them lands a haymaker and the other takes the count. The money is gathered up, the two owners clears out and the next two takes the ring. We watches this thing go on for about an hour, but from the number of roosters about the barn and the gang of Filipinos still coming up the road when we leaves, you'd likely get in before it's over if you sail for Manila not later than 1936. But that's how it happens we don't arrive at the Pines until dinner time, but I sure enjoyed them cock fights. After dinner, there being nothing to do, the inmates of this place all look like patients of something or other to me, so I suggests we tumble in and leave this sanitarium to its quiet. We do the one but not the other. We tumble in, but, boy, we disturb everybody's quiet, what I mean. About ten o'clock the management despatches half its staff to our suite to demand quiet, not to request, you understand, but to demand it. It seems it's a case of losing us or the other eighteen guests, so the manager gets his pencil out and shows himself where it would be better to peeve or even lose five than to lose eighteen-yes, better by some thirteen patients, I mean guests. Not knowing of any other place we could go in town, we holds a conference and decides to go to sleep. In the morning we employs a real Igorot tribesman, who has picked up some English words-like myself-to guide us through the mountain to Mummy Cave, a place reported by some adventurers in the hills and which our

WORLD BEATERS 

99 guide claims he knows. We drives about twenty miles to where even a new Buick must accept defeat and, parking, we starts out on foot for Mummy Cave, the burial place of the Igorots. It ain't too bad at first, but after we've gone so far that I'd never be able to find my way back alone the trail begins to get tough. 

After a couple hours we're just clinging to the edge of the mountain by the help of roots, the Igorot is in his bare feet, in fact practically all of him is bare, so he has the added advantage of a toe hold. These mountains might have valleys at the bottom of them but you can't prove it by me. I looks down and there's the clouds below us. If I was scared on the zigzag, I'm petrified now. Just then Theo makes one of his foolish remarks about, "Aren't you thrilled, Al?" "No," 

I gasps, "but I'll be something that sounds a whole lot like that if I lose my footing.' I hardly had that pun out of me when our guide comes to a stop, and looking all round, including up, says, "I lost." "Lost what?" 

I asks, trying to appear interested. "Lost way," this proper cuckoo says. "Oh, is that all," I cries, almost falling off mother earth, "I was afraid it was something serious." "Theo," I says, in despair, "we're only lost, 12,000 miles from my favorite restaurant." Theo would laugh at me if I was featured in a funeraland he says, "Who would have dared hope for such a wonderful experience?" "I'll bite," I says, "who would?" We gropes around in them impossible mountains until dark without, a sensible suggestion from a soul, except mine, that we murder the guide-and when it gets so dark it ain't safe to take a step, for fear your next one will be a long one-we decides to hang on the side of the mountain until dawn. What a night! Everything that brushes against my leg feels exactly like a boa constrictor around my neck. A rock broke loose from the mountain and came thundering down right past my ear and I manages to say, "Theo,

WORLD BEATERS 

113 if you get back to the Pines, I left a $1.25 under the cover on the dresser." The rest of the party seems to be taking this thing as an opportunity of a lifetime, but me-well, it ain't that I'm afraid to die, but I always wanted to know that they found the body. was never so glad to see daylight in all my life.

But when it eventually does arrive, I'm still sharing that wonderful experience of Theo's-lost in the Benguet mountains. We're all thirsty, hungry and tired but we plods on just hoping that Buddha can see 'way up there and show us the way. We has a little luck. 

Here's a mountain spring and we almost dries the thing up the way we takes on water. Well, that helps, so we carry on. About noon the guide stumbles on the right path and it's no time 'til he has us to Mummy Cave. But by this time I'm too close to a corpse myself to get much kick out of a cave full of mummies so I just nose in, autograph a few skulls and calls it a day. This cave has to be entered on the stomach and when you get in you can almost stand up. It's cold, damp, clammy and very dark. Someone strikes a match and all around the wall of the cave is mummies in a sitting position. The bats are so thick in there that you got to keep your hands up to protect your face. What a cute little mausoleum these Igorots picked out, eh? Now all we got to do is get back to the hotel. That's all. Not far from this cave we comes upon an Igorot settlement. From a little distance it looks like a field of beehives but as we gets closer they look more like dog houses. They are pup-tent affairs, about three feet high at the peak and three feet wide on the ground and perhaps six feet long. 

They are made entirely of what looks like swamp grass. They are individual houses, one for each Igorot, and he has to shove his way in through a little square hole in front which is hardly big enough to let me in-I tried it-and when he gets in he stays put

WORLD BEATERS 

'til morning. This tribe is a little, brown-skinned, kindly faced race who lives on fruit, nuts and herbs.

They nearly all crawled in their hole when we come upon them, but some of the raw, pot-bellied kids stayed out to give us the once over. 99 After many hours, famished for food and feet blistered, we actually gets back to the car. Thank heaven for that. And in another hour we're in the dining room of the Pines Hotel, giving imitations of starving swine let loose in a trough of vegetable soup. The next morning we visits the amphitheatre at Camp John 

Hay, which Theo describes as a "bit of paradise.' I never seen so many flowers or so many different colors in one garden before. It's a kind of sunken garden, shaped like the "stadium" of an American college, only just about one-quarter as big, and the flowers are terraced up the sides and there's a beautiful pagoda in the centre. We don't play around but we can see that the golf course at the camp is the best ever. We want to get back in town to see the market and it's worth seeing, too. Here the natives of the mountain tribes bring their handiwork, in the form of pillow cases, table runners, hand-bags, and many other useful articles, to sell to the visitors. They're strong for color and your pillow slip might be white and it might not.

We takes lunch at the country club-one fine club house-and after a drive through town, taking in the Catholic church, the city hall, the observatory on Mount Mirador, we starts back for Manila over that terrible zigzag. But everybody enjoyed Baguio-even me-and since we survived the trip to Mummy Cave 

I'm mighty glad we went. No matter what Baguio ain't it's beautiful and it's got one ideal climate. The drive back ain't half bad and I almost enjoyed it. I know it's getting closer to level every minute so I don't object to the speed we're going at, though it

WORLD BEATERS 

115 really is excessive. However-like everything else, so far-we gets through safely. And when we piles out of the car at the Manila Hotel we gives a unanimous vote of thanks to our architect friend from Manila and claims he's afforded us a trip never to be forgotten-I've no doubt about me remembering it.

The next day we spends just ambling about the city and roaming around the Church of St. Augustin, the oldest building in the city; admiring the hand-carved hardwood decorations of St. Ignatius church and we drives out a few miles to Los Banos to examine that bamboo organ. We visits the University of the Philippines; sees Fort McKinley-because it's another of them "world-beaters," being the largest military reservation in the world. And go through the Bilibid 

Prison where 2500 contented prisoners turn out some of the finest handmade articles I ever seen. They must of all been there for some time to get so clever and, from the time it looks to take to make some of them things, a lot of them must be going to stay there a long time yet. Then we strolls through the business. district and picks up a few things we needs and I can't help not noticing two things about every one of them 

300,000 Filipinos in Manila-if I seen them allnine out of ten have had small pox and ten out of ten have some gold in their teeth. It ain't reasonable to suppose that every Filipino had a cavity in a front tooth. I tells Theo that they must of realized how well gold goes with their complexions and if they wasn't lucky enough to have a cavity they had one made. So, if you're a dentist and business is dull, pack up your drills and go to Manila-I'd feel lots better if there wasn't a drill left in America.

That evening the five of us gets together again and takes in a pretty fair movie. For them two hours I was actually back home. After the show we goes over and takes in the Manila carnival where representa-

WORLD BEATERS 

tives of many of the island tribes of the Philippines puts on exhibitions of their own peculiar ways of doing things. Of course, every island wasn't represented. 

Besides the hundreds of islands that are named and mapped, one of the naval officers tells us that, from a flight over the Philippines, they figures there's at least 7000 islands they've never even charted yet.

Their native dances and folk songs are mighty interesting, and when the floor of this great open auditorium is cleaned up about 11 P. M. two jazzy orchestras favor the gang with real American dance music and we grabs ourselves some Filipino lassies and shakes the rheumatism out of the joints. 

The next day we devotes to politics. We goes out in the cool of the early morning and looks over the palatial residence of "Hizzoner," the governor general-not a bad place to have to live either. Then, since the senate and legislature, and all them unnecessary parts of the government is in session, we beats it back to the legislative building, which is a building. 

Boy, it would look good in anybody's capital-no foolin'. We just arrives at this photographic studio -humorously described as "Legislative Hall"-in time to cop a couple seats in the balcony, immediately over the senate body, where neither word nor deed of any member can escape us. The senators are in their proper stalls and looks to be all set to relieve the farmer or whatever happens to be the problem of the 

Philippines at the moment. But wait-who's them fellows hiding under black sheets, behind box-like contraptions on three stilts? "Photographers," Theo informs me. In every corner is a battery of them, the doors are clogged by more and the aisles is occluded with their paraphernalia. Their assistants are busy loading the flash-light racks; the senators are adjusting their ties and trying to replace their breakfasttable frowns with smiles. The lights start flashing.

WORLD BEATERS 

117 The dome of the assembly hall is rapidly filling with gray smoke. More flashes and everybody in the balcony is seriously threatened with asphyxiation. Bigger and better flash-lights seems to be the order of the day, so the merry work goes on. At 11:30 the president of the senate, remembering that a dinner is being given in his honor at 12 o'clock, orders the cameras from the room, after everybody has been photographed but the janitor, and calls the meeting to order. 

Can you imagine our chagrin—or can you?-after inhaling the fumes from flash-lights for one and a half hours, the meeting opens-in Spanish-and the only Spanish I knows is a senorita down in Brownsville, Texas. This first, and only, speaker reads a lengthy paper in Spanish, which is seconded by another member and, after a unanimous vote, the president, because it's time for his dinner date, adjourns the meeting until the afternoon. 

Theo turns to me and says, "Shall we return this afternoon and observe the continuation?" "You might," I says, "but if you think I will you'd better have another X-Ray taken." "What's the matter?" he asks. "Well, in the first place," I says, "I forgot to bring my gas mask; and in the second place, while it may be perfectly proper for a Spaniard to 'throw the bull', I still prefer my senatorial discourses served in English-though I confess the only senators I ever heard in Washington could of been speaking Spanish for all of me-I'll bet Hoover don't know what half of them try to say and I'll bet my last dime he don't care." But as we're leaving legislative hall we hears a terrible noise down the corridor and since we're going that way anyway we sticks our noses inside this room when we gets up to it and it seems that this is a session of the Filipino legislature. Boy, the senate is tame compared to these babies. This is the original school of oratory, without the ethics. There's more

WORLD BEATERS 

action in a session of the Filipino legislature that in a Russian revolution. The only thing required of a member is that he have a good set of vocal cords, a strong diaphragm, a powerful larynx, an utter disregard of the other fellow's opinion and complete intolerance of parliamentary procedure. Out of a possible hundred in the room I counts seven that ain't talking, but the doorman informs me that they just exhausted themselves as we come in and if we stay a few minutes 'til they recuperate we'll see the house functioning at a hundred per cent efficiency. There's just one thing I can't understand and that is how the theatres, Santa Ana and other high-priced places of amusement can carry on against such high-class entertainment as a session of the Filipino legislature, which is free. If they put a cover charge of ten pesos on attending this legislature they'd need the army and navy to keen back the crowd. But I suppose there's something in what Theo says, "While their methods of doing things may seem strange to us, and humorous, they do get things done-and done right-which is about all you can say for any legislative body and more than you can truthfuly say about many of them."

We're scheduled to leave Manila the next day so we spend this last night packing up and writing the usual "hooie" postcards back home. As usual, the next day immediately follows and when the S. S. President Van Buren clears from the Philippines me and 

Theo does likewise, leaving the architect, the German and the New Yorker waving us goodbye from the nier. As we steams out of Manila Bay around noon.

Theo takes a last long look back over the city and savs, "Al, we've just left the one city that has everything."

These boat rides of three or four davs don't seem long after the trip from Honolulu to Japan, except we had K and B for that and we're alone now. But

WORLD BEATERS 

119 with the usual deck sports and evening dances it don't seem any time 'til we're packing up again and getting ready to disembark at Singapore, a small island at, the toe of the Malaya peninsula, which Sir Stamford Raffles bought from the Sultan of Johore in 1824 for $60,000.00. It was a trading centre then of some 

10,000 natives—but today, just about a hundred years later, there's nearly half a million in the town and the port does an annual business of over a billion dollars. 

I ask you, did Mr. Raffles know his real estate, or did he? I know them figures is right because I copied them off Raffles' income tax report. As we leaves the ship for the Raffles Hotel, the purser, who is going into town with us, points out one of the largest drydocks in the world—yes, they've started that againand he also showed us where England proposed to spend over $50,000,000 on a naval base; this Singapore seems to be kind of a gateway to the eastern and southern seas.

These Malaya states, we learn, are partly owned by England; others have their own sultan with a British commissioner and the rest have their rajahs, but are British advised. "How's that for diplomacy?" 

I asks Theo. "England keeps these babies satisfied by letting them keep a sultan or rajah and thinking they have independence, but I'll bet you they don't run themselves any more than they run London." "Aren't you rather jumping at conclusions, Al?" Theo asks. "Theo," I says, "I ain't a statesman, it's true, but I ain't blind either. If England is going to spend $50,000,000 on a naval base in Singapore she owns the Malaya peninsula, you can call it being commissioned or advised or what have you, but them sultans or rajahs are just there for atmosphere to pacify the natives and to attract birds like us to travel through their jungle to see where they keeps them." "Travel is certainly broadening you, Al. That would have all escaped your observation two months ago and while

WORLD BEATERS 

it isn't probable that any of your conclusions are true, I'm glad to know you're thinking." And the only snappy comeback I can think of is "Oh?"

Well, now that I've reversed my usual procedure and completed all my opinions of the country, we're about to start out to see what percentage I'm batting.

Our drive to the hotel brought me a conflicting horde of opinions. The buildings look occidental but from the clothes hanging out every window and strung across the street on a line-it was wash day in town -the place looks like China. The native is a cross between a Chinaman and my idea of an Indian, but he is dressed for the most part like a young Igorot in the Philippines-a medium-sized handkerchief would make him a whole suit-with only a couple less than two pair of pants. All about town are both high and low types of every nationality in the world, and Theo says, "You know, Al, Singapore has the reputation of being the refuge for more outcasts of every class, color and creed than any other city on earth." That's two championships already for Singapore, providing they eventually build that naval base. Over on a distant hill I sees a swell looking cathedral-or maybe it's only a church-and not far away I sees the domes and minarets of a Mohammedan mosque. I don't know what I think of Singapore.

After getting settled at the Raffles, changing into clean whites and taking care of the inner man, we takes a cab out to the exclusive residential district and I never hopes to see such beautiful settings for homes this side of St. Peter. I just feels like somebody lifted me out of the world and says, "There,

Al, pick your mansion." These places each look to be in the centre of a famous botanical garden and set up just enough on a hill to look out over the sea and they make living in Singapore perfect. We drives through the zoological gardens, where the monkeys

WORLD BEATERS 

121 are thicker than passengers on a Japanese train. Every other monkey has a baby monk clinging to its bosom or riding top deck, and maybe they don't hang on when Mrs. Monk takes to a tree. They stick to her like a plaster on a Scotchman's back. These gardens are just part of the original jungle with drives cut through and small clearings here and there with gorgeous beds of tropical flowers. The trees of the jungle is one of the big things I notices between the countries farther north and these down by the equator. They're not so awful big but nearly every other tree is different. In these jungles alone there are over 9000 kinds. 

Leaving these beauty spots we goes to visit the private menagerie in Singapore where they bring the recently captured animals from the jungle and ship them to all corners of the globe. You get in for nothing but you pay twenty cents to get out-and you're glad to get out at any price. I'm just admiring a golden leopard, marveling at the wonders of nature and I guess that I've kind of forgot that the thing's alive and when it hissed at me I'm so surprised that 

I jumps backwards without looking and the most horrifying roar this side of Hades or the other side either for that matter-comes right from my heels. 

It's just a split second later that the attendant is informing me that I must go out through the gate and not over the fence. How I ever succeeded in doing a Douglas Fairbanks up that fence is beyond me, but 

I'll wager had the Venus de Milo-armless as that dame is backed into that Johore tiger, she'd of been a close second getting over that fence. Realizing, now, that the cause of my latest attack of heart failure is securely caged, I sheepishly climb down from the high board fence-with nothing injured but my dignity -and faces Theo, who is still in the middle of a bad case of hysterics, and to think I had just been telling him how anxious I was to do some big-game hunting.

WORLD BEATERS 

When he controls himself he says, "You'd better trade your gun for a vaulting-pole, Al." And then he's off again in the "ha-ha's." And so, as the boys with the 

Swiss movements say, "A good time is had by all,”all but Al. That tiger sure scared the "l" out of "all" for me. They had just brought eight tigers down from the jungles of Johore and, as Theo says, “I inadvertently disturbed one." But don't think he didn't get even. I have often heard that wild animals won't live in captivity-they just pine away and die. Well, if this tiger was giving an exhibition of pining away and dying, I don't ever want to even see a picture of one that gets so infatuated with life that it wants to keep on living. But after my heart quiets down and some of my native courage returns-everybody has a little, you know-I really do want to visit the country where them pets come from. 

So the next day all is made ready for a trip into

Johore—one of the British advised states of Malaya. 

We e rents an old car from a drive-it-yourself-stationonly I'd change it to "try-and-drive-it-yourself”—engages a native Malayan as guide and strikes out for the jungles. Leaving the Island of Singapore we crosses the great granite causeway to the mainland of Malaya and we're in Johore. We learns, after we arrive in Johore, that in order to arrange for a hunt in the province, a couple of weeks is necessary to go through all the red tape-and you have to put up everything but the old homestead as deposit-so, having neither red tape nor homesteads with us, we gives up the idea of a hunt and decides to content ourselves with merely passing through the jungles and viewing them. We drives up as far as we can get and then we starts out on foot to penetrate this jungle which is even too thick to see through. "So this is where the tigers slink silently through the underbrush, seeking what they may devour?" Theo asks the guide.

WORLD BEATERS 

123 "Yes," he replies, "and cobras, boa constrictors and other deadly snakes crawl all through here." "Let's go," I cries, making a mad dash for the car. "I don't mind climbing fences to get away from a tiger that's securely caged, but I'm altogether too young to appear on the menu of any cobra or boa constrictor." Thus it is that a used-much used-Durant car, hitting on three cylinders when it's going good leaves a cloud of dust behind that completely shuts off our view of the jungles of Johore. And do you think that makes me mad?

Our drive back to Singapore is through great rubber plantations, so we stops off in a field and watches them gather the "milk" from the trees in little cups and take it to a vat in the centre of the bush-like a maple sugar bush-and melt all the drippings into great pads of rubber. They draw this to the nearest rubber mill where it's washed, purified, rolled into pads again, baled and sent off to a factory to be put into automobile tires, goloshes, golf balls and teachers' necks. Our guide tells us that Singapore ships more rubber and more tin than any other port in the world. Two more marks for Singapore. 

That night there's a dance at the Raffles Hotel and a gang from another boat that's in is over for dinner and stays for the dance. For just a bunch of strangers we sure has a grand time. With half the world still panting for us to come and look them over we has to shake the dust of Singapore from our dogs and climb up the coast to Penang. There are moonlight nights again and how I'm cursing our luck that we're alone. 

We sees the Southern Cross for the first time that night-all alone. I guess it's quite a sight but I can't work up any interest in stars, or moons either, by myself. We skirts the shores of Sumatra, through the Straits of Malacca, and it ain't many hours 'til we're tying up at the second and only other port of

WORLD BEATERS 

any consequence in all the Malayan peninsula- Penang. 

Where, in Singapore, we landed some distance from the business district, here we seems to step right off the boat into main street, with all the hustle and bustle of the town forming our first picture of the city. It's not so much unlike Singapore-smaller, of course, but customs, habits, appearances and religions of the people are practically identical. I asks Theo what the place is noted for and he says that he don't know that it's noted for anything, except as a port, and the natural beauty of the island. "Ain't it got any world records?" I wants to know, figuring, by this time that it's a poor sort of town that can't boast something as the best in the world. "Well, I'm sure I've never heard or read of anything about Penang that could list her with those unique cities that claim world records unless," he remembers, "it could possibly be 'Snake Temple'. I don't recall ever hearing of such a temple any place but Penang.' So I digs down into a recess in my mind, where I puts cities that are to be forgotten, and I brings up Penang and credits it with one doubtful championship. "Well, let's go see it,"

I says, knowing our boat sails tomorrow and we've no time to fool. We hails a car and starts out for Snake

Temple. Out on the edge of town, in anything but pleasant surroundings, we finds this Snake Temple hold-up. Now, it's O. K. by me for a Malayan to worship snakes if he wants to-I wish I could make him a present of every serpent in the world-but I'm just about as much opposed to a Malayan or any other person stinging me as a serpent. We're lured out here by what Theo has read in a guide book, where it says, "Here you see the native in his lowest form of idolatry. Creeping about the high altar, the money box, everywhere the eye may chance to turn, there are creeping the great, slimy, venomous reptiles." If

WORLD BEATERS 

125 Barnum wasn't already dead he'd die of envy when he heard the truth about this Snake Temple. After we fights our way through the horde of beggars at the door, we move cautiously toward the altar, being careful to escape all cobras and the like. But it ain't long 'til the truth strikes us and we're at ease. It's just a plain hold-up. There's maybe half a dozen little garter snakes laying in corners, about as lively as last year's skin. I'm just about to tell Theo what I think when a monk or priest, or whatever they call their ring-leader, comes up and tries to sell us some snake eggs. Imagine. Wouldn't they go great on ham? 

I tells him, "No, thanks. Mama says she has enough eggs for now but she wants a five-cent soup bone."

Theo is pulling my arm to lay off, but I can't resist. 

On our way out another salesman grabs us and hands us a pencil and points to a big guest-book where visitors are requested to register-and off to the right is a column where you puts down how much you want to donate to the upkeep of the temple. I figures it's too late to start feeding them snakes now-they're too far gone to revive-and the monks can get jobs on the docks or buy themselves rickishaws and go into an honest hold-up business. So I just puts my "Al. 

Tool" down and am about to drop the pencil when 

I looks up the money column and sees that everybody has made donations from two to ten dollars and looking across to see who was goofy enough to drop ten bucks I lets a shout out of me that almost wakes up the snakes-it's Katherine Playfair. "Ye gods, Theo, I cries, "K and B are in town," and pulling him behind me to the car, I orders the driver to get back to the dock as soon as possible and I promises him double fare if he can chip ten minutes off that. and gets ourselves over to that boat and, sure enough, they're aboard. What a reunion, but not what it would of been if the other ninety-eight students wasn't grinning at us. We organizes a party of twelve to have dinner at the Runnymede Hotel and dance after, the party to be on Theo. Boy, it was one wonderful night. 

We only dances 'til 12-as the students have to be in by 11—but me and Theo and the Playfair sisters don't get back to the boat 'til 2:30. It's a gorgeous full moon that shows us every palm as we takes in the famous drive around the island of Penang. The girls speak, often, of the night we looked on San Francisco from Twin Peaks in the moonlight. During the drive someone mentions Snake Temple and I kids K for getting stuck for ten smackers. "Why, I didn't give them a cent," K comes back. "You didn't," I says, and turning to Theo, I says, "It's the old army game, Theo.” With K's head on my shoulder and her hand in mine we drinks in the beauty of the night, and I must confess it's plenty intoxicating too. When we arrives back in town we takes sampans out to the boat and if you ain't never seen the 'liquid silver' that drips from a sampan's oar in the moonlight, then you ain't seen nothing yet. At the foot of the rope ladder that is dropped down from the boat, we bids sad goodbyes with the usual hope that we meet some more. 

On our way back to our own boat Theo says, “And you were so disgusted with Snake Temple. If it hadn't have been for that we'd never have known the girls were in town." "Theo," I says, "if I thought that drum was open now I'd go out and give them whatever amount they've got opposite my name." 

The next morning I'm awakened by the blast of a steamer's whistle and looking out the porthole I sees

K's boat gliding out of the harbor for India. A couple hours later we lifts our own gangplank and bids farewell to Penang and the Malaya states and heads up

WORLD BEATERS 

127 north for Burma. Sailing all that day and night along the coasts of Malaya, Siam and Burma we're up early this morning to observe "The dawn come up like thunder out of China 'crost the bay"—at least that's what Theo claims we're observing. He says we're on 

Kipling's "Road to Mandalay"-but you've only got

Theo's word for that. We're heading into the yellow waters of the Rangoon River and we're due at the capital of Burma in a couple hours. We glides silently up this river, passing countless numbers of crude-oil storage tanks along the bank, and eventually reaches Rangoon. This is another city, I learns, where the 

British had to take over the reins to keep peace in this corner of the universe and, as usual, where them folks own the place, it's important. It seems, in 1885, when the Union Jack went up, Rangoon was just another town along the river and today it's one of the leading ports of the British Empire. It's a great place for shipping oil, tin and precious gems; also rice, and while I'm on the subject, that's Burma's world record -she has 8,000,000 acres in rice, annually, thereby taking the hand-painted Buddha for rice production. 

This town of Rangoon has about 340,000 Burmese -counting the monks at the Buddhist temples-and, while other authorities (did you get that "other"?) claim that the Burmese came down from Tibet originally, it's my opinion that they was always here in 

Burma. What would be the idea of nature having a country as big as France down here in a fertile valley and a great climate and keep the people 'way up in Tibet where even Commander Byrd couldn't get to them? But them college professors, who feel in duty bound to look up everybody's family tree, figures they'd get laid off if they found, after all their extensive researches and excavations, that a race which happens to be, for instance, in Burma today was always in 

Burma. According to science no such simple and ob-

WORLD BEATERS 

vious solution to a baffling situation could be true. 

When I goes on like this Theo seems to find his greatest joy in life. But I'm serious. Anyway, them 13,- 

000,000 Buddhists-or Burmese-are in Burma today, try and disprove that, Mr. Wells. There's one for them to work on, eh? These natives are broad faced, with heavy black hair. Their complexions is like a sun-tanned maiden's on Miami beach and their bright sparkling eyes makes them look alive. For the first time since leaving home here's a race where the men are good but the women are better. They seem to take the lead in business as well as the home. 

Three cheers for the Burmese women. As we drives through the wide streets of Rangoon, dodging automobiles and street cars, the public buildings, colleges, churches, museums, parks, hospitals, public libraries, garages and theatres makes me forget I'm abroad. 

This is a town, folks, a real town.

There are several places which we thoroughly enjoy, like a drive 'round Dalhousie Park, with its onetime royal lakes and richly gilded royal barges. The 

Kemmendine monastery is interesting-if you care for that sort of thing-but the big attraction at Rangoon is the Shwe Dagon pagoda, the mecca of the Buddhists of the world. They actually claim to have the old chap's carcass here, or, at least, a piece of him. One monk tells us they've got the whole works and another one tells us that they've only got one hair out of Buddha's head, and that's in a golden casket. Now, suit yourself; they've got anywhere from a hair to a whole cadaver, but if it's only a hair I'm glad they ain't got all of him or we'd never get out of Burma if we stayed to see what they built in honor of a whole anatomy when they built so much in memory of a hair. Folks, this Shwe Dagon pagoda is only 700 by 900 feet at the base and runs up to a peak 370 feet high. Is that a pagoda, or is it? The whole shaft is covered with

WORLD BEATERS 

129 gold and the dome rates nothing less that 15,000 gold and silver bells, tinkling in the breeze. Also, decorating this umbrella-shaped top is 3664 rubies, 541 emeralds and 433 diamonds. No, I ain't in a nightmare, them things are all there and the figures is correct. The pagoda was built about 2500 years ago and them gems have been donated by faithful rich Buddhists, just before or just after they took off for heaven-like most Christians, they held on to them as long as possible. I turn to Theo and says, "Theo, this thing is too big and too gaudy to be called beautiful," and stumbling over the carcass of a longdead dog, I continued, "and the idea of having to go through all this barnyard filth in our bare feet is too much. I wouldn't do it again for a bit less than the decorations on the dome and if they held out one bell on me I'd figure I'd got the worst of it." "But, Al," Theo attempts to correct me, "you're altogether too material. You only see the huge bulk of the pagoda and the debris from the animals and birds on the compound. You fail to see the wonderful faith that inspired it all. You lose sight of the genius that conceived and created it. You're completely blind to the religious generosity that brought all these priceless ornaments here. And you don't appear to realize the beauty in the character of folk who can so love wild life as to even permit them to usurp the sanctity of their shrine. Why, Al, it all makes a beautiful whole. Can't you see?" "Yes," I admits, "and I can smell, too. Let's get out of this." So, after we've seen the main pagoda and most of the 2500 Buddhas in the vicinity, we tips the first dozen beggars to get to us and escapes. What an experience! We rides back to town in our bare feet, having to scrub up before we can dress up.

As we sails away from Rangoon the next noon, we looks back from the river at the city, and there, re-

WORLD BEATERS 

flecting the rays of the noonday sun, towers the golden shaft of the Shwe Dagon pagoda and Theo says, “Al, there's the largest gem in the world."

As we traipses across the Bay of Bengal towards India Theo says, "Al, I had always wanted to visit the Orient and I've enjoyed every minute we've spent so far, among these strange and peculiar peoples, but the big treat should be ahead of us. India has ever been a land of mystery in my mind, filled with romance and adventure, it's all before us. Tomorrow we arrive in Calcutta, to be absorbed in her cosmopolitan life." 

No sooner said than done. Here we are winding our way eighty miles up the Hooghly River-that's se crooked I tells Theo our boat must have hinges in the middle to get 'round the bends—and all 'round us is Calcutta, the second largest city in the British Empire, thanks to the million and a half who live here. As we drives to the Grand Hotel I sees where my education has been sadly neglected. First, I thinks how much one loses by being as dumb as me and then

I thinks how much more kick I must be getting out of seeing this great modern city, where I expected to see a group of native huts, than Theo, who likely learned at Yale everything but the name of the streets.

Having got settled in our rooms at the Grand, Theo says, "Now, Alfred, we have some studying to do before going any place in India. We are face to face with a problem here which we have hardly recognized in any other part of the world. While I am a stranger here, too, I do know that a trip through

India would be almost useless without first acquainting ourselves with some knowledge of the peoples; their customs and religions, especially the latter. If 

I recall anything of my studies of India it is, that to know the countless beliefs of India is to know the native. So, since we are to spend several weeks in the

WORLD BEATERS 

131 country there is no need to hurry. I have brought along a few books by noted authorities on this and other subjects," and unpacking his bag and handing me half a library, he finishes, "let us brush up a little on these points which will enable us to really get something out of our tour of India."

So we stretches ourselves out on the bed and gets lost in the history of India. I don't know what Theo's reading but I'm reading about the religions of the country, and folks, it's a mess if ever there was one. 

I disturbs Theo to say, "Man, if I got to learn this Hindoo hooie in order to get anything out of a trip through India, then you'd better buy a third-class ticket for me and lose as little as possible. If I tries to crowd all this caste stuff in my head I won't get out of India with even my mind, let alone anything really worth while. I'll bet if the guy who wrote this book is still alive he's in a nut-house right now, cutting out paper dolls. Listen to this and if you can show me where there's even an acquaintance, let alone a relation, between it and sense, I'll eat the book, which is the only way I'll ever get its contents into me. Listen, it says, "The origin of Hinduism is lost in antiquity.' Now there's something we can do when we get to antiquity, Theo, who can try to find that origin they lost in that country. This bird goes on, but the caste system would appear to have had its beginning when the Aryans swarmed down upon Northern India and subjugated the natives; for their tribesmen prayed that the noble Aryans would defeat the black-skin; which is the drawing of the first 'color line' and the translation of 'color' from the Sanskrit is 'caste.' Then he goes into three pages here with the names of the different castes and their sub-divisions, and I'm betting the goof who thought these names up couldn't pronounce them himself. It seems there's a caste for every kind of a job a man could have and if you're a certain caste

WORLD BEATERS 

and unfortunate enough to be married and bad luck boards with you 'til you have a family, there ain't nothing else your boys can be but what you are and, therefore, they're in that certain caste, whether they like it or not. As this bird says, 'If an Edison were born to a Mehter, his inventive genius could never find a better medium of expression than the sweeper's tools, for the Mehter caste are sweepers. Or should a Lincoln gladden the home of a Syce he could never emancipate his people from the equipment of the groom, for the Syce are grooms by caste.' It also says in here that you break caste by marriage with a member of another caste and that makes the pair of them "outcastes." Another cute name they got for "outcastes" is "untouchables."

When I got the headache over this hooie I was just getting through the menagerie of animals which are sacred to the Hindu and the only reason I kept on reading was to try and find something that wasn't holy; cows, monkeys, snakes, pigeons, or what have youthey're all sacred to a Hindu and he wouldn't harm one if it curled up on his evening paper. The only sense I can get out of it-and I don't care if I flunk in that is that there's four or five main castes like the Brahmins, who are priests; Kahatriya, who are the warriors; Waisha, the artisans and Sudra, the laborers. The untouchables don't rate as any caste.

Now this system would be almost sensible if it stopped there; but no, each branch is cut up into countless slivers and each of them have a system all their own.

And, pitching the book at Theo's bag, I says, "Theo, let's go out and look these babies over and leave their religions to 'Allah'. That's another word I learned in that book. It's got something to do with another bunch of 'collection-lifters' here called Mohammedans.

It also mentioned something more about Buddhists in India. Boy, if you can't satisfy your longing for relig-

WORLD BEATERS 

133 59 ion in India it's too bad because that's the one thing the country went out of its way to do-have every kind of religion anybody could ever think of. I tells you, Theo, it's a mess." "Is it really as bad as all that, Al," Theo laughs. "That's only the slightest kind of an idea-in fact that idea just came off one of them eighteen-day diets-if I tried to give you a good idea of how bad this thing is I'd go nuts, no foolin'." "Well, I'll trade you books," says Theo, "here, brush up on that." And he tosses me a copy of somebody's "History of India." "Ain't you taking this thing a little too far, Theo?" I wants to know, "Are you forgetting it's K and B that's in school and not us?

But if you insist on educating me I'm declaring a recess right now. If I starts in reading about generals and second lieutenants right on top of Mohammet and Buddha I won't know whether Brahma, Siva, or 

Vishnu played the leads in Hinduism or the latest 

Hollywood release." So after winning my point, we puts our slates and books in the school-bag and calls it four o'clock, and, by a strange coincidence, it is four o'clock. 

So with the hottest part of the day gone we hails a cab and tells the Sikh to let us see Calcutta for a couple hours. And he does. 

And he does. And folks, if any of you shared my idea of Calcutta as being a conglomeration of Indian huts sheltering a million or so natives, then you can imagine my surprise when I'm shown over two dozen colleges and an equal number of 

Christian churches and six European hotels; also more than a dozen theatres. Think of that, or, rather, those. Naturally, being the biggest city in the British

Empire, next to London, it is, to all appearances, a great western city. Across from our hotel is a large open space two miles long and a mile wide called the Maidan. It is just one great playground, with the different fields for sports divided by beautiful drives

WORLD BEATERS 

lined with impressive monuments and statues of men who have done something worth while for India. And in the middle of this park is the world-famous Victoria Memorial Hall. It rates the finest piece of architecture in the world, next to some tomb in central India that was built centuries ago. But compared with modern structures it is second to no building any place. 

It's of white marble and it took fifteen years to build and cost about $75,000,000-which wouldn't be much for a prize fight in America but is plenty for a building. This hall is a memorial to Queen Victoria and a kind of monument to India from England. There's a monument to the Queen in front of the main entrance and just inside the main hall is a grand statue of her. The place houses articles that the Queen used and pictures of her time. The dome of the hall is 

200 feet high and outside are the most gorgeous beds of flowers and an artificial lake, so that when we're approaching we gets a perfect reflection, like Lincoln's Memorial in Washington. Theo just stands off and gazes at it with his mouth open. At the far end of the Maidan is the famous race-track where the Calcutta Sweepstakes is run. This is the only thing in

Calcutta I did know about-only I didn't know it was in India. This sure looks like home to me and though it ain't the right season for races I can just picture them ponies tearing up the turf—and then tearing up my bookmaker's slip when the report comes in that my horse didn't. But I care for the thrill of it all even if I do bet on a hay-burner that starts at five to one and don't get in 'til 3:30. Then we goes over through

Fort William on the edge of the Maidan. It's nearly two hundred years old and covers a couple square miles and is surrounded by a moat which can be filled with water from the river while the enemy's loading their sling-shots or painting their blades with venom. 

It's modern though; they've got a school, a church and

WORLD BEATERS 

135 all them things, Theo says is necessary to advancement; the two institutions that had so little to do with making me what I am today. I could almost claim to be a self-made man, and I don't suppose heaven would mind being relieved of the blame. 

That's the most we've seen in two hours since leaving home-not counting any two hours we've been with the girls-and the fresh air has worked up an appetite that's tough on the hotel's calories. After dinner we strolls across the Maidan in the cool, refreshing air and then sees John Gilbert and Greta Garbo go into a huddle for a couple hours. I turns to Theo when we're half through this crime and says, "I hope the natives don't think this 'Flesh and the Devil' thing is America." And, for once, he agrees with me.

The next day we continues our sight-seeing of Calcutta, only we does this part on the uppers. We walks through the Maidan to Dalhousie Square and watches the handsome Parawallah (short for "cop") handle traffic on Calcutta's busiest corner. It is busy too, and he does handle it. Across on one corner is the beautiful white post-office with its Corinthian columns and lofty dome. It's on the sight of the old fort, where, one hot June night in 1756, Seraj-ud-Dowlah attacked and conquered the British and imprisoned the 146 survivors in the Garrison Prison, a hole 18 by 14 feet. In the morning only 23 come out alivethe only advantage of dying in a place like that is. that no matter where you go from there it's an improvement. Today, there is a railing round that "Black

Hole of Calcutta" and on the wall is a tablet where I got all this information. As we hoof it about town, seeing the Government House, another great white edifice with a fine dome; the old Mission Church, built in 1772, the oldest Protestant church in town; through Clive Street, Calcutta's busiest thoroughfare and over Howrah Bridge, the most famous floating

WORLD BEATERS 

bridge in the world, we gets some wonderful close-ups of the natives. Though we don't see the whole million and a half that's in Calcutta I figure, roughly, that we must of seen the two-thirds that are Hindus, at least. 

Without a keener knowledge of this caste thing than even Brahma could hope to have, you'd never know which Hindu contributed to what temple, or when he went into a huddle with Vishnu what it was all about. 

If I don't get on another subject immediately I'll be frothing at the mouth. But just take this tip from me—no matter what Theo says,-don't try to figure out this Hinduism in detail or you'll go screwy. Just assume that two out of every three you see are Hindus and the other one is a Mohammedan. That'll make it a little hard to place a Buddhist or a Jain-but there's so few of them that it ain't worth getting balled up any more to know them when you do pass oneand anyway, that authority I was reading said they was both offshoots of Hinduism-so leave it at that; the first two are Hindus and the next one is a Mohammedan, then the next two are Hindus again, and so on. The only exception to this rule being when you see three or more at once. In this case, suit yourself. 

Call them all Hindus, or all Mohammedans, or all 

Buddhists, or all Jains. (And that word "Jain" means a sect not a sex.) The necessity for this mass classification is because you'd no more find them mixed than you'd find Democrats and Republicans mixed. And, now that I've simplified this problem, I've made travel through India much more agreeable, although I've done an irreparable damage to the aspirin business because this practically removes even the possibility of a headache.

I gets a great kick out of the side-walk barber who carries his works with him and when a victim is sighted he drops his box, sharpens his tools and is, at once, established in business-just like that. When his fel-

137 99 low-countryman is scalped he packs up and shuffles along until he finds another communist. Another that catches my eye is the one-horse shopkeeper-only he ain't got a horse, he pulls his counterlike cart himself and on display is every kind of trinket you can think of and a few things that's actually useful. Theo says, "Isn't that Boxwalla, with his business on wheels, an interesting type, Al?" "Yes, he's all of that, Theo,"

Boxwalla, hails us and we starts fingering his wares. 

I holds up a little ivory temple, figuring it would make a nice souvenir of India, and asks the price. "Ten rupees," he says fast, so it don't sound like much.

But it's $3.60 no matter how fast he says it and I lays it down again. "I make it eight rupees to American,' he grins at me, and seeing he's open for that kind of business I goes him several better and offers him four. 

He throws up his hands and laughs, "Four rupees for solid ivory temple? It cost me six rupees, eight anas. 

You take it for seven rupees.' "Four," I says, and starts to walk away. "Take it," he calls, "I lose two rupees, eight anas, but I need money to eat." So I buys it for four rupees and, as we walks down the street admiring the thing, commenting on what a Jew

I turned out to be, I turns the thing upside down and on the bottom is stamped "Made in Germany," and up in the corner in fine figures it says, "Price, two rupees." Theo almost has twins the way he laughs, "So you're the chap who took advantage of the poor 

Indian when he was hard up and needed food?" he kids me. And I reminds him of the Jap who gives him a ten cent rickishaw ride for $4.00 in Kobe. "When we gets back to New York, Theo," I says, "they should have Texas Guinan on the dock to greet

us-'Hello, Sucker'." But Theo claims it's all part of our education. But ain't it funny? We gets gypped every time we turns around in America-and like itbut if they take us for the suckers we are in another country, we want to lick somebody. 

The next day we takes a cab 'round to a few of 

Calcutta's 250 factories just to see what kind of plants they are to employ 500,000 hands. From where I sits in the taxi, jute looks like the big business of the town and the driver says I'm right, that it's fifty-five per cent of the exports of Indian merchandise from the city and, furthermore, that Calcutta ships twentyfive per cent of all the jute that's exported from India. 

Then there's cotton and paper mills, paint and oil factories, rice mills and concerns making practically every home necessity. It's plenty industrial, this Calcutta.

Then we drives out through their zoological gardens and casts an eye on their collection of birds, animals, and reptiles; but their artificial lakes, flower beds and beautiful trees looks better to me. Theo claims we got to see the Temple of Kali, so we do. It's supposed to be on the bed of the old Ganges, the sacred river, and, consequently, rates high with the Hindus. Tune in on this if you'd like an order of unadulterated bunk. 

The tale goes like this-Siva, one of the three gods of Hinduism, was married to Sati, a leading flapper of them days, and when she done her swan song Siva went kind of nuts and packed Sati's cadaver on his shoulder and started out on a world tour-they don't say whether he was on a floating university or not- Siva was so worked up that the folks figured that he'd at least destroy the world so Vishnu, another of the Hindu trio of gods, heaves a disc at Siva and made a bull's-eye. This hole-in-one shot that Vishnu made broke Sati's frame into fifty-one pieces, which scattered all over the world-including Philadelphia-and

WORLD BEATERS 

139 every spot where a piece fell became holy. Her little toe, so they claim, fell in Calcutta and this Temple of Kali is built on that spot. And the Hindu believes that too, but folks, no matter how you serve it—it's boloney.

The worship here consists of the slaughter of so many goats a day. While we're here they lay over a dozen goats on the block and the priest lops off their heads with one stroke. The carcass is given to the poor but the heads are just left there and it's a hot sultry day and the flies around there are in swarms that shut out the sun. I starts to tell Theo what I thinks of it all but he stops me by a traffic cop's gesture of the hand and says, "Don't bother, Al. I think the same way but still we don't understand their beliefs so we aren't in a position to justly criticise them." 

Determined to get all this kind of sight-seeing over with, I tells Theo if he has any more slaughter houses in mind, let's go see them while the spirit's willing and the flesh ain't yet too weak. So he tells the driver to get us to the Jain Temples. I grits my teeth and submits. But, it ain't bad, honest. These Jains are what we'd call Nonconformists in America, or one might call them the Quakers of India. There's a lot about Hinduism that they feel the same way about as I do, and they've really built themselves a nifty little temple up here in the northern part of town and they've got ground around it that would be fit for the fairies to dance on. Flower beds, artificial lakes and beautiful foliage. Over in one corner of this charming garden is the Shish Mahal or Palace of Mirrors, with floors, walls, ceilings and furniture, all of glass. 

It's clever I claims and nobody but an artist could of laid out this whole Jain temple works. Theo claims. there's just one more thing he wishes to see and that's the Parsee Towers of Silence. So, not knowing from the name, what it's all about I consents to the trip.

WORLD BEATERS 

It turns out to be the most gruesome cemetery in the world-as far as I know or want to know. The Parsee is a small element in India that believe the old religion of Persia, which reveres fire, water and earth as holy, hence-refuse to burn or bury their dead. So they build these big white-washed towers, off in a section of town, away from the backfire explosions of Mack trucks, where silence reigns supreme. There's a flight of steps up to these towers that nobody but the white-robed Nasalars can mount, and in the middle of the tower there's three grooves, one for men, the middle one for women and the inner one for children. 

At least, we're told that's the layout of the tower, we're not allowed to look and when a Parsee checks out the Nasalars lay the body in the proper groove in the tower and the vultures tear the flesh from it and the bones fall down through the bottom and into a well. All around the top of the towers and in the compound is great flocks of vultures waiting for a 

Parsee funeral. I turns to the boy friend and says, "Theo, I'm all caught up in my morbid education, let's go back to the hotel.' 99 99 "No, For some reason I don't have an appetite at dinner time. I just picks over the food and passes it up. 

Theo eats like he won't see food again for a month. and he says, "What's the trouble, Al, ill?" 

Theo," I says, "just suffering from an acute attack of memory. I can't forget them goats, flies and vultures. After dinner, our last night in Calcutta, just to get a change of atmosphere we takes a walk through 

Chinatown. If it's possible this bit of China in India is even filthier than China proper. The narrow, dirty streets just ooze everything that's nauseating. But it's interesting just to know that humans can sink so low and still live or exist. The place is crammed with restaurants of every degree from vile to style. In a hole in the wall there you can get a full meal for two

And I says, "Boy, if the D. T.'s were half this bad I'd of give up drinking long before

The next day, just after lunch, we're down to the station all ready to give Calcutta the bye-bye and to say "hello" to Benares. When I sees the gang on the platform I gets weak with a flash back to Kobe. I decides then and there if it's going to be another Japanese railway ride through India, it ain't because I'll walk through India rather than jam myself into a car with these natives. I don't mind being crowded with 

Japs, but, I've exactly no intention of sharing the minimum square footage for one human with three nude 

Hindus, or practically nude. I registers my recent decision with Theo, and he says, "Why, I'm surprised at you, Al. Just because these people happen to be the product of a custom and tradition which you resent is not to say that they are not as good as you and I." "Tell it to the health department," I says, "but I don't go through India like a sardine. These folks may be even better in their way than I am in mine that wouldn't get them any halos-but I don't want to rub whiskers with them, and I ain't going to.'

can't believe it but we throws our bags in anyway and claims the seats. It seems the natives ride second and third class in India and a first-class ticket really means something in the country. We not only have a whole compartment to ourselves but a private shower, basin and toilet. I leans back in the soft-plush seats and claims we're sitting pretty.

After sixteen hours run through respectably good farm country and several towns we pulls into Benares at the ungodly hour of five in the morning. After getting caught up in my chambermaid duties, we de trains is that what we do, Theo?-or is it disembark -anyway, we gives the mail express the air. As much as I dislike to do it I must clear up the foggy situation I've opened by mentioning my chambermaid duties. Much to my sorrow India don't have pullman sleepers so if one expects to do any night traveling he must pack his own quilts and slips. Knowing we'll have use for them we possess ourselves of a couple of complete outfits in Calcutta and I'm appointed as a committee of one to see that they get made up in the evenings and packed in the mornings. When we're getting off at Benares Theo looks at me, with the two bed packs on my shoulders, and laughingly says, "Everything O.K., captain?" And turning red around the gills, I says, "Lay off the captain stuff. I may be looking after the bed-clothes but I don't have to take care of the vessels."

We was told in Calcutta, where we bought our tickets, that to properly see the towns through India it's necessary to engage a native guide, so, since it only costs three or four rupees a day to have one, Theo picks one out of the gang that swarms around us as we alight,-imagine, at five A.M.-and asks him what we should do first. This baby he picks is a Mohammedan and speaks good English-in fact they can't get licenses to act as guides unless they can speak English

143 and pass an examination to prove they know their stuff in the section where they're going to work-a very good idea, I'd say, this licensing a guide so just any dumb Hindu can't take your money and tell you the first thing that comes to his head-another mark for

Hindus in the Ganges?" And Theo covers up our ignorance by telling him that we had but he just wanted to make sure that that was where we was being took. The drive through town to the river didn't mean a thing to me because I know the same about
