French rarebit. Our steeds comes to a halt on the top of the bank and our guide starts in at once on his lecture. "Gentlemen, that's the sacred Ganges Riversacred to a Hindu, I mean-those thousands of people you see down there at the water's edge are Hindus at worship. These buildings up here on the banks are 

Hindu temples, built by wealthy Hindu princes, as a kind of homage to the gods of Hinduism. These temples are used mostly as houses of shelter for the millions of pilgrims that come to Benares annually to worship at the river. There are over two thousand such temples at Benares and the shrines and chapels which are built by wealthy Hindu merchants are almost countless throughout the city. But let us go down to the river and take a boat-ride along the water-front where a better view of the temples and pilgrims at worship may be had." And with this out

of him he starts down the steep bank for the Ganges. 

Following close behind, I comments to Theo on how well our guide seems to handle the situation and Theo says, "He's real good, Al, and if I can keep him for our entire stay in Benares, I will."

On reaching the river, Mohamet-our guide told us to call him Mohamet-takes everything in his own hands. Chattering in the native tongue he dickers for a boat for a few hours and we takes off on the sacred Ganges in a pocket edition of the Mayflower. This boat is about twenty feet long, eight feet wide and a full double decker; the engine and crew-two names for the one department-is below, and on top deck there's three comfortable wicker chairs in which we're seated and ready to see the show. Mohamet calls my attention to the absence of any rail when he sees me shoving my chair about for a better look. He gives some kind of orders to the galley slaves below and we glides silently out into mid-stream. Boy, that's one grand way to see this town. And for miles along the river-on one bank only-is nothing but temples, except the odd mosque, where an enterprising Mohammedan emperor named Aurangzeb had the leading temple of the town destroyed and this mosque erected on the site. The opposite bank of the river is as deserted as the streets of Edinburgh on tag day. There's not so much as a sign board over there and boy, what wouldn't Wrigley or the makers of Camel cigarettes give for that privilege. In a year's time it would be read by nearly two-thirds of the population of India because every Hindu that can ride, walk or crawl comes to Benares at least once a year; but from the looks of nine out of ten they couldn't make the first payment on the coughs that's in a carload of Old 

Golds, let alone buy a package of gum. Mohamet makes a gesture towards the bank-where the thousands are bathing and crossing themselves and offering

145 prayers to the river and the sun and what have weand says in a disgusted tone, "That's Hinduism." I drags him out and he's all wet for once-and a scramble of Hindus from below on to the top deck has all the earmarks of a mutiny. Theo tells him to apologize to these Hindus to avert any trouble, which Mohamet must of done in that noise he made to them because they go back to their oars and then Mohamet apologizes all round for the trouble he's caused and tells us that a Hindu won't take nothing from a Mohammedan and vice versawhatever that is. "It seems we just attract boat fights," I tells Theo, who is standing up in the sun trying to get the Ganges dried off his clothes. 

Another order from Mohamet and our boat noses in towards shore where wreathes of smoke is curling up into the temple windows above. As we pulls closer 

I realizes that it's something the Hindus are burning on poles, perhaps some kind of sacrifice, but, as usual, I'm wrong. "That, gentlemen," explains Mohamet, "is the burning ghat, where the Hindu is burned after death and his ashes scattered on the Ganges, thus assuring him peace in the next world and freedom from a rebirth. That is why Benares is so crowded with old and invalided Hindus. They come here to die so that they can be burned on the ghat and their ashes scattered on the river." Up the long flight of steps that

WORLD BEATERS 

leads from the burning ghat to the top of the bank is a solid line of Hindus, waiting, with their dead, for their turn on the ghat. It's a sad sight, folks, and we don't watch it long. But then, it's only a crude form of our crematoriums in America. The bad part is they throw these ashes on the river and downstream from the ghat there's thousands of pilgrims bathing and drinking the water. A busy day on the ghat should make the water almost like soup; but there's apparently no ill effects from it so bacteriologists will have to figure out the reason or get a new theory for the cause of typhoid fever, etc. I points out half-dressed guys squatted under big umbrellas and asks Mohamet who they are and how come they rate the cover and he tells us they are the Brahmin or priests of Hinduism and they are there to instruct the lower castes in their worship-at so much per head. These Brahmin, it seems, are the only Hindus clever enough to make a living out of their religion. "Who are them baldheaded ones," I asks next, and Mohamet tells us they're widows. It seems when a dish-washer in India loses her sandwich man she's all washed up with matrimony. They used to burn them on the funeral pyre of their husands he tells us-and he seems to be in favor of that—but the British ruled that sport out and now they just shingle her head-as a warning to sheiks to lay off-and her father-in-law keeps her as a servant. I thought it was bad enough to be married to one of them Hindus but it seems tougher still if he knocks off.

Our two hours is up so we puts into the shore and after Mohamet pays for the boat we gets back to the hotel and take on breakfast. Mohamet tells us that after breakfast we'll take in a few of them 2000 temples; so, knowing what's before me I punishes the best part of a week's work for any hen and the only decent part of a good-sized hog. With this pleasant part of

WORLD BEATERS 

147 the day's work over we climbs into this semi-tally-ho again and starts out to see some more of Siva's town. 

As we heads for the golden temple, Mohamet gives us the low-down on this Benares place, even going so far as telling its right name. It seems, according to our guide, that 'way back in the same place where the origin of Hinduism was lost, this town of Benares had its beginning so that solves one point, it was in Benares that that origin got mislaid—and that early date-for know you, antiquity was a long way back in the book- Benares was called Kashi, and it appears that Hinduism had quite a record run in this Kashi place; in fact it beat the run that "The Birth of a Nation" made on

Broadway by several full length centuries. But when 

Mr. Buddha brought his stock company to town and pitched his tent at Sarnath-a flag station about four miles north of Kashi-he had intended starting a realestate boom out there as a sub-division to Kashi, but 

Kashi didn't have any post-office yet, so Buddha had no way of advertising his lots. How could he hope to sell his lots if he couldn't tell his prospective suckers how many minutes' walk it was from the post-office?

So when this fell through, Buddha, being a good business man and not wanting to stray too far from his good intentions, give up the idea of real-estate and went to preaching. He must of been a patent medicine salesman at one time because he sure wowed them nickel-droppers that come to tune in. One afternoon,

Yasha, then king of Benares, having sent his wives out for a sunning with the harem eunuch, took a spin out to Sarnath to see this latest road show. Buddha must of been hitting on all eight that day because Yasha swallowed it all, hook, line and sinker, especially the line. What does Yasha do but go back home and claim that Hinduism is the bunk and that Buddhism is the berries. This starts the ball rolling and with a publicity agent like Yasha Buddhism soon became the

WORLD BEATERS 

leading business of India. But, in the eighth century, A.D.-thirteen centuries after Buddha's sale, unlucky thirteen-a chap by the nickname of Sankaracharaya -they called him that because his right name was too long and too hard to say-comes to Benares and establishes himself at Sarnath and revives Hinduism in all its glory. He made a mess out of most of Buddha's temples and built his own out of the bricks he saved. So, that's that. But that ain't all. Not by thousands of temples it ain't. After this baby with the trick monnicker had got Hinduism back on a paying basis what should happen but another gang of religious fanatics come through the country demolishing temples and building mosques. They was Mohammedans. And when our guide tells us about this part of 

India's history his face lights up and you'd think he'd done the whole thing single handed. But that gives you a rough idea-very rough-of the tough times this Benares has had staying on the map. Personally, I wouldn't get my hands dirty for the whole town but that ain't saying it's no good. I mean it's completely native and I wouldn't give a goat's head for any of them native places.

By now we're at the Golden Temple, the holiest spot in Benares, which makes it one of the holiest spots in India. We parks the spavins close to the curb which allows traffic to pass, if they're careful drivers, and we starts up an alley for this temple. If there's one beggar in this alley there's 3000, and that ain't counting the priests, and most of them is blind, lame or lepers. As we gets closer the beggars gets thicker and lamer and, at last, we're at the temple. It seems a princess of Indore had it done and it's quite the temple. Its domes, that reach up into the fresh air above the squalor of the mob, like they was trying to keep from being suffocated, is all covered with gold-that's how come the name "Golden Temple," applied by

WORLD BEATERS 

149 travelers, like us. Its real name is the Shrine of Visweswara. Where we're walking there's about two inches of mud and I don't know what else. It seems the pilgrims bring buckets of water from the Ganges and slop it all over the temple and, boy, what a mess it makes. "Some of these pilgrims," Mohamet says, "have carried that water all the way from the source of the Ganges, 'way up in the snow-clad Himalayas."

I can't stand this, so I sees a balcony on a house across the alley which would let us see the whole works, if we could get up there, and I suggests it to Mohamet and in no time he has arranged for us to mount the balcony. I suppose if this temple had some air; for instance, was in the middle of the Maidan in Calcutta, it would really look like a temple, but standing in mud up to its sills and fenced in by dirty buildings on all sides and then all them half-dressed filthy beggars, it's just something to forget, that's all. I'm in favor of calling Benares "done," but Theo says "No," and Mohamet with an eye to business, tells us what a shame it would be, after coming so far, if we only seen so little. Temple. It seems this name of Monkey Temple was put on it by travelers from the numerous monkeys that swarm about it, but the proper name is Durga Temple and the book says this about it. "Away from the crowded haunts of pilgrims, in a fairy garden where squirrels play, unmolested, among the flowers, and brilliantly plumaged birds strike a gay note in the symphony of color amid the green of the trees, stands the gorgeous Temple of Durga." Applesauce, folks, pure applesauce. True, it is slightly removed from the crowded part of the town but that "fairy garden, symphony of color and gorgeous temple" hoopla is just an opium fiend's dream. In the first place I trips over a dung-heap on my way into the main entrance, and if you go there ten years from now you'll likely fall over the same pile-unless you see it and go round-because cow-dung is kind of sacred in India and they just leave it there. Sometimes they gather it up in baskets and plaster it on the walls of the houses to bake in the sun-I don't know whether they use it for fuel then or as an altar-anyway, a common sight through India is to see the natives-usually the women-out with a basket, gathering up this manure with their hands. That's my first impression of this "Durga the 

Gorgeous." Next, I fails to see the beautiful flowers with squirrels in them-though I don't know why sensible squirrels don't go there, there's lots of nuts-and the monkeys that swarm the place are three or four half-starved monkeys that drop down on the walls, occasionally, from the trees. But that's not worth a trip

WORLD BEATERS 

151 out of your way to see because most of the railroad stations in them small towns in India has more monkeys around them than passengers. After an eyeful of the gilded spire, the altar, with the goddess Durga on deck and the bull that stands by the door, we leaves this temple as we found it, a false alarm. I tells Mohamet if he takes us to another temple I'll make widows out of his seven wives and Theo reassures him with a grin and suggests we be shown the Sanskrit College. Not knowing this fellow Sanskrit that the school was named after I waits to learn all about the place. It seems I was wrong again; that Sanskrit was one of the first, if not the first, language ever broadcasted and this college is the leading institution selling that kind of chatter. Mohamet tells us this place is considered the seat of Hindu culture and learning and that anyone with a degree from this schoolhouse is rated as being up on his Eastern logic, grammar and philosophy. He also informs us-at least it's news to me, though Theo likely knew it-that this Sanskrit Colleg is world famous for its library, known as the "Place of Knowledge." It's one place in India where folks of any race, color or creed-including castescan go and use without charge.

Mohamet then has us see the Benares Hindu University, the only Hindu university in the world, and it really does look like a university too, with its several colleges crescent-shaped on the park-like grounds and the boarding houses behind. "What else have you to offer," I asks Mohamet, "besides these retail knowledge shops and the other 2000 temples?" "Silk factories," he claims. It's something different so I'm all for going. On our way to the silk factory Mohamet points out several other schools, mentioning that over 20,000 students are taught regularly in Benares, absolutely free; and that many of the schools which are built and maintained by rajahs and rich Hindu merchants have

WORLD BEATERS 

restaurants in connection where the students are also fed free. "Not bad," I tells Theo. "Yes," Theo adds, "and you're the chap that thought everything in India was wrong." Before we can get any farther in the argument, Mohamet claims we're at the factory and we enters a small factory-I think it was a house until the health department condemned it-and here's four or five Hindu youths pumping old rattle-trap weaving machines—at least as old as Buddha-and working the shuttle by hand. But, boy, they do turn out some great work. Don't think they don't. We maybe got some clever folks at home but I doubt if you could pick many out of Times Square who could even pump this this thing and toss the shuttle let alone know when and where. I watches a kid embroidering a temple in silver and gold on a black background of silk. He holds all three materials in balls in his lap and works them in from memory. I wish I could tell you what a piece of work this is. There's nothing on display on Fifth 

Avenue that beats it. It's grand, that's all. And Mohamet tells me the kid gets two rupees a day-that's seventy-two cents-and he's well paid, they figure. 

We takes in some more of these dumps where they're carving wood-and can they carve wood-it's good to me to see somebody in India can do something worth while. Others are making articles from brass, copper and silver-all of them clever. But then, why not?

These workmen are the sons of fathers who done the same thing; and their fathers' fathers' grandfathers done it before them because the caste system make them follow the business of their dads, and with it born in them that way they couldn't help but be clever at it. It's a wonder a wood-carver's son ain't born with a jack-knife in one hand and a block of wood in the other. It seems weeks since we left our hotel and I'm starving so we hikes back to the Chauk, Benares business centre-where our hotel is and we

WORLD BEATERS 

153 puts in for the rest of the day and night. Theo pays 

Mohamet off and tells him he can go with us tomorrow if he cares. I'll bet Mohamet slept in the lobby all night so he wouldn't miss us.

The next morning we strolls through the Chauk, Mohamet along, and visits some of the chief bazaars. 

Many of the best textile and brass establishments is in this section too. On this stroll we actually sees some modern buildings-while much of Benares is semi-modern this part is quite so-like the Carmichael library, police station and clock tower. Theo buys a few trinkets for souvenirs and when the storekeeper states the price I tells Theo to offer him quarter that and he don't ever pay half what the robber first asks. Theo buys some perfume that he claims is the very "spirit of the East, imprisoned in a phial." I don't smell it because if it's anything like the perfumes of the East I've been smelling, I'd pass out. A little tired from our shopping tour Mohamet commandeers a pony express for us and Theo suggests we just take a lazy ride through the beautiful parts of Benares. "Be yourself, Theo,' 

I tells him, "why don't you ask Mohamet to show you the pearly gates-as if Benares had any beauty spots." 

But Mohamet don't look worried so I lets it go and it's only a matter of minutes 'til he has us in a little bit of paradise—at least, it seems that good after where we've been. I sure enjoys the ride but on the way back 

Mohamet spoils it all by stopping at another temple he wants us to see. It's the Shrine of Dandapani and it contains the famous Kal Kup or Well of Fate. According to Mohamet, anyone curious enough to want to know what's in store for them, comes to this well at noon. Just at high-noon, the Brahmin, who has the concession for that day, opens a funny kind of slit and lets a ray of sunlight in. The curious one then looks down the well and if he don't see his reflection in the 99

WORLD BEATERS 

water he's booked for a berth with St. Peter within six months. Ho-Hum. What bunk.

A few more tours of this kind and I'm so caught up with Benares that I'm running over and I suggests we get some more mileage out of our railroad tickets.

Theo must have plenty of it too, for he don't object and so that evening I gets back to my chambermaid duties in the Bombay express and we're off for Agra, leaving Benares, India's oldest city, to keep watch over her 200,000 natives and her thousands of shrines and temples 'til we return-which will be when the sacred Ganges empties into my vest pocket. 

We don't pull out of Benares 'til a little after midnight and we're not due at Agra until 11:30 in the morning. For some reason I can't sleep and at six o'clock I dresses and sees some of India from a pullman. I think I'm up early but I must of slept in compared with some of the farmers in this section. I can't tell what they're doing because the crop looks like barley to me and I know they don't cultivate or weed that stuff in America-maybe they're just coming through the fields on their way home from a dance at a neighbor's-but then I discovers they're irrigating the fields and, I suppose, want to get the work done before it gets so hot the sun dries it up before it hits the barley. Scattered through the fields I sees what looks like wells-they are wells-and with the help of a cow or horse a native is drawing buckets of water and pouring it in a trough which runs off into the fields where other natives guide its course about the crops. We tears through miles of stone-cropped barren country, only knee-high bushes and clumps of grass, where herds of goats roam about by the thousands. Boy, I thinks, how easy it ought to be to get a

Hindu's goat-the country's lousy with them. Every few miles we dash through a cluster of clay huts with thatched roofs, and the usual number of temples and

155 

I'm getting ready for another horrible excursion through the temples of Agra-of course there's one consolation, when you get fed up with temples you can always turn to shrines-help, help.

At last we actually reaches Agra and I gets the beds done up while Theo slugs the baggage. Again we're mobbed by guides out of work, before we set foot on the platform, and Theo picks out a likely looking recruit and we're off for a hotel and lunch. 

Folks, I was all wrong about India. Me and Theo tarried for several days in this Agra place and I'm all set to hand somebody or anybody in India the plush chopsticks for grand buildings, and it ain't because they give us a new dish in buildings either-though it was a treat to see tombs instead of temples-but I still think it must of all been a dream. I can't believeeven after seeing them-that there's any such buildings in the world. And it ain't because I'm reverential just because they're tombs-personally, I don't care if a building houses your aunt's cadaver or the headquarters of the international alliance of crap-shooters, if it's a clever piece of bricklaying I'm for it and Agra can stun your imagination most any place you care to look. If I had another pail of ink and a few more armloads of paper I'd ruin the whole works just raving about Agra, but I don't have them materials, so it's your loss. But why not relax a few minutes and listen to what happened to us in this town? 

It was plenty.

159 prodigal son who went into a far country and wasted his substance on the wrong horse; or maybe Theo has found one of our ex-presidents in business for himself, but when I gets there and turns the optics on this freak of nature, who is it but the athlete that turned cartwheels and handsprings for us about an hour ago.

I kids K asking her how she'd like to be a widow in India and get one of them boyish bobs, but I don't get nowheres.

There ain't nobody gets ahead of K, nor kids her without getting a couple goats in return. She says, “I wouldn't be anything else in India. Of course I can escape matrimony in America but if I were over here where the parents put the ball and chain on you before you're old enough or strong enough to object, believe me I'd be a widow just as quick as I could find something to put in that Hindu's coffee." WORLD BEATERS 

and then she throws her laugh into high gear and B follows suit. I'm embarrassed myself, and she goes on, "but if a man kept me penned up like these highcaste Hindus keep their women behind closed doors, I'd get out-and, since it appears you can't get out unless you're a widow-then I suppose I'd have to be one of those." And she says it like she meant it and like it was wrong not to. I'm beginning to appreciate what Theo and B done for me the night they crashed our private party in the Imperial Hotel in Tokyo-I was on the edge of asking this woman to be my wife. 

Pardon me, folks, while I shudder. B appears to feel about the same way as K and claims that a woman's lot in India is terrible in any instance. She says, "Marriages are contracted by parents at ages of eight and ten when neither girl nor boy have ever met-not even seen each other-and if the husband is of high caste she's a prisoner the rest of her life and if he's of low caste she's a slave, allowed out only to do the work of a man. Much better she be a widow than a human incubator to raise large numbers of sons to become domestic despots, and daughters to re-live the hell she knows." For the first time since this subject opened, Theo speaks and he addressed himself to me, thusly, "Al, we've been so absorbed in material shrines that we have been blind to the shrine of womanhood. What the girls say is true. Woman's place in the world is paramount, but India hasn't learned that. They need a Lincoln over here, someone who will emancipate womanhood." And turning to the girls, who are looking at him like he was Lincoln, he continues, "Isn't it depressing to see the chaos wrought by false beliefs, to realize the pathos of such superstition?" I might as well be reading last year's almanac for all this conversation means to me. And to think I started it all by innocently kidding K. Well, the rest of them seems to be enjoying it and I learned one thing years ago, to

Taj Mahal is a tomb, built by one of the emperors of India-I think his name was Shahjahan-to his favorite wife, Mumtaz-i-Mahal. If I was a graduate from all the colleges in the world, and still had an ounce of common sense, I'd know better than to try and describe this place, but being as how they had to burn the school down to get me out of the third grade, I'm too dumb to know the danger of attempting such a thing. So here goes, and, of course, I'm not as dumb as I make out-I've really got my bachelor's degree and as long as I stay single I guess I can claim to have my master's. 

But, folks, when we stand there at the gate about a thousand feet back from the tomb, with its perfect reflection in the long pool of silent water that runs all the way between us, and the two long rows of cyprus trees on each side of the pool-tall, dark and stately like mourners in a great procession-it all looks like the sepulchre it is. The garden looks square and maybe ten acres in extent. It's divided down the centre by this marble pool with its numerous fountains, and on both sides of that again is red sandstone paths, cutting the garden into sixteen equally divided sections, each with its brilliantly colored and highly scented flowers. 

Across the front and along both sides of this garden is a great wall with an entrance on each side and in front. These entrances are as grand as I had imagined the Taj would be. At the end of the garden or

163 just behind the Taj, runs the beautiful Jumna River. 

Now there's the surroundings as they look to me from the gate and right at the end of this bit of paradise guarded by four great minarets or prayer towers is the final resting place of the "pride of the palace" in all its silent grandeur.

They're about sixty-five feet away from the Taj and they're built entirely of white marble. you what it all looks like but it can't be done, not by me. At last we descends to the platform and starts to really see the Taj itself. In the first place it's solid white marble, except for the inscriptions from the

Koran worked into it with black slate, like needle work. The whole thing is about 185 feet square with the corners cut off, making it really octagonal. Eventually we enter the Taj. Its interior is but one chamber shaped like the outside, and in the centre is an octagonal marble screen carved out into floral designs and more passages from the Koran. As

We steps slowly, reverently, through the archway of the screen over to the cenotaphs which is solid blocks of white marble, inscribed with more of the Koran. It's all something to just see and then go away and think about. If we stayed here for months it couldn't lose its spell. So we leave and walk around to the river side and there, across the Jumna, is the foundation of another great sepulchre that Shahjahan had started for himself and the guide says it was intended that a marble bridge should connect the two tombs. Boy, wouldn't that of been a sight? But I ain't sorry he didn't get it done. I couldn't stand a sight twice as good as this-I don't seem to gather all this one in even-but I suppose, as time goes by, it will gradually sink in and I'll realize what a gorgeous thing it all is.

The next morning when the girls come down for breakfast in their hotel who should be waiting in the lobby for them but Theodore Kingsley and Alfred 

Tool-world travelers from New York City. We

WORLD BEATERS 

167 think we might as well get the day started right because we never know when we'll run into a gang of temples and the only thing that gets you through that is sweet memories. So we has breakfast together and leaves at once for the fort-and what a fort! Of course Mohamet is along, so all's well; and he takes special pride in reminding us that while the capital of India has been here and there all over the country during its history, India has one city that not only all India recognizes as supreme but all the world; that city is Agra and it gained that reputation under the 

Mogul Dynasty-"and, remember," Mohamet stresses the point, "the Moguls were Mohammedans not Hindus." 

Well, personally, I'm an Episcopalian by birth 

-Methodist by Baptism-and Salvation Armyist by subscription, so I'm the last guy on earth to ridicule a Hindu or applaud a Mohammedan. I neither know nor care what the difference is but I'm in favor of anybody that can, and does build a place like this Agra town. And the last thing I'll ever ask him is, "Who's your God?" But Mohamet is pleased about it anyway and I must say he has a reason for his pride because Agra is nothing if it ain't a town of beautiful buildings-what if they are tombs and forts and mosques-a building has to be something and if a fellow's only a tourist anyway why should he care what they use them for? If they ain't paying propositions he don't lose any dividends. According to Mohamet and he should know, he passed his examinations and got a license-this fort, which took eight years to get built, was the work of Akbar, known as the great Mogul and also as the master builder. He started it in 1565-and if you've got a pencil handy you can figure out for yourself when it was finished. 

It's a fort alright, but Mohamet claims that when Akbar had it built he held the championship in the first class emperors' division and, consequently had an

WORLD BEATERS 

eye more to a home than a fort. Hence, this great crescent-shaped citadel on the bank of the Jumna River is more of a fortified palace than just a mere fort. But don't think that this Akbar baby was foolish enough not to have it well protected. He knew from past experiences in the lives of emperors-if he knew anything-that his job was only good as long as he could hold it; and holding it meant keeping every other aspirant at least a bayonet's length from the door. Proof of this is the presence of an outer wall forty feet high and a mile and a half around, and outside of that is a cute little ditch only thirty feet wide and thirty-five deep. A few yards inside this outer wall is another wall eighty feet high. I ask you, did Akbar believe in protection, or did he? Can you imagine the embarrassment of an enemy, who, under cover of darkness crept up to the fort, succeeded in swimming the moat, then in some unknown way scaled that forty foot wall and jumped over, thinking that they were, at last, in the fort? Boy, what a human rat-trap Mr. Akbar got himself behind. And them walls are of red sand stone. We crosses on one of the four drawbridges over the moat and enters the fort. Holy mackerel—it's a city in white and gold. 

White marble palaces, pearl mosques, gold throne rooms, marble courts, baths, pavilions, fountains, walks, all of pure white marble. If I ain't in a nightmare all them things I'm telling you about is there inside the wall. Mohamet tries to explain what each thing is but I don't follow him-I don't want tothere's too much in here for a detailed description. 

I just takes in what I can in one of them panoramic looks, and I sits on the ledge of what the guide claims is the Jasmine Tower. I looks out over the Jumna and there, glittering like a gem in the bright sunlight, is the Taj Mahal. I starts to see the picture Theo imagined when we was leaving the Taj the day before,

WORLD BEATERS 

169 when Mohamet says, "This is the room that was occupied by Mumtaz-i-Mahal while her husband was emperor in Agra; and this is the room in which Shahjahan died." Nobody speaks. We all looks across the 

Jumna at the Taj Mahal and I actually got a lump in my throat and I feels my fingers itching to get at 

Aurangzeb. We spends the entire morning in this fort and then we only sees a fraction of it. You could live in it for a year and see something new and different every minute.

Theo says as we're leaving, "India has produced some great men-for nothing but masters could produce such works-but the surprising thing is the history of India has been one conquest after another, with each new victor destroying the works of the vanquished and rearing new wonders from the debris of the old-none of them were seemingly capable of bringing to an end this destructive program and unifying the country on a basis of peace and progress-none until England came. Then the attacks upon India from foreign sources ceased. Then the plundering of one tribe on another was over. The offering of human sacrifices was outlawed and life and property throughout India took on a new value. And just think, England does, with 70,000 men, what no emperor of

India, with all his fabulous wealth and millions of followers, was able to do." "That's sure a mark for 

England," I says, figuring I got to be right this time. 

But how could I be? Theo checks me, "No, Al, not a mark for England necessarily, a mark for our modern civilization or if you like for Christianity." "You're wonderful, Theo," comes a chorus from the 

Playfair sisters. They don't even know that I've celebrated my first birthday-from all the attention I gets. "I don't know if I'm getting feeble with age or not, but I don't seem to stand the 'gaff' like I used

WORLD BEATERS 

to. Why, I've seen me do six turns on the old Keith circuit in one day-the last one at 11:30-and then go out after that on an all-night party and be fresh as a daisy for the same grind next day. But forts don't seem to be my forte somehow. How's that for a pun, Theo?" No, one morning like this and I'm done out. So the afternoon is spent in the shade on the lawn with the odd tumbler of lemonade. About five o'clock when the sun gets over the top a little, and we're refreshed we takes in some of the bazaars on foot. Mohamet tells us Agra is second to no place in the world when it comes to carved stone work. So, we picks up copies of the Taj Mahal in marble and a few elephants and the like, and the way them boys can turn out that stuff is staggering. Mohamet seems to know the boss at one of these marble factorieswe'll call it a factory, but it ain't, it's a place that was built for a garage but not having a car and not want< ing it left idle they puts about twenty Indians in there at about half a rupee a day and they do them wonders with marble. So he gets us permission to watch the natives cut out designs in stone that's so fine it would make a spider go insane with jealousy. They also do that embroidery in gold and silver and weave gold and silver cloth for them evening gowns you see the ladies in around the Ritz-and I mean "you see the ladies" too. But not being well enough acquainted we don't have to buy the girls any of this. It's time for dinner now so we beats it back and gets into the nose bags. That evening is spent like the night beforeonly I gets nearer and nearer to the burning question. 

About one more night and I should have it out. Before taking the girls home we suggests seeing this Fatehpur Sikri place on the morrow and the girls are all for it. 

So the morning finds us burning up the twentythree miles that separates Agra from this de-

WORLD BEATERS 

171 serted town of Fatehpur Sikri that Akbar built to be his capital. And I mean we're burning it up, not with a pony, no, no, but an honest to Akbar automobile.

I actually feels like an emperor after following a livery stable odor for days. When we're yet afar off, like the prodigal son, I sees a fatted calf in the fields but that ain't what I want to tell you about. Away off in the distance, is the great red sand-stone wall that completely surrounds Fatehpur Sikri. It looks like 

Agra fort as we approaches. Arriving, we enters through the largest gate in India and here we are inside a wall six miles around, and the city—a complete city in every respect-is perhaps the only place in the world built entirely by one man. In one respect Fatehpur Sikri resembles many towns in Florida. The plan is all laid out with paved streets, sewer and water, but not a living soul inside the city limits. According to Mohamet, Akbar had this place built for his headquarters to be the ideal capital city, and it was his capital, until, for political reasons, he was forced to desert the city for Agra-though some authorities claim that he left it because of the lack of enough good water. The whole city, walls and all, are of red sand stone, with the palaces, mosques, temples and tombs ornamented with inlays of black and white marble and mother-of-pearl. To think that it has stood all them centuries in a perfect state of preservation and absolutely abandoned.

We roams around this place most of the day, having lunch at a Government room there. And it's such a treat having a city all to oneself. I'd enjoy this sight seeing business so much more if each city could only be empty while we went through, but I suppose that would soon become so boring, I'd scream just to hear a human noise. On our return to Agra in the afternoon we detours off the main line and pays a visit to Sikandra, a little burg about five miles out of

WORLD BEATERS 

Agra, where this chap, Akbar, rests. And boy, he must of been plenty tired after building Fatehpur Sikri, the fort at Agra and what have we-in fact, he didn't leave nothing to somebody else, he even designed his own mausoleum and had it well under way when he handed in his time. And what one grand tomb this is! Set in the middle of a beautiful park of one hundred and twenty acres, filled with flowers, shrubs and palm trees; the whole garden surrounded by a red sand stone wall twenty-four feet high-believe me it looks like a fit place for a chap like "Aky." To prove that he recognized a little good in every religion, but didn't subscribe entirely to any one, he designed his mausoleum after a Buddhist Viharo, had it carved with Arabian and Hindu figures, had the odd inscription from the Koran and then ordered that he be laid to rest with his face toward the rising sun and not toward 

Mecca, as a good Mohammedan should. Boy, what a success Akbar would of been in Washington, eh?

He could agree with everybody and still have his own ideas. And to prove there was no limit to his tolerance, Mohamet tells us that Akbar had three wivesnot to mention a harem-one a Mohammedan, one a 

Hindu, and the other a Christian. But I noticed in

Fatehpur Sikri and in the fort at Agra he had separate palaces for each one. He might of been broadminded but he didn't have any more success in selling that idea to the Mrs. Akbar's than me or you would have. I suppose because Akbar put a little of everybody's art in his tomb it deserves the reputation Mohamet claims for it, "The most unique building in the world." Not the grandest, nor the largest, nor the most expensive-though it's a runner-up for all them championships-but it's the most unusual building in India, at least, and likely in the world. It's five stories high, of red sand stone, each story supported entirely by carved sand stone pillars, the upper story open to

WORLD BEATERS 

173 the weather but enclosed on all sides by great slabs of white marble cut out with delicate floral designs and in the centre of this space is Akbar's cenotaph, cut out of a single block of white marble and about four feet away is the marble pedestal that held the famous Koh-i-noor diamond, now a part of the headgear of the Queen of England, worn only when she's presented to 

Helen Wills. 

I claims it's a day's work we've done so we all vote Akbar the Soviet's whiskers when it comes to brick laying and beats it back to the hotel for dinner. I've walked 'til my soles are smoking, so, after a brief tête-à-tête in the garden, following the feed, we totes the girls back to their school teachers and hits the millet for the night. We don't have any dates in advance for the next day because I've voted it a day of rest.

I've done nothing but see shrines, temples, tombs and forts for days and I'm caught up-and anybody ought to be able to believe me. So we sleeps till noon, calls over at the girls' hotel and takes lunch together and then the four of us sits round the lobby and gossips about the floating university. And I never knew that a school could turn out such tasty morsels of gossip and scandal or I'd of maybe gone to one myself. Professor So-and-so has been seen coming out of Miss 

Whosit's room, and Patricia Somebody is going to marry Jack Thingamabob in Paris-at least everybody says they'd better—and, you know, just for a change from emperors and religions the afternoon's conversation is refreshing. But what I'm wondering is, had we been talking to a couple of the other students would we of been hearing this kind of chatter about K and B? When we leaves the girls and starts back for our own hotel I asks Theo this and he says "Why, Al, there's nothing wrong with that gang of students. 

They've just been thrown together for so long that they're like brothers and sisters, and you know how

WORLD BEATERS 

a boy will rush into his sister's room, unannounced, and vice versa. You know that's perfectly alright but it would be considered an unpardonable breach of etiquette to do it with a strange girl or a very recent acquaintance. Can't you see how it has all come about? They are brothers and sisters in action but still strangers in mind, and, consequently, each one misconstrues the actions of the other. Have no fear for their morals, Al. I really believe they are safer on this kind of university than at one on land.” hope some of that is correct," is my only comment. “I The next day we drives out to see the tomb of Itmad-ud-Daulah and the Chini-ka-Rauza, and, while they're works of art, I've seen so many tombs they don't seem to register any more. Theo goes into what he calls "ecstasies" over them but to me the drive is just so much hay and oats wasted.

A few days later we're on the platform waiting for the train to Delhi, only four or five hours north. Eventually she pulls in and we picks ourselves out a nice first-class compartment finished in red. I comments to Theo on such funny ways to run a railroad and he asks me what I mean. "Well," I reminds him, “here we are half-way across India and there ain't been a conductor look at our tickets since we passed through the turnstiles in Calcutta." He remembers that it's true and says, "How unlike Japan." He would remind me of that just when I thought I was happy. 

Of course, they've got conductors but since you can't walk through a train like in America the conductors just stands on the platforms and looks at a ticket here and there to make sure some of them Hindus is honest.

But it just happens they don't pick on us. I'm thinking all the nice things we could of bought with the money when a well-dressed, good-looking Mohammedan gets into our compartment with us. 

He passes the time of day with a smile and then the door of our

WORLD BEATERS 

175 compartment opens and a Hindu gets in. He ain't so good. And to make him less desirable he's smoking the most atrocious cigar that ever assaulted my nostrils-it must of been one from the box his wife gave him for Xmas-and then, just as I was about to suggest to Theo that we get ourselves another compartment, the Mohammedan opens the door and calls. the conductor.

Upon the latter's arrival he's asked if he allows third-class passengers to ride in first-class compartments, at which the conductor almost loses his turban shaking his head and exclaiming, "No, No." "But," says the Mohammedan, "I'm of the opinion that this Hindu is attempting to do so." Whereupon the conductor demands a look at the Hindu's ticket and, sure enough, it's a third-class pasteboard. The conductor takes him by the seat of the neck and drags him from the compartment in no mild manner and the 

Mohammedan smiles as if to say, "There goes another dirty Hindu to pyre." Our train pulls out shortly after this incident and I can't help wondering how this

Mohammedan knew that Hindu was trying to pull a fast one. At last my curiosity wins and I up and asks him and he smiles that contented smile and answers, "Nothing uncanny about it. When that Hindu entered the compartment he had his ticket in his hand and I noticed it was the same color as mine." Sufferin' cats, can you beat that? Can you tie it? After a few stops at unimportant monkey houses-called "waiting-rooms" by the railroad company-we pulls into Delhi, the present capital of India. 

We spends a week here, milling round with the 300,000 that figures in Delhi's census, and if we hadn't come to this town there's just plenty about India that I'd of never even imagined-but now, oh, now I'm educated. We strolls through the old wall-enclosed 

Delhi, visits the walled-off (not Waldorf) fort inside

WORLD BEATERS 

the town. Sees the new Delhi the English is building; takes in tombs, mosques and what have they, and, I'm telling you, somebody spent a lot of money some time on them buildings. Just to give you a little relief from my chatter—and to give me a rest too-here's a couple pages from Theo's diary that he wrote while in Delhi. 

Read it, it's good, at least, there's a lot of words in it that don't mean a thing to me and it seems that whenever he says anything that goes over my head everybody else thinks it ought to be put in with Shakespeare's ditties, so I'm just supposing his diary's the same way. Here's the words, try it out on your piano. "Up in the heart of India there lies today, a new Delhi, the capital of that land of mystery. Planned by Sir Edward Lutyens, it is to be the perfect city. 

The Parliament building, inspired by the Coliseum at Rome, is a 'thing of beauty'. The whole scene is superb and its setting, in the very cradle of history, as it were, adds romance to beauty and lends to new Delhi something of long-lost yesterdays of the land of the turban. "Just four miles from this great architectural achievement stands the wall-enclosed Delhi of the Mogul dynasty, and within easy range of one's vision are the ruins of six former Delhis-having boasted, perhaps, greater government buildings than new Delhi's, who knows? For their debris is the only evidence of their having existed. Lost in antiquity are the records of these earliest Delhis. But the story of nations is an old one and the rules of the game that emperors play are unchangeable. Thus, we may safely conjecture that the rise and fall of those Delhis is but the authentic history of invasion and conquest by hostile tribes, the destruction of the capital and the establishment of the new regime; a short term of pseudo peace, and then history repeating itself until today finds forty-five miles of country strewn with the

WORLD BEATERS 

177 evidence of constructive building, international strife, destructive violence, and bloodshed that invariably accompanies such acts. "What a field for reflection it offers. What a lesson those age-old ruins should teach the nations of the world; but nations seem slow to learn the folly of war. Standing amid these ruins I overheard a traveler comment on the ignorantly destructive propensities of those long forgotten potentates and I couldn't but ask myself just how far we had progressed in this respect from those lamentably dark ages. I visioned the poppies of Flanders fields; the ruined Cathedral of Rheims, the demolished towns of Belgium; the misery and horror and suffering of only ten years gone, and I looked again across those battle-scarred sights of former Delhis and I couldn't justly force a criticism of those responsible for the ruins. "If the environs of Delhi teach us anything, they preach in eloquent style the folly of war. Would that the ruler of every country before ascending to his office could look from the tower of Delhi's new government building out upon forty-five miles of ruin from war. "Still to the traveler these ruins are what make

India the land of mystery and while one's better self condemns the destructive proclivities that brought it all about, he secretly thrills at thought of the courage and adventure of those conquerors of yesterday, and against his better judgment finds himself standing where those mighty men of bygone days commanded their hundreds of thousands of infantry and he becomes envious of their daring. "Authentic history of these Delhis begins in the twelfth century when Mohammed Ghori, the first Mohammedan conqueror of India, overthrew the Hindu powers of the capital and built his own city from the ruins of theirs. He proved to be one of India's best

WORLD BEATERS 

rulers but his assassination only twelve years later brought to the throne Kutb-ud-din-Aibak, Ghori's commander-in-chief, the deceased emperor having left no son. With the ascension of Kutb-ud-din-Aibak begins the rule of India's slave kings, having risen from slavery to rule an empire. During their reign India was constantly invaded by the Moguls and it was in combat with them that the son of the Indian emperor met his death, which so deeply grieved the emperor that he died of a broken heart and his two successors were assassinated in quick order, bringing the reign of this dynasty to an end three years later. "During the next two hundred years, India saw no less than four dynasties come and go in rapid succession, all contantly hampered by Mogul invasion, until in 1526, Babar, the first of the Moguls, succeeded in taking Delhi and setting up what was destined to be the last dynasty in India. "The story of the Moguls reads like fiction; what a motion picture D. W. Griffith could make of this line of rulers. It flares with intrigue, thrills with romance, is bathed in innocent blood, glories in the acme of architecture, lures with invasion, saddens in pathos. 

In fact, every human emotion has reached a climax in this great panorama. "When Babar marched on Delhi that hot day in 

June, he headed but twelve thousand men, but they were soldiers to the core. He was met at the gates of Delhi by one hundred thousand men and one thousand elephants. But these were poorly disciplined, and training always tells, and so the army of the last Lodi king was scattered in confusion and Babar marched triumphantly into Delhi. Ruling but four years Babar left the vast empire to his oldest son, Humayan, who ruled India for twenty-six troubled years, caused by his two younger brothers who sought the throne. At last he was forced to flee for to usurp

WORLD BEATERS 

179 his life, and while escaping across the desert of Scind, his famous son, Akbar, was born. Humayan retired to Kabul where he passed the next nine years. In the meantime his conqueror at Delhi, Sher Shah, ruled India like a master. Reforms beneficial to the masses were instigated by him. A new land revenue system was initiated and religious tolerance was observed. 

Had Sher Shah been favored by the gods to carry on, the story of India might have been vastly different, but six years after his invasion of Hindustan he was accidentally killed. For the next nine years chaos ruled in India. Then Humayan, quick to seize the opportunity caused by disorder, marched upon Delhi and easily subdued its ruler and again established himself as emperor. But his revived glory was short lived, for one year later he died from a fall in his palace and his desert-born son, Akbar, ascended to the throne at the age of thirteen. For fifty years Akbar earned himself the name of Akbar the Great, the finest of all Mogul emperors. He was fearless in battle, which won him vast extensions to his empire. He was a great builder and most tolerant in religious matters, having chosen his ministers from Hindus and Mohammedans alike. He had weekly meetings held where the followers of all faiths gathered and expounded their beliefs and Akbar attended these meetings himself. 

He demonstrated his tolerance further by taking a 

Hindu bride, a Mohammedan and a Christian, and it is told that perfect harmony existed in his domestic life. Akbar's last days were darkened by the rebellion of his son and heir apparent, but a reconciliation was effected before the emperor's death and his son forgiven, whereupon Akbar placed his own imperial turban upon the head of Jahangir Shah and handed him the sword with which his father, Humayan, had taken Delhi; and the son ruled for some twenty-two years, during which time his wife exerted so great an influ-

WORLD BEATERS 

ence over him that it was really she who ruled. Shah 

Jahan succeeded Jahangir and his fame as a builder. spread throughout the then known world and aroused the aspiration for possession in the rulers of distant lands. In the thirteenth year of his gorgeous reign 

Shah Jahan fell ill and, thinking he could not recover, his four sons rose in arms for the throne. Aurengzeb emerged victorious and imprisoned his father in the 

Jasmine Tower at the fort of Agra where he remained until his death eight years later. It was Shah Jahan who erected the Taj Mahal to receive the remains of his beautiful and beloved wife, Mumtaz-i-Mahal. It is this superb structure that lures thousands of travelers yearly, who stand in awed silence before its fretted loveliness and marvel at such a conception, such a love-and beside the lady of the Taj Aurangzeb had his father, Shah Jahan, placed upon his death.

What a picture flashes through the mind as one stands within that almost sacred sepulchre. For the fifty years following the death of Aurangzeb disorder was the rule in Hindustan. Attacks on Delhi followed in quick succession and the succeeding Mogul emperors were extremely weak and vicious, and the days of their dynasty were numbered. The final downfall of the 

Moguls occurred in 1857 when the British took Delhi and assumed control of India. "This, at least, is the glimpse of the romantic history of India that flashed through my mind as I stood upon the ruins of those one-time glorious Delhis."

Now if that's all that flashed through Theo's mind you can imagine the volumes that flashed through mine. To tell the truth, them ruins meant so little to me that there wasn't enough in my mind to light up a flash so you could see it, but who cares which one of us has the bright ideas so long as I record them?

We tramps through the fort that Shah Jahan had built for himself and which they say was the grandest

181 thing in the world. But it seems, one Hallowe'en night, in 1739-about a hundred years after Shah Jahan had turned out the lights-that a chap named Mouzzam, who had got the place from his old man's executors, left the gate open while he and the Mrs. took in the first night of "Abe's Irish Rose," and a certain Mr. 

Nadir Shah, known to the police as "two-gun Shah," and wanted in Persia for non-support of some 200 wives and for using the mails to defraud, succeeded in gaining an entrance and carting off the famous Peacock throne and the Koh-i-Noor diamond; also, he paused to strip the silver from the ceiling of the Diwan-i-Khas and, too, the gold inscriptions from the ends of this hall of private audience. He was going to wait and get the gold fillings out of the owner's teeth but he had a date with a blonde at ten o'clock, so he took off-and they say when Mouzzam staggered in the next morning with his own wife-that's how they knew he was drunk-that the place was a mess. Well, maybe it was, but that don't make the place look so grand today. In fact I've only got the guide's word for it that it ever was fixed up that way.

One afternoon we drives out to see the Kutb Minar 

Tower, the highest observation point in India. Boy, what a view you get from there! Over about four miles is a British military post and looking straight down at the base of the tower is the most remarkable monument in the world. It's a shaft of solid iron, sixteen inches through and over twenty three feet high.

It has stood there for 1700 years without a sign of rust. A Sanskrit inscription gives its age by allowing as how it belonged to Raja Dhava. It's an Indian superstition that anyone who can stand with his back to the pillar and touch his hands behind will have any kind of wish come true. That sounds easy, and it looks easy, but try and do it. I sprained eight ligaments, wrenched my back and got a permanent kink in my neck trying to do it and Theo says I wasn't within a foot of getting my hands to meet. But neither was Theo, so we both wastes our wishes. 

We starts back for Delhi about 5 P. M. and "when we're afar off" we sees a crowd around the new Parliament buildings and it's got all the earmarks of being unruly. We gets closer-I was right-it is unruly. 

It turns out to be an anti-British demonstration of Hindus. It seems they're fed up with British rulewhich is like Sambo getting mad at you for chasing off the Ku Klux Klan and giving him a watermelon. 

But whoever's responsible for stirring them up deserves the sea-weed turban for doing a good job. Forgetting that we had a Hindu guide for a change, I turns to him and says, "By the way, Mohamet-" and that's as far as I got for quite a while. Folks, if you ever want to see real fire-works call a Hindu "Mohamet." It's the same kind of mistake as calling

I think that gang ought to have floral offerings for Lord Irwin instead of bombs." "That's what all tourists think," he says, "but you should be here and see England take our taxes and spend sixty per cent of it on her army instead of building schools to educate our people.
