The next day we just drives about the parks, really beautiful parks, outside the wall; purchase a few more souvenirs and calls it a day. That night in our room 

Theo's cleaning out his pockets and along with the rest of his possessions is the tickets. Having nothing else to do I starts reading the stuff that's printed on the front of the little booklet that's attached to them and what do I find but that we've only got ten more days to get to Colombo if we want to get full mileage out of them coupons. That means we leave Delhi in the morning and make Jaipur our first stop-and a short one.

Well, we does just that and it turns out this Jaipur place would of been a flop if it wasn't for the rajah's palaces built all over the country. Boy, they're grand homes and his town house is right in the centre of Jaipur, the pink city. It seems one of his forefathers had a weakness for pink and had the whole place painted that color, every house, store and buildingpink-pale pink; but he owned the whole place and nobody could prevent it. This rajah that owns Jaipur now also owns 16,000 square miles of surrounding country and for pastime he has 300 thoroughbred saddle horses, 200 imported carriage horses, 150 camels and 50 elephants. I think this is clear hooie when the guide tells us, so we gets passes for the palace and sees it for ourselves. Every pony is there and every hump and trunk in its place. And in case the kid's in a hurry sometime there's sixty foreign-made automobiles in the garage-"Home, James." That's what's meant when they say, "the rich in India are very rich and the poor are very poor." It's the truth. 

We hikes out to Amber City-another deserted

WORLD BEATERS 

185 town like Fatehpur Sikri with its swell palaces, etc. 

Folks, I'm getting dizzy looking at gorgeous buildings and half-starved Hindus-but it's all the product of centuries ago—they'll probably strike more of a balance in time.

We beats it on to Bombay, after a few other stops, and here we are, it seems, back in Calcutta. For all the world, an occidental city,—at least from a first and superficial look it is. Calcutta and Bombay has one thing in common-a very strange thing-they're both the second city in the British Empire. Now don't ask me to figure it out. They're almost of a size-around one and a quarter millions-they're almost tied for manufactures and for shipping and they both claim this title. Personally I think it's a matter for the 

League of Nations to decide-surely they could decide that without France objecting. I tell you one title 

Bombay does hold, undisputed, she's got a bigger melting-pot than Calcutta. I never seen so many dif ferent kinds of faces. But of its temples, towers of silence, Chinese quarters and all that mess, there ain't enough difference to bother telling. It's a real city you understand, but seeing Calcutta is seeing Bombay -though I wouldn't say that backwards. I guess that gives the edge to Calcutta.

We rides for five days through central and southern India in the hottest weather I ever hopes to sweat through and if I had a cent for every goat I seen on that ride I could make Henry Ford look like a public charge. We spends a day in Madras, and if that was all we'd seen of India it would of been interesting, but after where we've been, Madras is just another place for the engineer to take on water. After a rough ride on the ferry over to Ceylon, we grabs the train that's waiting and it's only a matter of some ten or twelve hours 'til we're in Colombo. This ride through Ceylon is the prettiest train ride yet. This

WORLD BEATERS 

island is one fertile spot if India has one. No matter where you look is terraced rice fields, or coffee beans or cocoanut groves or tea growing; and all through the place, dotted here and there, as if nature was outdoing herself to make it beautiful, is sparkling lakes. 

Boy, I care for Ceylon. And now, at last, we're in

Colombo, the last stop of our tour of India, and it's a city that can send anybody off with a good impression.

As the farther north we went in India, the larger and sturdier the people, so, the farther south, the smaller. The natives don't look like the natives in 

India, for some reason. They're darker and have more delicate features. The men wear more ornaments than they do in India and the women are my idea of real gypsies.

We're put up at the Mt. Lavinia Hotel, one grand place, a few miles below the business section of Colombo. A stranger wouldn't know that it wasn't part of the town. It's built out on a rocky cliff and overlooks one of the most wonderful bits of ocean I've seen yet. The place is almost lost amid the palm trees and you should see a native boy go up one of them cocoanut palms in his bare feet. He'd make a black squirrel gnash its teeth. The native girls are around selling hand-made lace, and doubting that they done such fine work by hand we goes over to one of their houses and sees it being done. Oh, they're clever.

The main part of Colombo is strictly modern, though she has the unsightly native quarters too. But the majority of her 210,000 look to be housed in the better section. But after Singapore, Bombay, Calcutta and other great seaports Colombo don't register much outside of its natural beauty. "Looking out," as Theo says, "upon a sapphire sea," and when we're steaming out of the harbor a few days later, into the Indian 

Ocean, we looks back at the palm-fringed shores of

WORLD BEATERS 

187 Ceylon and Theo says, "Al, that little island, endowed with the glories of nature and ornate with her four and a half millions of picturesque peoples, is one of the most beautiful spots on earth."

The two weeks run from Colombo to Port Said, across the Indian Ocean, through the Red Sea and up the Suez Canal is without much kick. I guess, after a few weeks of travel through India, we're too content to sit out on deck and watch the flying fishes and bet when the next school of porpoises will appear. I know 

I'm tired out and this trip, with nothing to do, comes just at the right place in my plans, anyway. There's just one thing I remember on this whole ride across the Indian Ocean and that's the sunsets. I never seen such gorgeous sunsets in all my life. When we was in San Francisco I went over to Oakland one evening just to see the sun set over San Francisco bay and through the Golden Gate and I thought I had seen the last word in swell sunsets, but you got to see old Sol drop into the Indian Ocean and see the rainbow of colors on the eastern horizon before you've seen anything in sunsets. I wish I could remember what Theo said about them sunsets, I only recall that it was one of his rarest bits of eloquence. 

Up at the north end of the Red Sea Theo points out Mt. Sinai-he heard the purser say it was-and he tells me that's where Moses got the Ten Commandments dished up to him. I says, "Well, they held a world record a long time but the Volstead Act has more violators now.' A little further up we're showed a stone on the bank and it's supposed to mark the point where the Red Sea parted to let Moses make his getaway and then closed up on Pharoah. With the end of the sea only a mile or so away, where they could of walked around in nearly as good time, it makes the tale a wee bit fishy, you know. When we're passing through the Suez Canal, a ditch about 300

WORLD BEATERS 

feet wide and nearly a hundred miles long, out through the sands of the desert from Suez to Port Said, Theo says, "Al, would you imagine a little ditch like this could be the key to England's power in India and the 

South Seas?" "What do you mean?" I asks, “Is this something else England owns?" "Yes," he tells me, "and up until recent times the loss of it would have sounded her knell in the East; but now that nations protect their holdings with air fleets, things like this are merely financial propositions." "Oh," I says, knowing nothing else to say.

Since Port Said was really the result of the canal, being built at the end of the canal while the work was going on, it naturally is without any past history to make it important. It's a seaport on the Mediterranean end of the canal with about 90,000 people, a few from everywhere. It's almost without anything to make it attractive and nothing around it but drifting desert, so we only stay here 'til the first train leaves and then hits it off for Jerusalem, to see this Holy 

Land place. 99 

After the ride across the desert of northern Egypt and Palestine, we hits into the hills and our iron horse just about goes into reverse. "I'm glad we got first class tickets," I tells Theo, and, as usual, he wants to know the why of it. "Because," I says, "I can see where the second-class customers is going to have to get out and walk and the third-class get out and push.'

But none of that's true. We actually gets through them barren, stone-clad peaks without help and it ain't too long 'til we're at the station in Jerusalem. 

As we steps out on the platform, Theo takes a deep breath, surveys the hills about, and then, like he was Moses, says, "We're standing in the cradle of civilization." I don't know what he means 'til we're started through the City of Jerusalem, where we're either going up or coming down all the time. There ain't a

WORLD BEATERS 

189 block in town where a guy could be on the level even if he wanted to be, and, as I nearly rocks myself to sleep on these ups and downs, I sees what Theo means by "cradle."

We don't have a guide in Jerusalem-only a printed one. The streets ain't wide enough for three to walk abreast and a guide's no good if he can't be with you. 

So we just gropes through the Holy Land-like everybody else looks to be doing-and gets what we can from the book and from taxi-drivers and odd conversations with natives and other travelers. Without exception Jerusalem is the strangest town I've seen yet and the characters on the streets are the goofiest looking numbers out. The book says the town has over 

60,000 inhabitants-I suppose that's counting them "cuks" on the street-and they are matted into a wallenclosed section about half the size of Central Park in New York-and don't think they live in sky-scrapers either they live in squalid little two-story stone buildings, tight together and practically no modern conveniences. After our first walk through the place I tells 

Theo that if this place was the cradle of our modern civilization then civilization sure got a long ways away from the old homestead. There's just about as much in this place to suggest civilization as there is at a lynching in Georgia. When I makes these cracks to

Theo he takes me to task for it and rebukes (that's a good word) rebukes me thusly; "I'm surprised at such sacrilege, Al. Don't you know that Jerusalem is sacred to Christian, Jew and Mohammedan alike? It was here that Christ lived, taught and died. From here 

Mohammed ascended into heaven; and this is the 

Jew's Promised Land." "That might all be true, Theo," I admits, "but the day I smuggled a bumblebee into Sunday school, I remember the teacher telling us that Christianity was a brotherhood of men. 

Well, if a brotherhood of men need fifteen armed

WORLD BEATERS 

soldiers to keep the different members from bumping each other off-like we seen in the Church of the Holy 

Sepulchre yesterday—I don't see where they rate that's so sacred." "Al," he comes back, "you're blinded by the illusion of the near. If you could only see the stages through which civilization has passed since the advent of Christianity, you'd have a finer appreciation of all this. Naturally enough a shrine attracts the religious minded so why shouldn't the greatest of all shrines attract the most religious minded? And what makes a man a greater fanatic than an insatiable zeal to religious belief? Of course Jerusalem would be filled with this type, how could it be otherwise? And they wish to preserve the atmosphere and appearance of the Jerusalem that Christ new-that's why it hasn't changed-and these are the reasons why it's the strangest city we've seen. Why, Al, I can fairly hear the mad mob crying, 'Release Barabas-give us Christ to be crucified,' and I can see the nonplussed Pilate, hesitating, trying to think of another way to appease that frenzied mob, and, failing this, handing the innocent Christ over to be crucified. I can see them following Him through these tortuous streets to Calvary, and, as He hangs there, between the two thieves, the mob jeering and spitting at Him, I can hear Him say, 'Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do'." Then he just stands there, looking off into space, like he really IS seeing it all. "Well, maybe you're right," I says, "but I'm still in favor of civilization's new home." And my mind goes back to America.

We takes a car ride down through the Hebron valley, visiting Hebron, Bethlehem and other towns and villages, all built of the gray stone that forms the bulk of the country. Since there's several thousand preachers, priests and rabbis telling the story of this country every Sunday, there's no use of me stealing

WORLD BEATERS 

191 their thunder-and anyway, I ain't got you where I can lift a collection-so I'm just referring you to the neighborhood church or synagogue to get this part of our trip from an expert. And when you hear him read the story from the Bible and preach about it, just shut your eyes and picture this country as it was written about them many centuries ago and you'll have as true an idea of the country as I got, because there ain't a stone been changed since then-not that I noticed, anyway; and if you don't go to church for a thousand years this advice will likely be just as sound, because I can't see how anybody would be sufficiently interested to change it—and if they was I don't know how they'd do it.

Solomon's pool is still doing business and the valley of Je-hosha-phat has still got lots of stones for future sling-shot Davids. And all about, on the hills, is herds of sheep-I don't know what they're eating-if they was goats I could understand it, but I never seen sheep eat rocks. The next day we hoofs it through Jerusalem to the temple area where the Mohammedans are doing business. On our way through town we passes the Wailing Wall. It's Friday, which happens to mean the same thing to the Wailing Wall as the Fourth of

July means to Coney Island-almost exactly the same thing because all the Jews from both Jerusalem and New York City turn out. This wall is about one hundred feet long and looks like any hundred feet of the wall around Auburn prison. The Jews gather here to cry over their lost glories-I don't know why, I never heard of a Jew losing anything—but it could of happened and still escaped my notice. 

At last we arrives at the Dome of the Rock, a great octagonal dome-crowned structure, built over a huge rock which is supposed to be the air field from which 

Mohammed took off for heaven. And we thought 

Lindbergh had the solo flight record. Oh, there's so

WORLD BEATERS 

much for us to learn, I'm afraid we'll have to stay in after school and get caught up with our lessons. Just outside this Dome of the Rock there's a pocket edition called the Dome of the Chain-it is also called David's judgment seat-in every respect it's a copy of the 

Dome of the Rock. The Mohammedans claim that at one time a chain hung in this dome that was suspended from heaven and that it had the ability to decide the honesty of man. It seems, if a man was honest he could reach out and grasp the chain but if he was dishonest the chain would swing away from him when he reached for it. The reason it was abandoned was because a Jew tricked it and since it was no longer able to compete with man's trickery it went out of the magistrate business. This story they tell about the Jew is the first Jewish story on record-as far as I know, and I was on the Keith circuit and should know the age of all jokes. According to the present owners of the Dome, a Mohammedan once loaned a Jew a sum of money (that's the weak spot in the story) and when he demanded it back the Jew claimed he had paid it. So they took the two of them to the Dome of the Chain. The Mohammedan stepped up first and grasped the chain without difficulty. Then the Jew stepped up and handed his cane to the Mohammedan to hold while he made the test-he had concealed the money in the cane-and, claiming he had returned the money, he reached out and grasped the chain. Then, taking back his stick, court was over. That's the story they tell. Suit yourself. 

Then we takes a spin down to the Dead Sea and the River Jordan. Imagine this sea being only forty miles from the Mediterranean and it's over 1,200 feet below sea level. It's so salty you couldn't sink in it if you had your pockets filled with a bride's first batch of biscuits. And the Jordan River, pouring it's fresh water into it can be seen for half a mile out into the

WORLD BEATERS 

193 sea, just like it was inside its banks. This Jordan is a cute little stream but hardly rates the title of a river. 

If that tiger that scared me in Singapore was behind me I could almost clear the Jordan in one jump. After paying our respects to Jericho we heads back for Jerusalem, past the old inn, where the Good Samaritan took the chap that fell among the thieves. I turns to 

Theo and says, "That boy was lucky he only fell among thieves. If he'd been in Illinois and fell among machine guns he'd of had no use for an inn-he'd of been out-and how." Passing through Bethany, the sun having set, I sees these hills do have some grass on them and millions of little short-stemmed poppies -they call them the roses of Sharon. It's funny you don't notice them in the sunlight.

That night in our room Theo gets to recalling his history and he says, "Al, I just realized a very peculiar coincidence in the history of Jerusalem. This city bowed to a new ruler seventeen times before the birth of Christ and when General Allenby marched through the Jaffa gate in the month of December, 1917, the world witnessed the seventeenth capture of 

Jerusalem since the birth of Christ." "Can anybody explain that?" I asks. "Explain what," Theo demands. "Why anybody that's sane wants this crop of rocks bad enough to fight for it." "Al," he admits, "I guess you've stumped me at last-but I would think that a city so unostentatious as this, that can inspire more nations to possession than any other city on earth-must have moved them by what has taken place here for it is to Jerusalem that the three dominant religions of the world pay homage, and in the shadow of whose wall countless thousands of pilgrims pass in awed humility." What's the use of me asking a bird like that anything? If he don't know, I don't learn anything and if he does know, I can't understand his answer.

WORLD BEATERS 

Theo wants to go out where they're doing some excavating. We finds the "archeologists," as he calls them, out on the edge of town-that's outside the wall, I mean, out where they're building a modern Jerusalem and they claim they've unearthed the ruins of five former cities built on top of each other. It's their opinion there's plenty more under that. I say, "Theo, if a tidal wave or a sand storm or something should ever cover up New York City and a gang of them ditch diggers uncovered it a thousand years later, they'd claim there was fifty cities built on top of each other. They don't know any more from them ruins than they'd know if they was fooling round in any second-hand brick yard." Theo just looks disgusted.

So, having seen Palestine, we hops a caboose for 

Cairo. Our train does better going back-down hill -and the ride across the desert is as interesting as when we went. The scenes are all new. They had a sand storm while we was in Jerusalem and where there was hills before now we have valleys, and, as Theo would say, vice versa. The only thing that breaks the monotony of the sand view is the foot-prints of a camel that must of just passed this way, and once in a while we catches a glimpse of the blue Mediterranean between a couple of sand-hills. It's an all day run-in fact they've took the sun in before we reaches Cairo, so our first impression of this burg is by Mazda -and don't think Cairo ain't lit up. When we comes to a halt in front of Shepherd's Hotel and I lamps the tuxedos and the bare backs sitting out on the open veranda and my ears registers familiar strains from 

Broadway, I lets out a "whoopee," for I sees us getting acquainted round here and pulling another of them Cleopatra and Mark Antony acts. The door of our car opens and we steps out like we was both the Prince of Wales. All round us is tall, dark, good-look-

WORLD BEATERS 

195 ing chaps, dressed in long flowing robes of every color you can think of. They speak much better English than I do and they want to sell themselves to us as guides for our stay in Egypt. They work fast. We ain't even in the hotel yet and there's twenty-five of them out of a job and anxious to work for us. We waves them off and tells them we'll pick a candidate in the morning, but they sure are the most picturesque characters we've seen yet and even if Shepherd's 

Hotel does look like the late Waldorf-Astoria, them guides would keep you from forgetting you was away from home. As we escapes from them Theo says, "They suggest the Aurora Borealis," and laughing, like I knew what he meant, we push on into the hotel and gets assigned to the snappiest suite of rooms since we left the Imperial in Tokyo. A little pamphlet on the dresser says that Shepherd's Hotel is over a hundred years old and that it's built on the site of Napoleon's camp when he strutted his stuff in Egypt-as if it could be the site of his camp while he was at Waterloo-though it could have been without changing the score at Waterloo. Anyway it seems to be a popular place whether Nap's ghost attracts the crowd or that gang of guides in front that looks like the northern lights.

Well, it's past dinner time, so we has a bite brought up to the room, and maybe this place don't give service. That tray is dressed up like a table at the Ambassador and the food is food, what I mean. It's nearly ten o'clock by now and we hears the orchestra tuning up for the evening's dance. "What do you say if we dress and take in the dance, Al?" is Theo's contribution to the evening's entertainment, and anytime I skips the chance to let my arms go to waist I hope somebody hits me with a mosque. So we dress and parades to the ballroom. Folks, I never seen such lowcut evening gowns in my life-and remember, I'm

WORLD BEATERS 

from the stage, too. Theo takes a look from the corridor, where we're standing looking down on the scene, and says, "Al, did you ever see anything like that?" "Not since I was weaned," I admits. But we're broadminded and don't let a little thing like that keep us out-we orders a table and gets seated. We watches a couple of dances as we sips some drinks and while it looks good to see a bunch of our own people dancing to good old American jazz it ain't exactly as exciting as doing it yourself. It's a little warm in the hall, so during an intermission we strolls out through the lobby and on the porch for air. "Catch me, Theo," 

I cries, "I'm about to do a swoon." "What's the matter with you, Al? Has the heat gone to your head?" 

I don't answer him, I'm just looking straight ahead. 

Then the girl I got my eyes on turns her head and it is 

Miss Durant. I leaps over chairs, table and bell-hops, towards her table, and then I jams on all four brakes.

There's her and the London doll, but there's a couple of sappy looking French sheiks grinning across the table at them and saying what a pleasant evening they're having the frogs. I drops my head and turns back towards Theo, who don't know yet why I dashed off; and then my ears picks up, "That isn't you, Mr. Tool, is it?" And my first thought is to let her think she's right but I can't do it. I turns and from the look on Miss Durant's face everything changes. We rushes to each other and I almost squeezes her hand off the wrist. In short, gaspy terms we tells each other how we comes to be in Egypt and what does she tell me but that her uncle was called back to America on business and, figuring they was safe now, he took the old tutor with him. I want to shout for joy and then I gets another look at them "Parlez-vous, monsieurs" and my heart sinks again. By now Theo sees what it's all about and comes tripping over, all smiles and a "How thrilling to meet you at Shepherd's," and now Miss

WORLD BEATERS 

197 London is with us too and the four of us is as close together as four quarters in a dollar bill. "Come over and meet our friends from Paris," says Miss London, and she drags us to the table to meet the Frenchmen. 

I couldn't remember the names if they'd been my father's-and the six of us stand there chatting about this and that and the Frenchmen turn out to be regular fellows. Not at all what I first thought. It seems they are waiting for their real girl friends who are inside "making up"-they've all been in to dance and are out here cooling off. "What fools for luck we are," I says to Theo, and just then the two French babies show up and they don't lack nothing to make them beautiful. One of the monsieurs suggests the eight of us go back and dance for a while and from then on Egypt means everything to me that Theo had been telling me about-the romantic Nile—the enchantment of the desert-the lure of the pyramids and the incantations of the people-I don't know yet what the words mean but I know all about their feelings. 

We dances until well after midnight—during which the party consumed several bottles of champagne and two bottles of ginger ale. "Haven't you graduated from ginger ale yet, Al?" asks Miss Durant, who never called me anything but "Mr. Tool" since we met. "No, Miss Durant," I says, "I'm still on the wagon." "Don't call me Miss Durant, Al. Call me "Joan'." "All right, Joan," I agrees-and now I knows they've had too much. The London doll makes the same demands on Theo and lists herself as Muriel. 

The quartet from France is carrying theirs like gentlemen and they seem to get quite a kick out of seeing our dames getting giddy, and they're very much surprised at a couple Americans like me and Theo not touching the stuff.

After the dance we stands around on the porch a while and passes remarks about this and that and then

WORLD BEATERS 

the French representatives shakes and says they're leaving for home in the morning and they extend one of them cordial invitations to all of us to go look them up when we reaches Paris. We tells them we'll see them later but they don't respond. In our room later, Theo says, "Al, aren't there any American girls who do not drink?" "Sure," I says, "do you think they all come from wealthy families?" Then we remarks about them giving us their first names and Theo says, "Muriel and Joan, eh? Well we can't reduce either of those to a letter, so we won't get confused with K and B." And he laughs. "No," I says, "those girls ain't so much for letters but they've got beautiful figures.' "You would think of that," is Theo's only addition. 99 About ten o'clock of this same morning we puts in a call for the girls and to our surprise they've been up and had breakfast, so we just grabs some coffee and toast and puts off eating until noon. Then I says, "Well, Miss Durant, what have they got in Egypt worth seeing?" "First," she says, "before I answer any of your questions, you answer one for me." "Shoot," I says, and she asks me whether I did or did not promise to call her Joan and not Miss Durant. I tells her I had promised but I was afraid she maybe hadn't remembered. 199 well as the next and when I'm in America I seldom refuse it, but since we've been on this trip-" and then I tells her about our experiences in Havana and she pulls my cheek and says, "Al, I think you are adorable." 

Theo and Miss London-I mean Muriel-are standing off talking by themselves, but they think it's time we went places and seen things so Theo calls, "What do you say to a camel ride?" "I've rode the 

Wabash," I tells him, "and a camel can't have more humps than that-I'm on." So the four of us heads for the curb to grab a taxi. We just touch the top step when that gang of dragomen-short for guideswarms down on us, rather up on us. Theo consults the girls as to whether or not we should engage a guide and they think we should, so Theo picks out a royal purple. Boy, those robes are grand. Immediately after hiring a dragoman in Egypt one's brain might as well be in Washington-for all the good it is to him or anybody else. He takes complete control of the party, hails a car and orders the driver to the Mena House. On the way through Cairo's wide, business-bound avenues, Mohamet-oh yes, they're all Mohamets-points out the Egyptian Museum-which we only gets a side view of, stating that we will go there later. This bird has apparently given himself a life's job. Then we passes in full view of the armories, the biggest armories I've ever seen, and now we're at the Nile-and we're passing in between the two great stone lions that guard the entrance to the Kasr El Nile Bridge. It's some bridge, wide and all steel construction, but the first sight of the Nile somehow makes heart beat faster and I unconsciously squeeze Joan's hand and she looks at me with them dreamy eyes of hers and I feel exactly like the night Ethel Barrymore took me for her brother-in the darkened wing-and kissed me. Out there on the river, as far as you can my

WORLD BEATERS 

see in both directions, is just hundreds of them overgrown rowboats with the one great "V" shaped sailthey call them Dahabiyehs. Continuing on through town we pass some real swell homes and then we hits the great open spaces where the natives do their farming. I'm just about to make a break about the funny looking mountain in the distance when Mohamet saves me by letting us in on the secret that the mounds in the offing is the pyramids, and he says that while they look to be very close they're six miles or so away. "And just now," he goes on, "we're passing through some of the most fertile farm land in Egypt. You may, or may not know (he put in them last four words after a glance at me) that in all the 363,000 square miles of Egypt only 11,000 are capable of producing a crop and this is made possible by the Nile flooding the valley every year. You will notice that all kinds of grain are being raised and many forms of vegetables-also some fruit and some cotton." "What's the idea of the little ditches all through the fields ?" I asks. "Irrigation ditches, carrying water from the irrigation canals direct to the fields," he informs me. "Oh," I says, "I thought maybe they were moats to keep the neighbors' kids off your farm." And we all laughs-all but Mohamet he says, "Well, you're right, in a way. They are boundary lines as well." Can you feature that?

The only time I tries to be funny and it's the only time 

I'm right. "Yes," Mohamet goes on, "the native is so poor that the average farm is only a little more than an acre. In fact only 8000 foreigners own over half the agricultural land of Egypt and the other 5,- 000,000 acres are divided between nearly 2,000,000 natives. But by irrigation and good farming a native can make a living on an acre of land." And from where I sits in the car I would say they was good farmers. Not modern in any way-in fact, I sees an old bird in one field plowing with oxen and I'll bet it's

WORLD BEATERS 

201 the same plow Moses used to make that furrow through the Red Sea. But their little farms look like the rice fields of Japan-just like gardens. By now we're reaching the edge of the farms and they seem to just stop dead like they had four-wheel brakes and the golden red sands of the desert start in and do a stretch of a thousand miles without a break. Right on the very edge of this sand finished desert is the place we started out for, the Mena House. It's one grand hotel and it has a garden around it that sure is a great sendoff to the folks who start out across them thousand miles of drifting sand-only a little too suggestive of the bleached white bones I've read about travelers finding out there-because this Mena House is just covered with flowers-you know, when people "look so natural."

As we drives up to the hotel I notices a string of about a hundred camels and nearly as many donkeys and I asks is Ringling Brothers in town but it turns out they're all for hire for people like us who want to do the desert in true desert style. But we drives right up to the main door of the hotel and since it's almost twelve o'clock we decides to take on groceries before going out to call on King Tut and the Mrs. So we puts in orders for four nose-bags and then strolls out to admire the wisteria, etc. The pyramids look to be only a stone's throw away-in fact I bet when the sun is low in the west they throw a shadow over this Mena place. As we walks about the grounds, Joan says, "Isn't it wonderful out here, Al? Only I wish they hadn't built this tourists' hotel so close to the pyramids. After all, they are tombs, you know, and it seems like a morbid form of commercialism to have this hotel out here just catering to those who come to view these sepulchres.' "Maybe you're right, Joan," 

I agrees, "but if Cheops Pyramid was in America we'd likely use it for a service station or put a sign over the 99

WORLD BEATERS 

entrance-Cheops' Place'-and sell hot dogs and icecream cones." "Al, you're impossible," she laughs, "but you do have a refreshing sense of humor.' 

Before I can blush Theo and Muriel has joined us.

There's a couple snappy tennis courts on one side of the hotel, and they supply everything to guests, so me and Joan takes Theo and Muriel on for a couple sets and we pours the naphtha over them both times. 

We've worked up a lather out there in the sun, not to mention an appetite, and we stops to relax just as the boy pages us for lunch. The feed goes over big and after a brief pause in the lounging room, to kind of let the proteins settle, we arouses Mohamet and orders our "camels, please." Really, you know, it's so exasperating having to wait for one's camel-my gawdif the gang on Keith's could only hear me ordering my camel brought up-they'd slay me. When the camels come and I takes a look way up there where I got to sit, I looks around for a stepladder but there's none on him. I enquires of Mohamet, "How do we mount the hump, with a vaulting pole or by airplane?" He looks at me, dumb like, as if I meant it, and then explains to the others that the camels will presently lower themselves that we might mount upon them. 

Folks, a camel is slower getting down to the ground than Congress is getting down to business. First, they drop down on one front knee, and about fifteen minutes later they decide to bend the other one. They remain in this toboggan-slide position for another fifteen or twenty minutes and then rock forward 'til the two rear extremities has gone sympathetic with the front quarters, and there you are. As all this is going on they're chewing their cuds like there was nothing in the world for us to do but wait till they decided to move. Our guide tries to take our mind off the time that's being killed by them camels by telling us that a camel is one of the most trustworthy animals in the

WORLD BEATERS 

203 world and that they live to an extremely old age. I says, "They'd have to live at least a thousand years to get a month's work done." But they're actually down now so we each climbs on one. Mohamet is back with the girls telling them to be careful when the camel gets up or they'll fall off-but I don't hear the so-andso say that and when my camel goes to get up I gives a perfect exhibition of Annette Kellerman as I takes a nose-dive straight over the head of that son-of-ahumped-back camel and flat on my face in a heap of

Sahara's sand. The only consolation I has is when 

Theo falls off his, laughing at me. The girls must of been stuck on the humps of theirs or they'd of fell off too. If I could of got hold of Mohamet, alone, I'd of killed him. He knew that a camel stood on its nose while it hoisted its rear end toward the clouds, and he knew that I didn't. It's one of the few times in my life I'm a hundred percent mad; but since it affords the rest of them the best laugh of the trip, I guess I should see the funny side of it too and laugh with them. Anyways, I digs myself out of the desert and not more than an hour later I'm back on this oasis hunter and this time I stays on. Boy, a camel can only pull that gag on me once. With Mohamet taking the lead-on a donkey-we starts off like a quartet of 

Pharoahs to see what kind of bunks them old kings built for themselves. It ain't long 'til the dromedaries has us up to Cheops Pyramid and I'm only saying if the old boy was half as safe when he was living as he should of been in a tomb like that, it's a wonder even 

Father Time knocked him off. Mohamet calls up to us from his donkey and says, "This is Cheops, the largest of the pramids. It is 5000 years old and is three-fifths of a mile around, towering to a peak 481 feet above us. WORLD BEATERS 

It cement joining them, and it is practically impossible to fit a calling card between any two stones. The stones were brought from the mountains across the Nile and it is believed that an hundred thousand slaves were used in the work. On our left-" "Just a minute, Mohamet," I says, "that train you just heard whistle wasn't for us. We got as much time to see all this as a camel's got to get down on its haunches. Take us easy and let us actually see this stuff." "Pardon me for rushing," he says to Joan-and that's another reason I don't care for this guide. Theo, "can't you just see those thousands of half-clad, emaciated slaves tearing the very hearts out of their bodies as they tug and pull to bring those mighty stones all those weary miles? And what ingenuity was employed to raise them to such a height can only be conjectured-could human hands have done it? seems I see another pyramid, of sun-bleached human bones-those countless slaves that paid with their all for this stupendous yet ghastly sepulchre." I don't hear what Muriel adds, but from the way she's looking at him I know she's going to agree that he's right -she would, if he thought the whole works was made of applesauce and said so that way. Ain't girls funny? 

If a guy looked like Bull Montana and talked like 

Theo, he could "wow" nine girls out of ten; and he'd at least weaken the tenth one. I asks, seeing something down on its quarters like a camel waiting for a passenger. "That's the Sphinx," he tells Joan-but I overhears him. 205 99 ing the Sphinx-spoken of as the 'Riddle of Egypt.'

There is nothing definitely known about the Sphinx. 

Its history was as much a mystery to those who built the pyramids as it is to us. It is 150 feet long and 70 feet high. You will notice that the head has the features of a man and the body is that of a lion, crouching.' I turns to Theo and says, "This Sphinx baby must of been a leather-pusher, look at that busted smeller and them cauliflower ears." "Theo," I tells him, "I can act like anybody from Edwin Booth to Gloria Swanson but I can only talk like three people on earth-I'm one of them and the other two is Al Tool." Theo, what it couldn't tell about the human race,' says Muriel.

And that's just enough-in fact it's plenty to start Theo off on another of them word exercises. "Yes," he begins, "if its sealed lips could but release the knowledge they conceal, the world would have a story unparalleled in the annals of history—for it would be the drama of unrecorded time." 

I'm thinking if I could only get that guy to do his stuff in a tent, what jack I could corner at ten cents a listen. Muriel just goes nuts over him; and maybe Joan would too if only Mahomet would lay off her long enough for her to tune in. There's a thousand or so other things Mohamet's anxious to show us but it's getting fairly late and I figures by the time we gets off these camels and gets back to Shepherd's and cleaned up it will be time to wield the silverware again, so I suggests we puts back to the Mena House and see the rest some Thursday. All agreeable, we turns back. 

Mohamet thinks to give us all a thrill, so he says something to the camels and slaps the one in front-which

WORLD BEATERS 

is Joan's, of course, and we all starts bobbing up and down like corks in a rough sea. I no sooner thinks of that than I gets sea sick and by the time I reaches the Mena House my camel's a sight and I'm actually hoping that I not only fall off again, but that I break my neck at the same time. Imagine, after traveling twothirds of the way round the world without as much as a hiccough, I has to get sea sick on the Sahara Desert. 

I'll believe anything from now on. During the ride back to Shepherd's I recovers considerably and by dinner time feels like a meal again. After dinner the girls want to take in the bazaars, so we charters a carriage and gets drove to Khan Kalili where we looks over the rugs, embroideries, etc., and then we starts up and down the little narrow alleys that's as full of stalls as 

Ike's second-hand store on Eighth Avenue is with used furniture. These little bucket shops are about ten feet wide and consists of a box-like counter filled with 

Woolworth jewelry, that can be bought anywheres from five dollars to five cents-depending on how good a trader you are and on how healthy your patience is. You pick up a pin or an ear-ring and ask how much; and this son of Egypt looks you over to see what he thinks you'll stand and then says so much. 

It's a great place to get what they call "psycho-analyzed." You can tell from the price he gives you what kind of a sucker you look like. If he says 25 cents, it means you fooled him-proved by the fact that you throw it back in the pond and look for something more expensive. You pick up another specimen which cost him the same as the first-and he tells you that one goes at three dollars, so you take it. This initiates you into the D.T.'s (demented tourists) club; and permits you to return home singing the theme song of Egypt-"'E Gypped Me." Anyway, it's a pleasant evening we has and plenty can be added to anybody's education on such an expedition.

I thought we had all the high-pressure boys corralled in New York, but these Egyptians would yawn themselves to sleep selling stuff in America. Let me give you their act, it's good. In the first place, the salesman that sees us first gets us. He's good looking-darned good lookingand he smiles on the girls and on us till we feel we live here. He don't act like he wanted to date the girls up —like the guide did—and you like him for that. Just so that ten minutes won't hang heavy on our hands he does this. 211 kidding-I agrees with him that the Malamukes was bad actors and good for nothing but hash-but secretly I'm holding to the original decision that this guy Mohammed Ali was an "Ali rat"-no matter how he spelt alley. Of course, that maybe ain't too many to serve Cairo's 800,000 natives, but it means a lot of piastres has gone into mosques in one town, for they don't build cheap places. A mosque is my idea of a palace-they're grand, that's all. Leaving the citadel we drives to what used to be the El Azhar Mosque, but way back in 988 it was converted into a university, and today, besides being the oldest university in the world, it is, as Theo puts it, "the fountain head of learning in the Islamic world." Mohamet tells us there is 11,000 students here and 300 professors and that they not only get the course for nothing but that food and pocket money is provided from endowments. I almost passes out when he tells us that the course lasts seventeen years. Imagine, that one batch of students represents 187,000 years of study. By the time they're seniors they're likely proving that half the stuff they learned as freshmen was the bunk. It only goes to prove that there's just one way to get a thorough school education and that is to enter when you're five and let the undertaker get you out.

From here we call on Sultan Hassan's mosque which he built in the 14th century and took the handpainted dahabiyeh for the best example of Arabian architecture. The next day we spends in the Egyptian Museumand while it's plenty interesting it don't keep me from knowing when 12 o'clock is at hand. We sees all the old relics, dug up on the desert by folks who ain't got nothing else to do but go nosing into other people's private mummy garages-boy, they got a mummy there for every dent in your last year's Ford; and the stone images-why every guy with enough money to buy a slab of stone in them days must of had himself chiseled out of it. The place is just lousy with stone images and mummies. Theo and the girls just go wild over all this and they seem to know most of the exhibits by their first names. -like they was waiting for the auctioneer to put them under the hammer-but then I reads on a card in the case that it's all from a tomb they uncovered in the desert and that the whole works was covered with gold and is over 4000 years old. Then we strolls into a suite of rooms reserved for King Tut. He ain't arrived yet but his fixtures is all here. An attendant tells us they were obliged to leave Tut's mummy in the tomb where they found it. It seems they thought it was sacrilegious to take it from where it had rested peacefully all those many centuries-so they just ripped off the lid of his coffins-three of them-and took the decorations away, including the gold from his fingers even, and then laid him gently back to rest -nothing sacrilegious, you understand. They just looted his grave, like a flock of hyenas and then, with solemn reverence for the dead, they stole silently from the tomb and here's the results from their pilgrimage in glass cases in this museum in Cairo. Oh, I don't suppose Tut gives a hoot-but ain't it funny how we reason a thing out so that we completely justify everything we do? I suppose when Germany writes a history of the World War, to be taught in their schools a hundred years from now, it will be so worded as to look as if the angel Gabriel, himself, called the men to arms and appointed them his agents down here to see that the dirty Allies didn't do wrong by our little nations. I gives up. I guess it's human nature. There looks to be enough jewels and gold in them cases, that they got when they hijacked Tut, to satisfy a chorus girl I never seen so much finery in one place in my life. "Isn't it enthralling out here under the canopy of heaven, dipping and rolling in the same waters that bathed the infant Moses as he lay hidden in the rushes; the same romantic river that lured Cleopatra to its shores? 

And, as we sail along, under this moonlit sky, one can only attempt in vain to conceive the empires that have come and gone as these age-old shores have guided their burden to the sea." "Well, that's his way of winning hearts," says I to myself, "and here's mine."

Getting back to town about 4 o'clock, we drives out to see some Coptic churches-not wanting a day to get by without seeing a church. These Copts is the Christian Egyptians that still hold to the old Testament teachings-I think Moses is their sky pilot.

Their churches is up any dirty alley and if you don't have a guide you never find them because they just look like the rest of the section. Inside, though, they do look something like a church-they suggest the one at Dobbins Crossing that was condemned after doing business for eighty years. We don't spend much time with the Copts. The only thing that attracts my notice is the dirt of the district and the fact that every other one you meet is cock-eyed and the next one blind-I bet there's more eye-trouble in one block among them 

Copts than in all New York.

219 99 to Shepherd's," I tells him, "we'll figure out the next move from there." "Why, Al," breaks in Joan, "We haven't been to Heliopolis, Memphis, Sakkara or any of those places yet and I wanted to spend a night on the desert, too. "Well, so long as the places you want to see are away from Cairo, it's O. K." "Why?" she wants to know, "are you so down on Cairo all of a sudden?" 

And I tries to laugh it off by saying I guess I'm getting homesick and want to be getting along. Anyhow, it's agreed that early in the morning we have Mohamet arrange for a tent on the desert for us and we'll take in this Heliopolis and Memphis place and then cut across the desert to Sakkara and from there right out to our tents. Everybody agrees it's a swell idea, so we says good-night and leaves a call at the desk for seven A. M. We wants to be out of Cairo before there's any chance of them students prowling around. 

We're having breakfast next morning at eight o'clock and by 8:30 we're in the car and Mohamet says all will be in readiness for us to bunk on the desert at night. About six miles from Cairo and we're in the modern suburb of Heliopolis. As far as I can make out from Mohamet's story, back in 1905 a gang of crap-shooters and poker sharks pooled their winnings and bought 6000 acres of desert and decided to build a gambling den that would make Monte Carlo look like a nickelodeon. The name of the dive was to be the "Heliopolis Oasis" but this oasis was only going to be fertile for the owners. They planned to take all comers to the cleaners. Work progressed on a great scale and then-bang-the war started and everything was off. Now this place is becoming a very fashionable suburb to Cairo. The only thing that impresses me here is the Heliopolis Palace Hotel,

WORLD BEATERS 

admitted by all travelers-including me—to be one of the show places of the world. It's only open about four months in the year and I hate to think what they nick you for pounding your ear in this drum. 

I'll bet your bill for a week here looks like the Allies reparation agreement with Germany. 

And if I was the customer they'd stand the same chance of getting it, but if you can afford it, it must be like a week in heaven. I guess at one time before we arrived—a city had been on this same spot, for Mohamet shows us the Virgin's tree and says it was under this that the Virgin and Christ rested on their flight into Egypt-I didn't know they had airplanes then, did you? He also shows us the place where Pharoah married Joseph to the daughter of Poti-Phers, priest of On. Them things might of happened for all of me, but I know this Palace Hotel is there and it's worth the trip even if them other tales is from Aesop's Fables.

From here we go to Memphis, a few miles south of Cairo, and see the remains of what was the greatest city in antiquity-the old name for Egypt. "This place," Mohamet says, "was built by Menes, the first 

Egyptian king, and as early as five thousand years ago its fame was known far and wide and attracted tourists, even then, as it does now; also, men came from distant lands to trade here. The remains that can still be seen show that it was a city of beautiful temples, colonnades, obelisks and statues. But it was lost to Thebes in the 15th century B. C. and when 

Alexandria was founded in 331 B. C. Memphis lost its place of importance in Egypt and was soon deserted." "Very good, Mohamet," says Theo, "you've painted us a very fine word picture of the old city. I could just see it happening as you went along." “Thank you, sir," says Mohamet, with a gratified smile. "Who's the boys in stone?" I asks, pointing to a

WORLD BEATERS 

221 couple of statues about thirty feet long. "They are both of Rameses the Second," the guide tells us. "The short one, only thirty feet long, is of granite, brought from 500 miles up the Nile. The other, 42 feet high, is of limestone. They both stood, originally, in front of the Temple of Ptah." We're right on the edge of the desert and still the place is just thick with palm trees. Over in a hole, recently discovered, surrounded by sheep and donkeys, grazing, is another sphinx, this one made of alabaster. They ain't got any stories about this one yet, but they'll likely keep digging 'til they find some love letters or something that they can sell to William Randolph Hearst, and then they'll be happy.

From here we starts out across the desert for this Sakkara place, which turns out to be a couple halfburied, sun-baked sheds, where we stops for lunch and

I never seen so many flies in my life-except at the Temple of Kali in Calcutta. We just buys some oranges and eats them out in the sun where the flies ain't so bad. That's another meal we miss. We've come this far by auto and here we take to camels again on our inspection tour of the Serapeum, Mastabi of Ti, and other interesting bits of the desert. When the camels come up I sees the guide wears a donkey instead, so I asks what the allowance would be if I turned in my this-year's camel on a used donkey. It turns out they have the same depreciation, so I gives the hump the air and straddles a donkey. I feels lots safer on this because if he gets frisky with me I can just drop my feet and let him go out from under me. 

The only drawback is I'm too far away from Joan.

The only way I could carry on a private conversation with her would be by the signal code or by telephone. 

But that loss is more than made up for by the comfort 

I'm enjoying down here.

It ain't long 'til we're up to the Serapeum and this

WORLD BEATERS 

place takes the quilted sphinx for wasted time and material. In order to keep you from getting as balled up as I am, let me give you a piece of Egyptian mythology which we gets off Mohamet-maybe you can make sense out of it. According to him, one of the gods of 

Egypt was called Osiris, and a sacred bull that the people worshiped was called Apis, and they believed that when a bull died its spirit was at once united with Osiris and became sacred, so they called it Osiris- Apis. When a bull died everybody flocked to its funeral to pay their respects and make vows to do better by our Nell from now on. Boy, that is a lot of bull. 

So, this Serapeum thing is an underground alley about a quarter of a mile long and all along both sides is stalls for bull's coffins. Some of them has been looted -if you can feature a guy robbing a bull's gravebut there's twenty-four of them still here and they're all made from solid blocks of granite, chiseled out by hand to make room for a bull to stand in. The blocks of granite is 13 feet long, 7 feet wide and 11 feet high, weighing a mere 65 tons. Can you imagine it? There's one in the main entrance, just where it was left by the makers 3700 years ago and it staggers even my thinking riggin' to figure out how they done any of it; so you can tell from that what a problem it represents. 

We moves on to the Mastaba of Ti, the tomb of a wealthy real-estate agent of about 5000 years ago who must of staged a "Florida boom" in Egypt in his time, because he sure staked himself to one fine tomb.

The walls is covered with mural reliefs, which Mohamet claims is the best examples of the art of the ancient empire. These reliefs shows the daily life of the people of that time and I couldn't see where we had got so far from them in 5000 years. We takes in several other tombs-most of them underground—

WORLD BEATERS 

223 but pretty much like Mr. Ti's, except they couldn't afford the interior decorators he had. 

It's about 5:30 now, and Mohamet suggests we better be starting for the tents, as they're a long ways across the desert and we got to get there before dark. 

So we leaves this graveyard district and goes west. 

In an hour the minarets of Cairo's mosques and the bulk of Cheops' pyramid is all out of sight and there's nothing in any direction but sand, just more and more sand. The whole desert is rippled like the sea with waves of drifted sand and in some places the sand is blown off right down to the gravel. Another hour and the sun is sinking into this sea of sand and the colors in the sky looks like the myriad shades of flowers around the homes in Honolulu. There ain't no tents in sight yet and Mohamet says we better go into a trot and makes the tents before dark or we'll be caught on the desert for the night. Joan looks at me kind of scared and I know that if we'd of had that first guide, the good-looking one, I'd of been thinking plenty; but 

I can't bring myself to think ill of old Mohamet. We slaps the animals and goes into a jog. We ain't seen any form of life since we left Sakkara and just then a caravan of camels looms on the horizon, miles away, and marches straight across the setting sun, and Theo turns to Muriel and says, "Oh, for the brush of a 

Michelangelo, or the chisel of a Cellini, that I might put on canvas or preserve in eternal stone, a scene so replete with grandeur." And Muriel just looks off into space. 

Over another mound of sand, and there, spread out in true Arab style, is our little community of tentsthree of them, one for the girls, one for us and one for Mohamet and the two servants he sent ahead to make this set-up. Their tent is also the kitchen, and the boys has dinner all ready for us. So we washes upoh, they brought everything, including water-and

we're ready to eat five minutes after we arrives. We had sent a bag on with the boys with a few necessary personal effects and it's in our tent on the bunk. We has about as tasty a meal as you'd hope to sit down to and as we eats Mohamet entertains us with lies about how he was hi-jacked by a gang of sheiks on the desert one night. He had pitched his tent-just about where these are-and they took everything he had, including his camel (a small loss, I says to myselfa camel) and then left him, helpless and alone, on the desert. Joan snuggles closer to me and I keep Mohamet going by asking fool questions about the bandits. "Yes," he goes on, "this section of the desert is alive with bands of Arab robbers"-and he's putting it over big when we hears a funny noise outside and 

I dashes to the entrance of the tent—and what do you suppose it is? A fleet of old battered Fords tearing across the desert. That don't fit in so good with Mohamet's story-and at once he notices the girl's reactions and to save his face he quickly adds, "Of course this all happened forty years ago, before the automobile civilized the desert."

After dinner we retires to our tents which is coneshaped affairs with passages from the Koran worked in all over it with different colors of wool. They sure are snappy tents and seem to lend just the proper touch of native color to the whole setting. The girls is tickled pink with their desert home and after Mohamet assures us that each of the servants sit up and guard the entrance to our tents all night we goes to bed fearlessly. I wondered, when I first looked in the tents, what all the bed-clothes was for-it's so hot these days-but I knows now. After the sun goes down on the desert, you'd think you was in zero's home town and you find them bed-clothes ain't one feather too many. That hike through them tombs must of been tougher going than I thought at the time, because my

225 head no sooner hits the pillow than I'm back on the Keith circuit and playing the old Hippodrome. I has to get up through the night-just to see that no robbers is stealing sand off our desert-and I stumbles over the servant, fast asleep in the entrance. What a protection he'd of been, eh? I ain't forgot that the sailing university's in town and as we treks across the desert (there's a cute word -trek-to travel) I suggests that since we've seen about all Egypt's got to offer, we move on to Alexandria and get ourselves a couple cabins for the Mediterranean cruise. The idea clicks, so when we arrives in Cairo it's only a matter of a couple hours 'til we're all packed up, hotel bill settled for, Mohamet released and a cab waiting to get us to the station. Just like that we gives Cairo the atmosphere without a sign of friction from any quarter. I'm actually pleased with the way things has went off. 

The four hour ride to Alexandria is through low, swampy land, so not too interesting. We finds the town itself rather modern, at least the European section is, but that's only got 50,000 of the 450,000 in the city. The Mohammedan or native section is, of course, as squalid as usual. The Place Mehemet Ali -named after the man that rebuilt the town after it had been reduced by numerous battles, from 1,000,000 to 5000 inhabitants-is in the centre of town and is surrounded by real attractive buildings. The town overlooks the Mediterranean Sea and is the only important seaport of Egypt. Theo tells us how the town was founded by Alexander the Great in 332 B. C. and that it was given to Ptolemy, one of his generals, to run; in fact this chap become king of all Egypt, and established a library here of 700,000 books—the most famous library in the world, but they was burned when the Romans sacked the town. Nice fellows, them Romans, eh?

We sees Pompey's pillar too, which Theo says is considered the most prominent relic of antiquity. It's supposed to mark the burial spot of Pompey the Great. It's just a great stone column up on a mound in the centre of town about as attractive as the toothache.

WORLD BEATERS 

227 We don't spend much time here as there's a boat sailing for Italy about 11 P. M. and we want to make it. So we do make it, and about 11:30 as we pulls away from Alexandria, we leaves the ancient world behind-but they can't blame us for that-it was behind before we even got there. The four of us is out at the rear of the boat watching the lights of Alexandria do a fade out and Joan says, "It seems like our world tour is over, now that we've left Egypt." "Why, Joan," I says, "Mussolini is still before us-we ain't seen nothing yet." But Muriel agrees with Joan, that, "It won't be long now." And then Theo finishes up the whole thing with one of them famous oratoricals known to the press as "verbal barrages." He straightens up, like Lincoln at Gettysburg, and lets forth as follows: "Four score and seven years ago-" no, I'm wrong that was Lincoln. Theo began like this, "There flicker the fading lights of Alexandria, which may be the last sight any of us will ever have of the land of the Pharoahs. There runs in my mind the dramatic life of one of the grandest men that ever lived and he was born in this land we're leaving- Moses. We saw where he was found in the rushes, along the Nile; where the Red Sea parted to allow him to escape with his followers from Pharoah. We looked out across the wilderness where they roamed for forty years and we gazed up into Mt. Sinai where the Ten Commandments were revealed to him. Again, we stood on the banks of the Jordan and looked up into the mountains of Moab from whose prominence he had viewed the Promised Land; and off from the shore of the Dead Sea we looked upon his unpretentious sepulchre-what a picture his life portrays—and he was born over there in Egypt." He stops, and I think he's through his trance, but I'm wrong again. 

He's only out for air, and then he goes on: "Just think, as you travel over the Sahara, high on the back

WORLD BEATERS 

of your aristocratic camel, and view those stupendous works in stone, that for ages were covered by the sands of the desert-and only by accident discovered-your imagination runs away with your rational faculty and you see whole cities concealed from the inquisitiveness of chattering tourists by the kind sands of time. You see them plying their trades in the cities and peacefully tilling the soil; you see rulers with their vast armies; men of learning; gorgeous temples of worship; you conceive of a great inundation that destroyed all trace of them all but those imperishable works in stoneand as the waters receded, leaving but silt and sand, and the winds blew and the sands shifted and the years gathered into centuries and they into ages, all became silent, and only the hush of eons was broken by the excavator's pick as he unearthed, to a bewildered world, those evidences of prehistoric nations. That's the picture of Egypt I'm taking away with me." Not a bad picture, eh? How many do you want, and will you take them with you or will I send them-Mehemet

Theo finishes his history of Egypt, we are out of sight of everything but the Mediterranean. Joan seems to immediately forget Egypt and says that she has looked forward to a cruise on the blue Mediterranean ever since she left home and now she's having her dreams come true. It is a wonderful boat ride-especially under a full moon.

We sits out on deck and watches the course of the moonbeams across the water, like a path of gold through a sea of sapphire. I wish I had some of

229 beautiful sights I've ever seen. It just fascinates you.

Joan is in my arms and there never was a more perfect opportunity afforded any fellow to tell a girl what he's thinking, than that-but how in the Ganges can a guy, who still owes $1.50 on his last meal ticket, tell a girl worth $3,000,000 that he loves her. It would sound to her exactly like Tunney telling his manager-before that bout in Phili-that he wasn't interested in the purse, he just wanted to afford his public an evening's entertainment. But I'm just burning up with a desire to pour out my heart to her. Boy, I care for Joan. It's late though, so I don't take up any new business until the next night.

The setting is almost identical with the night before. 

We're up front on the hurricane deck,-all by ourselves-I don't even know where Theo and Muriel is, and I don't care furthermore. Joan has on the cutest little evening gown you'd ever hope to see and in the moonlight her big brown eyes just sparkle like the phosphorus in the water. Boy, she's plenty sweet tonight, and I ain't so bad myself. I got the old tux cleaned up and I do know how to wear one, though. you may doubt it, and I was at least easy enough on the eyes to get Irene Castle's call to sub when her partner sprained an ankle in New York; so I'd say that, with both of us at our best, we don't make a bad looking couple. Joan starts the fireworks by asking me what my profession is and am I already established or is this trip just the present Dad gave me for finishing college. Is there anybody present who ain't able to imagine my embarrassment? If there is, have an X-ray taken and charge it to me; you're in worse shape than I am. I'm wishing I'd of drowned when I fell into the Nile or broke my neck when the camel give me the air. No foolin', I'm plenty low.

I've had to answer some tough questions in my time, like, "Are you going to pay this installment or shall I

WORLD BEATERS 

garnishee your salary?" and, "Where were you on the night the crime was committed?" But I never had to answer a question that called for such fast thinking as this one Joan has thought up. My first thought is to sing her the old Vanderbilt song but, while I recall the air I ain't got the right words and anyway, when 

I looks at Joan I know I could never bring myself to give her the air. My next accident-I mean, my next idea is to tell her the truth, and let the chips fall where they may. If the end is at hand I decides to make it sure and complete—I hate these lynchings that slowly guzzle you to death. So I says, "No, Joan, I don't want to fool you. The facts are, I'm not traveling on Dad and I don't own any diplomas. You know, when we visited the El Azhar in Cairo and Mohamet told us the course lasted 17 years, we all thought that was a life sentence, but, Joan, there's one school that has a longer course and I've been in it since I was twelve years old—yes, you've guessed it, the school of experience. They don't have any sheepskins in this school, Joan, and they don't give degrees but a fellow learns there what the rules of the game called 'life' are. He sees them set aside by folks who are in a hurry to the top and he sees their wounds from the inevitable fall. In this school he sees others playing the game according to the rules and he sees them climb on and up toward the big prize. Joan, if a student in this school has half a brain he knows how to play the man-and he plays it. True, he may not be able to express his ideas like Theo can-it takes book learning to do that-but he can have ideas like Theo or like any other man that ever won all the degrees in the world. For, after all, and I'm not offering this as an alibi, Joan, I know I'm not your kind-but, after all, success in life isn't measured by piles of dollars or numbers of degrees, but by the number of times he breaks the rules of the game of life."

231 She's squeezing my hand-and I'm actually surprised myself, at the good job I've done. But I meant it. About noon the next day we sights the toe of Italy and the scenery along the shore is great. The Mediterranean is as calm as Coolidge and the mountains along the shore throws perfect reflections into the sea. On our left is Sicily in plain view and as we sails along, almost motionless, Theo remarks that soon we'll be walking where the great Caesar held sway.

About 7 o'clock, just as the sun is down so low that the water looks a spooky black, we slides in between the Island of Capri and Amalfi, on the cliffs of the mainland and into beautiful Naples bay. Theo's been here before, that's where I gets the names. In front of us, spread nearly half way around the bay like a great crescent is Naples, the largest city in Italy with her 800,000 Fascisti; and off to the right, fuming and fretting like an assistant prosecuting attorney, is Mt. 

Vesuvius said by Theo, to be the oldest active volcano known. From out here in the bay it looks innocent enough, but every few minutes when it spits fire and throws out melted rock it makes you think how sweet a chorus girl looks from the first row in the balcony, but refuse to buy her the necklace she wants and you've got plenty fireworks.

Docking at Naples we has to pay a "debarkation fee" of so many lira, (Wop for nickel) before we can get off the boat. They're not satisfied to just have you come and spend your money on seeing their country, they make you pay to get in. Theo does, and we takes a cab to the Excelsior Hotel-and what a nifty drum it is. Over the dinner table about eight

WORLD BEATERS 

233 o'clock the girls tell us their plans. It seems they spent their vacation two years ago in Italy and Switzerland and they ain't fussy in seeing it all again so soon, and they're leaving in the morning for Paris. 

My spirit drops right out of my spats. Here I was thinking we'd see this Europe place together and now they're leaving. The only bright side of the story is, they're staying in Paris and we'll be there soon. 

When the girls leave the next morning, I says to
