So we spends six weeks according to the calendar, but according to me it's six eternities —seeing Italy. Theo had been in the country the same time the girls had been, a couple years ago but they didn't meet. He thinks Italy is the only spot on earth for a real vacation. He says it has everything a person could ask for-mountains, plains, rivers, lakes, oceans, picturesque peoples, operas, fine hotels, all kinds of sports, art and sculpture of the old mastershe says it's fairly alive with the ghosts of old Rome and too, the country is clean, prosperous and happy. 

Well, that's a boost for any place-even if only part of it's true. So after keeping out a few necessary things and sending the rest of our bags on to Paris, Theo takes me out to prove that what he says of Italy is true. And he does. It is.

I gathers from what he says about travelers that 

I'm likely the only person in America who ain't been here before, so there's no sense in me giving you my impressions of Italy. You've undoubtedly got your own-and even if you ain't, you're as far ahead as you'd be after hearing mine. It seems, the first time

Theo toured Italy he made extensive notes on the

WORLD BEATERS 

country but this time he only jots down a kind of hint that he's been in again. Here's what he puts in the book about them six weeks. "It seems the rule today if one writes about Italy, he must, of necessity, write about Mussolini,-or else -he must write about Mussolini. But to separate 

Mussolini from Italy would be about as simple a task as separating the Alps from Switzerland; and while I won't attempt a biography of him, he will play a leading role in my story. "True, Mussolini is loved and admired by his people; as evidenced in the recent election when 97 percent of those having the franchise voted and 95 percent of them voted for Mussolini-although they might have been influenced by the fact that no other name appeared on the ballot-but that is Mussolini's way of doing things. "When he took his seat in the stadium one Sunday afternoon to witness Mascagni conduct a 5000 piece band, the 60,000 who had jammed the stadium rose as one man and the ovation accorded Mussolini was tremendous. He was sitting only a few feet from me and I was able to view at close range Italy's man of destiny. He looks like the reincarnation of all the rulers of old Rome moulded into one. "When King Emmanuel III appeared on the balcony of the city hall in Florence the same city hall in which Savonarola had been imprisoned-I was but one of the hundred thousand who helped fill the square, but it was curiosity and not admiration that had brought them there-their cheers were reserved for Mussolini. Under his regime Italy is clean, prosperous and carries its chin high. Never has history known such a complete recovery from such a chaotic state as that experienced by Italy in the past seven years; and all because one man knew it could be done, and knew that he could do it. What a tribute to a

WORLD BEATERS 

235 man's powers of conception, his determination, stamina and ability. "When Mussolini marched his army of 'blackshirts' through Italy seven years ago, he marched them through streets that reeked with filth and evidenced poverty and pathos on every hand. Today, those streets are spotless, the homes that had wanted for the necessities of life, are happy; and the kiddies smile once more, as they play in the streets in safety. 

And not only has this miracle-worker brought prosperity and happiness into the home but he has brought a new respect from the nations of the world for Italy. "It was this new Mussolinized Italy where I spent six wonderful weeks on her lakes, in her mountains and along her rivers. "On the southern shore of the beautiful Bay of

Naples, perched high on the precipitous rocks that rim her shores, sits the ancient town of Sorrento. From here in their safety the inhabitants of two thousand years ago had witnessed the destruction of Herculaneum and Pompeii when Vesuvius erupted that bright 

June morning. "Looking out my hotel window, 200 feet straight down into the blue-green waters of the bay, across the bay, busy with the affairs of the largest city in Italy was Naples; to the left, the famous Island of Capri with its world-famed 'Blue-Grotto'; and to the right, towering majestically into the heavens, Vesuvius poured out its constant stream of smoke and lava-I recalled that saying about the strawberry and thought how well it applied here, 'Doubtless God could have made a better [scene] but doubtless God never did! "A visit to the unearthed ruins of Pompeii gave me a full appreciation of that ironic saying 'sympathetic as Pompeiian lava.' Here, one morning, just thirtyeight years after the Crucifixion of Christ, one of the most gorgeous cities of all time bowed to an irresis-

WORLD BEATERS 

tible force of nature, and under many feet of molten rock, its splendor was hidden from the eyes of the world until today. Resting, as it does, at the foot of 

Vesuvius, it was an easy prey of that growling, imperturbable monster. Continuing on to Rome we find more ruins-grander ruins, yes, even sacred ruinsfor here, at the corner of the old Roman Forum is the prison where Paul was incarcerated. And out the 

Appian Way, near to where Peter met Paul on his way to Rome, are the Catacombs, where many Christian martyrs lie buried. Amid the ruins of the old 

Forum one imagines he can see Augustus on the Palatine Hill; and on the opposite prominence in all his pomp and splendor Nero stalks. Down the walk comes Caesar to greet you; and in his study writing his character-building philosophy, you know Marcus 

Aurelius is there. "Just across the way, its massive walls commanding a view of Rome, what remains of the old Coliseum thrills one at the thought of what took place there. 

What headliners of history must have been amused there. What humane societies of today would have found to criticize within its walls. What anguish, suffering and death, all to amuse the spectator. "But there are those who have passed to their reward, who have left to posterity something of greater worth than ruins. Perhaps no other nation ever has or ever will produce greater treasures in art and sculpture than Italy-and Rome has no small share of these priceless works. In the Vatican and Church of 

St. Peter (which institutions should be described by themselves, but time and space forbids) are the rarest works of art on earth. Here Michelangelo and

Raphael left their greatest monuments to art and sculpture. On the wall behind the altar in the Sistine 

Chapel-where only the Pope officiates-is Michelangelo's 'Last Judgment.' Seven years of the artist's

WORLD BEATERS 

237 life were spent in executing this masterpiece which has awed multitudes these many centuries. "In the Pantheon, the best preserved building of the ancient Romans, and spoken of as the Westminster Abbey of Italy, because of the kings buried there, one will, invariably, find a throng about the tomb of 

Raphael. 

"Look where one may choose in Rome and there is the evidence of the ancient Roman, or the sculptor or the artist. Through the heart of the city runs the 

Tiber, that famous old river over which crossed the bridge that Horatius defended. It's an extremely crooked river and yellow with silt from the hills but it has seen the day when its waters ran red with the blood of Rome. "Moving farther north, we stop in Florence, the cultural city of Italy. Here was the birthplace of 

Michelangelo, Dante, Galileo and many others of like repute. Here they produced most of their immortal works, and here in the church of Santa Croce they all rest in peace. Here Savonarola proclaimed his truths to the world, and here, too, he was burned at the stake. "What a claim to greatness a country makes with such names as these to offer. Then, on to Venice, with its gondolas and serenaders luring one and all to romance. What a pedestrian's heaven-not one automobile in all Venice-not a horse. "Finally, to Milan with the one cathedral I have seen since leaving America that makes one want to worship instead of sightsee. "This is a too brief review of my six weeks in Italy, I confess but then, churches and ruins and men of yesterday are not interesting if told of in detail."

Now that gives you an idea where we was in Italy and if it wasn't that my mind is in Paris I could likely add something to them observations of Theo's; but I

WORLD BEATERS 

have to have my mind on my subject to even think of the dumb things I say. There's one thing I remember that Theo didn't speak of-he wouldn't-what's money to him—but I'll never forget the gypping Italy gives a tourist. Of course she still owes a few installments on her last war, but why should Americans have to pay it? We was nicked plenty in Egypt but when we arrives in Naples the old game of "Tourist, tourist, who's got the tourist" begins among hotel proprietors, taxi drivers, travel agencies, etc. I remember looking over our hotel bill from Sorrento and besides the regular Jesse James tariff for each of the seven days we was there, I finds an "arrival tax" that's for going there in the first place; then there was a "sojourn tax" for each day-that's for staying there after you do arrive. Then a stamp is stuck on the bill and a few more lira added for that. And looking over the items I sees we're stuck ten lira extra for each bath that we took and then, when the whole bill is added up, they put on ten percent for service-and that's your bill. I understand Mussolini will give 5000 lira to any person showing him a new way to tax the tourist-but there's the same chance of him ever having to part with that 5000 as there is of Harry Lauder having to make his proposed donation to the widow of the Unknown Soldier.

The museums and art galleries in Italy are free on Sundays when the native is off work and able to go, and the places are so full of Italians you couldn't move and the air is so thick with garlic that you couldn't breathe, so a tourist waits till a week day, but on week days when the Italian is working and can't go, and when a tourist has to go-they charge 12 lira to get in. Don't you love Mussolini for that? 

Leaving Milan it ain't long 'til we're in Switzerland and, folks, this is a pretty country. Not big, you understand, but more beauty per square foot than any

WORLD BEATERS 

239 other country I've seen anyways. We spends a little time in Lugano, a swell little town on a glistening lake -and one day, when we're up on one of the mountains looking down into the valley of orchards and off at the city, nestling at the edge of the lake, Theo takes in the surroundings and says, "Al, aren't you seized by the majesty of the Alps, as they shove their snow-clad peaks into the heavens? And sparkling in the frosty air of these high altitudes that lake looks like a great pool of diamonds. Look down this slope, Al, how consistent nature has been in her color schemesblending with these snow-capped heights are those pink and white apple blossoms that fill the valley with their delicate beauty and rare aroma. And listenhear them, Al, the sweetest-throated song birds on earth, pouring their harmonious symphonies into this paradise-how it all brings one 'in tune with the Infinite'." That's my picture of Switzerland, painted by 

Mr. Theodore Kingsley. Ain't he wonderful, girls?

But we gets back to the hotel and after dinner strolls along the promenade. This promenade along the lake at Lugano has even the boardwalk at Atlantic 

City beat to my way of thinking. In the first place, it ain't lousy with pop-stands and hot-dog booths, and it looks out on the prettiest piece of aqua-Bostonian for "water"-I've ever looked at, and there's three miles of nothing but hotels, real hotels. In fact, there's only two things I see that a Swiss does-besides chase sheep over the Alps-and that is, run hotels and yodel. If he's doing anything else and you ask him what his business is he'll likely claim to be in a hotel or yodeling, just to escape suspicion. But, getting back to the promenade, I sees more jewelry on parade this night than you'll see at the Ritz in a month. If some of our real successful bank robbers in America could only pull a holdup of this walk some evening, they could afford to stay at the Excelsior Hotel

240⚫ 

WORLD BEATERS 

in Naples for a month. Every other dame you pass is humpbacked from carrying round her pearls and her diamonds. Of course, they ain't natives-not all of them-Theo tells me they're the elite of Europe. I tries to find out what that word "elite" means. I can't.

But it must mean "pawnbroker's daughter" because nobody else could afford so many gems. 

Out in front of nearly every hotel is a snappy orchestra and most of them has a yodeler or two along. 

Folks, if you've never heard a Swiss yodel you ain't heard nothing yet. Boy, when he throws his head back on his shoulder and goes into a yodel, he yodels, what I mean. He must hold air like a camel holds. water—he seems to gargle them notes for five minutes without stopping, and the way he opens his mouth would make a dentist weep with joy-you can see what he had for breakfast two miles away. 

But we can't stay too long here, with Joan and

Muriel probably crying their eyes out in Paris, so we pays a hurried call to Luzern, Zurich and them places and then hops the border into France and when we wakes up in the morning we're in the suburbs of “gay 

Paree." Whoopee.

The girls had told us they'd be at the King George. 

Hotel so I'll give you three guesses where we heads for-and if you don't have two left over, you've never been in love. When we're registering at the King 

George, Theo asks the clerk if he has a Miss London and a Miss Durant registered and, with a glance at a wall file, he tells us at once he has-"but," he adds, "they are out of town just now. They went down to 

Nice for the week-end." If that ain't a tough break neither was that long count in Chicago for Dempsey. 

Here we hurry through to be with them on Sunday and they're gone. "Well, Al," says Theo, "I guess we'll have to see Paris alone for a couple of days.' "O.K.," I says, downhearted. Once in our room and 99

WORLD BEATERS 

241 cleaned up, Theo goes over to the window and, taking a good big look, his grin getting wider and wider, he says at last, "Good old Paris. You don't change your looks much-like New York does-but you don't lose your grip on a fellow's heart even if you are the same old town. What is it you've got, Paris, that other towns lack? I much prefer the Metropolitan to your 

Opera; I detest eating out on your sidewalks in all the dust, and exposed to the staring crowds; I think your night clubs are beastly, and I'm sure Ziegfeld outclasses your follies. What is it-why is it-how is it you win the heart of your visitor until he must come again and again?" "What is there about this burg you do like, Theo," I asks him, not knowing any other reasons a fellow would want to come here for except for the things he says he don't like. "Have you a weakness for brunettes ?" "No, Al," he tells me. "It isn't that. No man with a weakness for girls would ever leave Fifth Avenue, not just to better his advantage. Feminine pulchritude reaches its apex in New 

York-or on any Main street in America for that matter-no, it isn't her girls that makes me thrill at the very sound of her name-but there's something about Paris that wins its way right inside my vest.' "You don't suppose there's anything round the room, Theo? I brought a can of Flit along, just in case—.' "You're certainly a cure for sentiment, Al," he says, "I only hope you don't appear when I'm proposing to 

Muriel," he laughs. "Don't laugh," I tells him. "You've ducked the ladies for thirty-two years-but remember Beachey looped the loop forty-seven times before he fell-and that first fall was fatal." you're prophesying my future, Al, I'd just say you're mighty pessimistic, but," he finishes, "I'll see what I can do to hold up your reputation as a prophet." the jack you got, I'd of beat you to it by at least two months." "Why, Al, are you that strong for the girl," he asks. "Strong for her," I says, "that ain't the word. 

I'm weak over her." "Well, cheer up, old man," he tells me, "she's got enough for both of you." "Yes, Theo," I says, "I know that but imagine me trying to keep up with her company." And Theo says not to worry over that because her company will likely be drunk all the time and won't know whether I'm right or wrong. This gives me new courage and I feels refreshed. "How about a bite to eat, Al?" is his next suggestion and one that I've never yet put up an argument with. So we drops over to one of them sidewalk cafes that Theo "detests" and we sits out there and gives Paris a close up of our tonsils while we fill the hatch with "pork-on-rye." "You drink, yes?" the waiter both asks and answers the question. "Two coffees," Theo tells him. "But, American, nice liquor." "Coffee, please," Theo repeats with emphasis, and the 

Frog looks disgusted and beats it for the order. "They can't believe an American who doesn't order liquor,' says Theo, and looking round at the tables I see plenty reason why they can't. We're at the Café Rue de la 

Paix the most famous sidewalk café in Paris and every table looks crowded and I'll bet three-quarters of them is Americans and I'll bet my other dime that we're the only ones there drinking coffee-and if it wasn't for that resolution I'd be sociable and make it unanimous. Our table is right on the corner and we can look right up and down the Rue de la Paix, the main street of Paris, or to our left into the Opera, one snappy show house, believe me. 

After the nose-bags is empty Theo suggests we take a rubberneck bus ride through town. He knows Paris like I know the Automat on Fortieth Street, but he's never been through town with a megaphone, so he stakes us to a couple outside seats and we leaves from a square out in front 99

WORLD BEATERS 

243 It's of the Madeleine and the chap with the megaphone goes into action at once and lets us in on the secret that the Madeleine is a church built by Napoleon and copied from a Greek theatre-imagine a church taking off a movie house-but it's plenty good looking, with double rows of great pillars all the way around. 

Then we drives over to the Place de Concorde and from the megaphone I picks up, "We are now in the largest and finest square in the world. It is the real centre of Paris-here the fashion of the city is on parade. In 1793, during the Terror, the guillotine was erected in this square and many hundreds of the titled bluebloods of France breathed their last on the block.

The Egyptian Obelisk of Louqsor, now in the centre of the square, is 75 feet high and weighs 240 tons. It is reputed to be 3300 years old." We turns out of this square into the Champs Elysees, "the most beautiful boulevard in the world"—so the Frog says, and I can't dispute the claim from anything I've ever seen. only a mile and a quarter long-but, boy, it's grand.

It's 300 feet wide and lined with many fine buildings and cafés, in front of which in the shade of the beautiful trees along the curb, sit the boulevardiers, eating, drinking, and staring at tourists like us. At the end of this boulevard is the Arch of Triumph, which, we're told, was begun by Napoleon to kind of impress his victories on the people. It's the largest arch in the world and while it still serves the purpose Nap had in mind for it, they also got France's Unknown Soldier there. From here we drops down across the Seine to the Eiffel Tower, a skeleton-like steel tower, 330 feet square at its base and towering 984 feet; making it the highest structure in the world. This Paris place seems to have more than its share of world records. 

The bus stops long enough for us to take the elevators to the top of the tower and, folks, I'm altogether too far away from that terra firma place to enjoy the

WORLD BEATERS 

view. You can see for eighty miles in any direction but I can only see straight up-if I looks down I just know I'll jump. I do look out of the elevator on our way down and sees the beautiful parks of Paris and the swell buildings-most of them looks like palaces to me. I'll say Mr. Eiffel built a tower when he put this metal together. From here we slips around through swell park-like boulevards to the Hotel des

Invalides which is quite the museum, but the main event here is the tomb of Napoleon. Boy, if this baby was grand in life, he's even more so in death. He don't have a roof over him like the Taj Mahal, you understand, but in a country where they know better than to spend all they had on a building, he sure rates a snappy piece of marble.

We're standing there, looking down on his tombthe megaphone gets a chance to cool off in here, they don't allow guides to disturb Nap's nap-and Theo says, like he was talking to himself, "The great Napoleon-but, like Alexander, 'greater in genius than in character'-he crowned a brilliant career at Austerlitz but he left countless homes shrouded in sorrowand though he rose from mediocrity to juggle with thrones and kingdoms, that same cruel hand of ambition robbed him of his beloved wife, Josephine." And turning to me, he went on, "Al, under that great block of marble lie the remains of one whose possessions were the greatest of his day-but I maintain he paid. too great a price." "Well, Theo," I puts in, "if that bird's favorite outdoor sport was war, I'm glad that hunk of marble ain't one pound lighter-that ought to be able to hold him down."

Back in the bus and we beats it through town to the river again and crosses to a little island-though you ain't conscious that it is an island-and here, in the middle, the guide shows us the Cathedral of Notre Dame. He says it was begun in the twelfth century,

WORLD BEATERS 

245 and that it's one of the best examples of Gothic architecture in France-also he adds that it's got the biggest organ in the country-and in his rambles about the place he claims Napoleon was crowned Emperor of France inside, and Mary, Queen of Scots, took the Dauphin for a meal ticket here. It's quite the church, only it's too dark and gloomy inside for me. We're nicked two bits extra to get into the treasure room and they got a lot of old soiled dinner jackets in there. that's claimed to be made of gold cloth and some pieces of jewelry that even King Tut wouldn't of wore -providing they are real diamonds, like the guard says. Then we takes in the Hôtel-de-Ville along the river and from what the guide turns into his megaphone all I gathers about the place is that they've staged more murders here than even at the opera. It seems from 1310 to 1832 they held their public executions here but in 1873, during the revolution, single executions was taking up too much of the butcher's time, so they herded a gang of 600 into this drum and filled the place with gunpowder and petroleum and then got careless with matches. That was one busy day for St. Peter-checking in (or is it out) 600 at one shot. But the place don't look too bad after all that.

It's getting late now and we've had almost full mileage out of our tickets so we heads back for the place we left from, passing the Louvre on the way but we don't stop. Theo says, "We'll take in the Louvre tomorrow, Al. I've studied every picture in it but I know 

I'll enjoy another trip as much as the first." I takes a look at it-it's about the biggest pile of brick I've ever seen yet-and then I looks sympathetically at my dogs and says, "Can we take rickishaws through it, Theo?" 

He don't say anything but he looks like "No" all over.

After dinner-which is a dinner-we trips over to the Albert Theatre and takes in "Journey's End," one

WORLD BEATERS 

clever play at last. The next day is Sunday, so I delays getting up as long as possible, for I know we got to do that Louvre place on foot. It turns out though that 

I've seen worse paint jobs-I lived next to a body repair shop in Jersey City once-so the day passes, eventually. And Monday, we're to see the girls. 

We do. And what a reunion it is. Right there in the lobby where we first find them and Joan couldn't of been sweeter if we'd been on the boat from Alexandria. All of a sudden I cares for Paris. We spends most of Monday just complimenting each other on how good we look and telling how we missed each other this time I can speak truthfully. At night we dine and dance at the Lido and I guess them two days waiting for Joan got me worked up beyond control because that night-after the dance-when we was sitting behind a big palm in a darkened corner of the hotel reception room, I admits to her that I should know better-but a fellow can't help heart trouble any more than he can high blood pressure-and so I breaks down and tells her I'm simply nuts about her. 

It maybe wasn't the most romantic way to put it, but it seemed stronger than just that old line "I love you.' 

I've told thirty-seven blondes and eighteen brunettes that not to mention all the red-heads I've met. Joan looks at me, with a look I can't even figure out-and 

I've had girls look at me every way I thought one could look and then she says, "Is that a proposal, Al?" "Joan," I tells her, "it's as close to a proposal as I'd ever have the nerve to get-with you. Imagine, you've got three million and one dollars and fifty cents more than me-how could I ever ask you to marry me?" "But," she comes back, "would you let anything keep you from having the girl of your choice, providing she were willing?" "Joan," I cries, "would you have me?" And she throws her arms around my neck and it's hours before either of us comes up for air and 99

WORLD BEATERS 

247 when we do both our cheeks are wet. Ain't love grand? I'm so happy I actually forget for the moment that she's worth $3,000,000. "Let's go up and tell 

Muriel," Joan beams, and we dashes upstairs as happy as two kids watching their school burn down. When we crashes the gate on Muriel, her face lights up like a dragoman's cloak and before me or Joan can speak, she says, "Joan, Theo and I are engaged." And Joan turns on me like I was poison and says, "So it's a game, eh? You've wagered each other you could win us first-you-you-oh,-Muriel-," she gasps, and throws herself on the bed. I'll leave out of the records the long, hard battle that followed, but it's all of an hour later before I've convinced Joan she's done me a great wrong and we're all happy again-but can you imagine two things like that happening on the same evening?

There's just one thing that the girls forgot to tell us and that is that the marriage must be on one condition that their mothers live with us. Well that's a tough blow to Theo, but it's O.K. with me-who am I to look a gift horse in the mouth?

That night in our room Theo gets a great kick out of my tale of what happened in the girls' room. The next day we all gets together and decides to do the thing up in a hurry, right here in Paris, and then wire the mothers-in-law. The girls have seen Paris-so has Theo-and I don't care if I never see any more of it, so we makes immediate preparations for the great step. 

Two days later Mr. and Mrs. Theodore Kingsley and Mr. and Mrs. Alfred Tool board a plane for London. The honeymoon turns out to be a tour of England, Belgium, Germany, France and Spain-but if you think I'm going to tell you what we done on our honeymoon-then I know you're single. 

The ride across the Atlantic is like a tour of heaven

WORLD BEATERS 

-except for one day. Folks, that one day it's rougher than Dempsey and I doubt if there's a cemetery in America that's got a man in it half as sick as me. I even forgot I'm married, and I'm still sick at night-even the moon comes up. 

But after that all is lovely. 

We're sitting out on deck one afternoon, reviewing our world tour and telling what had impressed us the most and the girls are unanimous on the Taj Mahal. 

I claims that of all the things I've seen since leaving America Joan made the biggest impression. This gets me a kiss from the bank-I mean, the wife-and a laugh from the two Kingsleys. But Theo, as you'd expect, offers something worth jotting down. He says, "Well, I won't be the parrot I'd like to be, and repeat what Al said-only Muriel knows I could, don't you, dear?—and the Taj Mahal stands out in my memory, and always will-but I really believe, of all the innumerable sights and impressions of the trip, there is one big thing that stands out above all else it is the shrines of men. Isn't it a singular thing that whereever a body of men have banded themselves together, they have first set up a shrine. Not only is this a condition prevailing throughout the world today but such has been the history of the human race from time immemorial. I would almost dare to differ with Pope when he says, 'The proper study of mankind is man.'

I would feel I had gained a more comprehensive knowledge of man through a study of his religion. I believe the Hindu, worshiping the Sun-God as he bathes in the sacred Ganges is no less sincere than the 

Pope who praises his God in the Sistine Chapel. If there be a difference in their degrees of faith the favor indisputably rests with the Hindu, for his belief has not been subjected to the lacerating facts of science, while the enlightened Pope must weigh one against the other in scales of truth-accepting one and rejecting

WORLD BEATERS 

249 the other. That is the reason we found the greatest demonstrations of unswerving fidelity to religious beliefs in those countries which have not yet fallen in line with the march of occidental progress.' "That's my Theo," says Muriel, with noticeable pride. "Yes," I adds, "religion looks like the world's leading industry; and whether the Hindu's right or wrong, I have to hand it to him for one thing-when we were in his country he just went ahead with his worshiping-he didn't try to shove us in the Ganges too-though one of them did put Theo overboard. 

That's the one thing I've got against high-pressure

Christians-they spend half their life getting their Bible down, and the other half trying to get it down everybody else. I can't understand why a Christian who works out his own salvation-as he calls itwon't allow other people the same privilege." "Al," says Theo, "you've opened up too big a problem to be solved on this trip-you know we arrive in New York in the morning." And with that brought home to us the girls decide to get packed up and be ready to hail their mothers and exhibit the fruits of their travels, i. e., us.

The next morning-having passed Quarantinethe four of us are out on the promenade deck, with the girls straining their eyes for their mothers. I don't know how Theo is but I'm as nervous as a ham actor when he sees vegetables crossing the footlights. The boat creeps closer and closer and then, with a shriek, the wives cry in chorus, "Oh, Mother," and turning to us they try, frantically, to point them out. "Which ones?" I ask. "The two off by themselves, in black." "Not over by that trunk," I gasps. "Yes, that's them,' 

Joan beams. "What's the matter, Al?" Theo asks, as 

I leans heavy on him. "Do you see our mothers-inlaw?" I puts to him, pointing toward the trunk; and
